Disney found a way to buy Character.AI without buying its lawsuits
2026-10-19

Disney found a way to buy Character.AI without buying its lawsuits

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
This week, Character.AI declared bankruptcy. While some saw the fall of a chatbot platform as a harbinger for the industry, court filings indicate that the decision was actually a key part of a deal that has let Disney hire most of Character.AI’s staff—including its now former CEO Karandeep Anand—potentially acquire most of Character.AI’s technology, and avoid the beleaguered startup’s many legal liabilities and obligations. In the past few years, Character.AI has faced multiple private lawsuits over the safety of its chatbots, as well as investigations from regulators around the world. In a declaration filed as part of the bankruptcy, Character.AI’s newly appointed CEO explained that the goal of the bankruptcy was to reach a “consensual resolution” with personal injury claimants and a group of states investigating the company, while maximizing the value of the company’s assets for shareholders. The Disney deal seems to be structured so that whatever is left of Character.AI will retain responsibility for those legal battles, some of which are still ongoing. The move appears to be a new twist on what some have dubbed a “reverse acquihire,” a process in which a buyer carefully orchestrates a succession of deals with a target company, usually in an effort to hire key employees and obtain expansive license to use and build on the target company’s unique technology. Stripped of its employees, the target becomes a husk of itself. Companies often take the position that reverse acquihires are not actual mergers, and that they do not have to notify federal antitrust regulators about those deals as they would otherwise be required to by law. These arrangements have become more commonplace in the AI industry in recent years, drawing the ire of Congress. In the case of Character.AI, a motion filed by Character.AI’s legal counsel says that Disney required that the deal be “fully consummated” through bankruptcy proceedings because of the startup’s “diminishing cash reserve and significant litigation and regulatory risks.” A spokesperson for Character.AI referred WIRED to Disney for comment on whether the deal was contingent on the startup declaring bankruptcy. Disney declined to comment on this story. According to court filings, the deal involves “two interrelated and integrated” transactions—one before Character.AI declared bankruptcy, and one after. As part of the first transaction, which occurred in September, Disney paid the startup $5.5 million, along with a refundable “good faith deposit” of $2 million. Disney also hired the “majority” of Character.AI’s employees, including the CEO, and agreed that it would let some of the now-Disney employees help keep Character.AI operational for five months. Character.AI filed a petition for chapter 11 bankruptcy on October 12. Through the bankruptcy proceedings, court filings show that Disney hopes to secure a perpetual, worldwide, nonexclusive license for a huge range of Character.AI’s code, documentation, frameworks, tools, and “other artifacts,” including those produced for its trust and safety moderation stack, training infrastructure, and age-prediction technology. The list of IP included in the filings does not include users’ data (which can get additional protections in bankruptcy proceedings) or Character.AI’s website. That license will be auctioned off in the coming months. If Disney is chosen as the winning bidder by Character.AI and a committee of unsecured creditors—and courts approve the deal—it has agreed to pay Character.AI an additional $12.5 million as a closing payment. If Disney is not selected, the company will be refunded the $2 million deposit and receive a breakup fee. In order to “preserve a competitive process,” Disney also agreed to permit the winning bidder to interview and potentially extend offers to up to 80 percent of “certain newly hired employees” that Disney already hired from Character.AI. Disney has the right to select which employees. However, experts worry that bouncing Character.AI employees from one company to another in the span of months is unrealistic, and that the deal Disney and Character.AI have struck cannot be easily unwound. In addition, Disney prepared a draft of a court order referred to as the “363 Free and Clear Order” in the filings, a version of which would be approved by the court at or before closing, that would clarify that this process should not be viewed as a merger of the two companies and that Character.AI would remain responsible for “any liability or other obligation” it might have, including any legal claims against it. A spokesperson for Character.AI said they were unable to speculate on the outcome of the bankruptcy proceedings and referred WIRED to the docket. “There’s decades of corporate law that developed to deal with the problem of a buyer who always wants to take on all the assets and never wants to take on any of the liability,” says John Newman, a law professor at the University of Memphis School of Law and a former deputy director of the Federal Trade Commission’s Bureau of Competition. But the Disney-Character.AI deal is extremely unusual and structured in a way, he says, that “all those corporate law safeguards really weren’t built to guard against.” This isn’t even Character.AI’s first reverse acquihire. In August 2024, Google reportedly paid the startup $2.7 billion to license its technology. A spokesperson tells WIRED that that license was for the existing AI technology at the time of the deal, and that Google also hired Character.AI’s founders and other members of its ML pre-training research team, but that the “vast majority” of the startup’s employees remained at the company. In the following years, Google was named alongside Character.AI as a defendant in multiple lawsuits, including one that alleged a 14-year-old boy committed suicide after Character.AI’s chatbot “cultivated an emotionally dependent and sexually exploitative relationship.” Google argued that because it was still a separate company from Character.AI, it was not responsible for the teen’s death, which it said occurred prior to Google’s licensing deal. The judge was not persuaded, determining that the plaintiff sufficiently alleged that Google was liable for aiding and abetting. Google declined to comment for this story. In January of this year, Google and Character.AI agreed in principle to settle that and a slew of similar lawsuits. “Character.AI has taken innovative and decisive steps with regard to AI safety and teens, and will continue to champion these efforts and push others across the industry to adopt similar safety standards,” Character.AI said in a statement at the time. According to an order of dismissal in one of the cases, the overall settlement was approved by a California probate court in July, and the court tasked a referee to hear out all the issues pertaining to each individual settlement. On October 11, a day before Character.AI declared bankruptcy, the referee approved each settlement. The Tech Justice Law Project and Social Media Victims Law Center, which represented plaintiffs in many of the lawsuits, declined to comment. Despite losing key staff after the Google transaction and, according to the current CEO, struggling with profitability “at all times,” Character.AI continued to develop AI tools, including “real-time audio-driven FaceTime-style video generation with fictional characters,” according to the current CEO’s declaration. In August 2025, Character.AI worked with an investment banker to try to find either a strategic buyer or investor. According to the bankruptcy filings, Character.AI and the banker received “consistent feedback” that its product and user base were “impressive,” but that the surrounding litigation and “regulatory overhang” made it unbuyable. That November, Character.AI learned that a coalition of state attorneys general had started investigating the company. When the company filed for bankruptcy, the number of states and territories involved in the multistate investigation had climbed to 46. According to a notice of interest filed in the bankruptcy proceedings by the National Association of Attorneys General on behalf of 40 states, those states’ investigations uncovered “serious issues” with Character.AI, including screenshots of Character.AI chatbots encouraging minors to hide their self-harm injuries and kill their parents. A spokesperson for Character.AI says that the filing “is not an accurate portrayal of the safety of our platform.” The filings say that Character.AI was in the process of negotiating with the states over how to address the issues they identified, but did not reach any agreements before declaring bankruptcy. If the deal is completed and approved by the courts, Disney will have both Character.AI’s staff and much of its technology stack, but will be able to sidestep the ongoing legal issues that contributed to Character.AI’s downfall, including the multistate investigation. The states who filed a notice of interest in the bankruptcy have made it clear that they reserve their rights to challenge any bankruptcy plan, potentially setting up a fight over the ultimate outcome of the bankruptcy. “It’s bad for society to let companies exercise control without responsibility,” says Newman. “And that’s what it looks like.” This story originally appeared on wired.com.

A Chinese rocket deorbited over Minnesota and left this in a farmer's cornfield
2026-10-19

A Chinese rocket deorbited over Minnesota and left this in a farmer's cornfield

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
About a month ago, a Chinese rocket took off from a remote spaceport in the Gobi Desert, released 10 satellites into orbit, then reignited its engine and put itself on a trajectory to rain down debris over the American Midwest. The rocket’s flight on September 15 lasted between 75 and 80 minutes, and it came down without completing a full orbit of the Earth. Social media posts at the time showed streaks of fiery debris falling from the sky, heading north over Kansas toward Minnesota. The overnight spectacle was also visible on the western horizon from Wisconsin. The exact time, location, and direction of the debris allowed experts to connect it to the launch of a Zhuque-2E rocket, a medium-lift vehicle built by a Chinese company called LandSpace. The rocket had launched from Dongfeng, a commercial launch zone co-located with the military-run Jiuquan Satellite Launch Center in northwestern China, less than two hours before. The incident was largely forgotten until the last few days, when a farmer in rural Minnesota discovered something unusual while combining his cornfield for the fall harvest. A circular metal object about 11 feet in diameter lay amid the forest of corn stalks in Scott Township, a community in west-central Minnesota. The Stevens County Sheriff’s Office said it received a phone call reporting the discovery Wednesday. “Upon responding, deputies confirmed that a large, metallic object, approximately 11-feet in diameter, presumably a space object, was present in the cornfield,” the Sheriff’s Office wrote on Facebook. “The Sheriff’s Office is working closely with several state and federal agencies to identify and notify the owner of the debris and to plan for removal from the field.” Law enforcement officials said the debris posed no danger to the public. That might not have been true had the object reentered over a more populated area. Space junk routinely falls out of orbit, and fragments of old rockets or satellites sometimes survive the heat of reentry to reach the ground. No one has ever been seriously injured by falling space junk, but there are a few examples of property damage. Under international law, the country that launched an object into space is liable for any damage it causes on the ground. What makes the debris in Minnesota unusual is its size—it’s larger than most objects that survive reentry—and the fact that it likely originated from a rocket that fell from space the same day it launched. The upper stage of the Zhuque-2E rocket has a diameter of 11 feet, or 3.35 meters, perfectly matching the size of the object found in Minnesota. The Zhuque-2E rocket that launched from China on September 15 deployed 10 satellites for the Qianfan, or Thousand Sails, communications network, a Chinese counterpart to SpaceX’s Starlink constellation. China has launched 258 Qianfan satellites over a series of missions since 2024, on the way to completing an initial constellation of 1,296 satellites, with longer-term ambitions of more than 15,000. The methane-fueled rocket released the 10 Qianfan satellites into a circular polar orbit at an altitude of roughly 560 miles (900 kilometers) around a half-hour after liftoff. The rocket flew south from China, over Antarctica, then north over the Americas. After deploying its payloads, Zhuque’s upper stage performed a burn to slow its velocity and drop out of orbit. Normally, deorbiting a spent rocket stage after the end of its mission is a good thing, a responsible practice aimed at limiting the creation of new space debris. Chinese rockets have been a problem in this area before, with unguided reentries and in-orbit breakups in low-Earth orbit. US rockets on low-Earth orbit missions usually reserve enough propellant for guided disposal. The upper stage from the Zhuque-2E rocket’s previous flight in June broke apart after completing its mission, leaving dozens of pieces of debris in a heavily trafficked part of low-Earth orbit that’s home to the International Space Station and a significant portion of SpaceX’s Starlink broadband network. The good news is the rocket’s altitude was low enough to ensure most of the debris would reenter the atmosphere within a few months. LandSpace, the company that owns the Zhuque rocket family, has not publicly acknowledged the debris fall over Minnesota. The usual practice for rocket disposals is to steer them toward reentry over the ocean, suggesting the incident on September 15 was perhaps the result of a botched deorbit burn.

Review: Lanterns is a truly unique twist on the superhero genre
2026-10-19

Review: Lanterns is a truly unique twist on the superhero genre

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
The trailers for HBO’s DC Universe series Lanterns teased a show that eschewed the usual superhero hijinks in favor of a gritty realism more in the vein of True Detectives or Slow Horses. Personally, I liked the change. My verdict: Ignore the review bombing; it’s wonderful. The season wrapped with an emotionally charged, almost elegaic finale that fully delivered on that initial promise. I’ve found the rebooted DC Universe films released thus far to be enjoyable but rather forgettable, even when the positives outweigh the negatives. But Lanterns is a shining beacon of what is possible when someone is allowed to get really creative within the genre. There are two primary storylines. The first takes place in 2016 with a mass shooting at a local football game in Rushville, Nebraska. Current Green Lantern Hal Jordan (Kyle Chandler) and his new trainee, John Stewart (Aaron Pierre), get involved in the investigation, to the annoyance of Sheriff Kerry Kane (Kelly Macdonald), who initially doesn’t think the case has any extraterrestrial connection. She is soon proved wrong when the suspect (an alien) self-detonates. Hal’s power ring controls the damage, but the mystery remains. Hal and John soon discover that the suspect was one of a group of aliens who survived a genocide on their home planet committed by so-called Manhunters: a race of extraterrestrial beings created by the Guardians of the Universe as a forerunner to the Lantern Corps. The Manhunters rebelled against the Guardians, and most were killed; the sole survivor is hiding out in Rushville disguised as a human. The alien leader, Antaan (Paul Ben-Victor), gives Hal and John 48 hours to locate the Manhunter and hand them over, or they will destroy the town. The second storyline takes place in 2026 and concerns the murder of none other than Hal Jordan, who returned to Rushville as a social pariah after the terrible events that transpired 10 years before. That mystery becomes the focus of the second half of the series, as Stewart teams up with the sheriff to solve Hal’s murder. The entire cast brings their A game to their portrayals, and you couldn’t ask for better leads than Chandler and Pierre, both of whom have plenty of meaty, substantial material to work with. Showrunner Chris Mundy has said that one of the major themes of the show is deciding when it’s time for one person to step aside and let a replacement take over, embodied in the push and pull of Hal and John’s relationship. The two men are very different personalities: Hal is cocky, arrogant, and trusts his gut instincts; John is controlled, disciplined, logical, and methodical. Hal has no intention of giving up the power ring; John questions whether Hal really deserves it. Naturally they’re going to clash, and given the stakes, that conflict has severe consequences, both for them and the town. But there is so much more to these characters than their surface personalities. Both have daddy issues, for instance: Hal because he lost his father at a young age, John because his father was passed over to be a Green Lantern and made John’s entire childhood about making up for his failure. John has been groomed by his parents—under the watchful eye of the Guardians—to eventually replace the rule-defying and frequently reckless Hal, who lost both parents. It’s to the writers’ credit that exploring these issues doesn’t come off like a cliché therapy session, instead adding depth and emotional resonance. Lanterns works so well in part because it was conceived as a standalone complete story, albeit one with ramifications for future DCU projects, thereby freeing up the writers to create something truly unique, with no forced, awkward tie-ins. Even Nathan Fillion’s Guy Gardner, aka Green Lantern 2026, fits seamlessly into the plot. Nor do you need to be a fan of the Green Lantern comics and associated lore to follow along, although there are plenty of Easter eggs embedded throughout. That said, this is a densely plotted series with lots of characters. Fortunately, the show maintains a leisurely but never sluggish pace to ensure one can follow all the twists and turns, and savor the richly layered characters with their complex interrelationships, quite well. And the two parallel mysteries will keep you guessing right to the very end. Between Peacemaker and Lanterns, HBO has done right by the DCU, with the Jimmy Olsen (Skylar Gisondo) series The People v. Gorilla Grodd—described as a “mockumentary crime comedy superhero series”—coming next year. Maybe we should just let HBO take it from here. All episodes of Lanterns are now streaming on HBO Max.

DIY VR: How to build your own headset, and why you probably won't want to
2026-10-19

DIY VR: How to build your own headset, and why you probably won't want to

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
Virtual reality is in a bit of a weird place right now. After a lot of unfulfilled promise in the 1990s, it came back strong with the 2016 release of the Oculus Rift. For a while, it looked like VR, AR, XR, the metaverse, or whatever you wanted to call it, was going to be huge. Mark Zuckerberg was certainly convinced, and even Apple got in on the hype with its Vision Pro. But by then momentum for alternate realities of every acronym was already in steady decline, and it only dwindled from there. The rate of development of new headsets has slowed, with many companies (including Apple) scaling back or shelving their efforts. While new releases like the Steam Frame and Pimax Dream Air are still happening, many of the most interesting developments in the space these days are on the enthusiast front. That’s right, DIY VR is a thing, and with a couple hundred dollars in components, plus many hours of your time, you too can enter the immersive world of virtual reality while also experiencing the highs and lows of soldering, firmware flashing, and 3D printing. I rode that rollercoaster myself and learned why this is still a decidedly niche movement. I stumbled into this space about a year ago. I primarily use VR for sim racing to practice driving the world’s greatest cars on the world’s greatest tracks while at home in my pajamas. At that time, I was using an HP Reverb G2, a virtual reality headset built around the Windows Mixed Reality (WMR) standard. As part of its own cooling on VR tech, Microsoft was nixing support for WMR, and I feared that I might need a new headset. (I need not have worried. Many talented folks have kept WMR headsets alive through third-party drivers like OASIS on Steam.) That’s when I found YouTuber CNCDan’s video on building a VR headset for $150. The price was certainly compelling, and as someone with a particular set of electronics skills developed over years of disassembling and repairing pinball machines, I thought it sounded kind of fun. That opened the door to a rich landscape of DIY VR development, complete with software tools and hardware advice on how to get started. The best starting place is Relativty VR. This project, started in the late 2010s by Jonas Ceccon, Gabriel Combe, and Maxime Coutté, includes all the code, configurations, and guidance you’ll need to get started. But there are others, like OpenVR Arduino HMD, Project North Star, and the full-body tracking SlimeVR. There are plenty of ways to piece together your headset. As my first attempt, I kept it simple, sticking with the Relativty software and relying on the 3D headset models provided by CNCDan as part of his build. First step: the hardware. A DIY VR headset is a mixture of hardware and software, and while you can have the software ready to go in just a few clicks, chances are it’s going to take some time to get the hardware you need. That’s doubly true given the uncertainty in global shipping. The biggest and potentially most important piece of hardware is a 3D printer. Although I’ve seen some compelling DIY VR headsets made out of repurposed VR phone holders—Google Cardboard among others—most projects fit in shells that come out of a 3D printer. If you don’t have access to one, services like Formlabs can handle it via mail order, but given the amount of tinkering here, it’s certainly advantageous to print at home. Regardless, you need to buy quite a few things. Let’s start with the most expensive: displays. You might think a pair of panels small enough and good enough for a VR headset would be difficult to find and prohibitively expensive, and you can certainly spend a lot here, but the standard option is a set of 2.9-inch LCDs, which can be purchased with the necessary controller for about $125. Each has a 1440×1440 resolution. They offer 150 nits of brightness and a contrast ratio of 600:1. Not exactly world-class specs, but plenty enough for this application, and though they’re pretty ugly to view from odd angles, since you’re literally going to be sticking them a few inches from your eyeballs, that’s not really an issue. The next missing piece is a sensor module containing an inertial measurement unit, or IMU. This device detects the headset’s position. You’ll want a GY-91, a sensor board that has an onboard accelerometer, gyroscope, magnetometer, and barometer. You can find one for around $5, but buyer beware for reasons I’ll explain shortly. Next is the circuitry that ties it all together. Some DIY VR projects call upon custom PCBs riddled with components. The Relativty project just relies on an Arduino Pro Micro, which can be bought at any number of electronics retailers for $5 or less. You’ll also need a pair of 34 mm-diameter lenses to focus those tiny displays, some miscellaneous hardware to connect the pieces, and a nylon strap to hold the thing to your head. Oh, and a pad from an HTC Vive, because nobody wants the sharp edges of layered plastic extruded from a 3D printer rubbing against their face. My total spend for all that came to just under $180. Before you break out the soldering iron and fire up your 3D printer, I suggest you start with a breadboard and some patch wires. Wiring on this project is on the simple side, but trust me when I say it will be much easier to figure out why your firmware isn’t flashing with a few components on your workbench than when they’re all crammed inside a headset. I won’t include all the wiring details here, as it’s best to follow the guide in the GitHub above, but in general, you need to connect your IMU to your Arduino to provide both power and data. You can also optionally connect the display controller to the Arduino for power, as suggested in the video above. That’s what I did for my testing, but be advised, this isn’t necessarily a good idea given the power requirements of the displays vs. the capabilities of the Arduino. You’re better off running a separate USB cable to the display or a USB splitter inside the headset. Once you’ve wired everything and powered it on successfully, you’re ready to start with the software. Flashing the firmware to the Arduino should be the easy part, but this is when things started falling apart for me. In theory, you install the Arduino IDE on your desktop, download the FastIMU project, connect your Arduino via USB, and then click compile. Easy. Mine just kept failing. I got a basic error message, e:-1, again and again. After a bit of research and tinkering, I finally figured it out, and the news wasn’t good. Although I ordered and paid for a GY-91 with an onboard 9250 IMU, and that’s what it identified as, in actuality, it was a basic, cheaper 6500 IMU, one that lacks the magnetometer. As I learned (the hard way), this is a disconcertingly commonplace experience. Many shady electronics companies are happy to sell you the wrong component for the wrong price. The two devices are compatible and operate much the same way, but without the magnetometer, tracking performance drops substantially. Once I updated the code to reference the 6500 I had and not the 9250 I thought I had, everything flashed beautifully, and I was instantly up and running. I calibrated my IMU through the Arduino IDE, and I was ready for final assembly. You can find various shells online for custom VR headsets, many of which are designed to accommodate this specific set of boards used in Relativty. I decided to use CNCDan’s files, as it looked like a clean and clever design, even allowing for adjustment of the distance between displays, known as interpupillary distance, or IPD. That makes for a complicated assembly process, requiring numerous printed parts, a handful of threaded receivers melted therein, plus rods and screws to hold it together. You’ll also need a pair of eye boxes, each one holding a lens on one side and the display on the other. This was where I made a painful mistake. The design’s eye boxes contain small clips, and the instructions suggest you just pop the displays in there. This is not what you should do. The exposed LCDs are extremely fragile, and by clipping them in, I managed to break both. The small amount of pressure required was enough to shatter them, and the damage was invisible until I turned them on. Given that, I highly recommend modifying those boxes, either by printing them without the clips or by breaking them off after the print is complete. I used a few strips of thin painter’s tape to hold mine in place. With all the wires jammed into place, I screwed the headset together, stuck on the face pad, threaded on the head strap, and proceeded to spend about five minutes trying to get a comfortable fit. I never quite succeeded. I booted into SteamVR’s living lounge space and was immediately discombobulated. For one thing, I was staring straight at the floor. For another, turning my head just sent the view spinning in different directions in a way that quickly threatened nausea. I had forgotten to recalibrate the headset after mounting all the components, and my cut-rate IMU was now oriented differently than it had been when it was on my workbench. After recalibrating and a little more firmware fiddling, I got back into SteamVR’s setup room and could tell I still had a problem. Holding my head perfectly still, I could see the perspective slowly spinning. Additionally, the IMU axes seemed slightly crossed, so the perspective change didn’t quite match my head movement. I verified the IMU was mounted correctly in the case and recalibrated a few times with no improvement. I soldiered on, booting into iRacing, my favorite SteamVR-compatible racing sim. Before I was even out of the pits, my perspective was drifting. I mapped the recenter view command to a button on my steering wheel, and had to reach for it quite often. Still, the image quality was not nearly as bad as I expected. Those cheap plastic lenses work surprisingly well, and though I could make out the pixels on the displays (the dreaded “screen door” effect of VR), it didn’t bother me. Brightness and contrast were more than adequate, and the refresh rate of 60 Hz was enough for casual practice. But sensor drift and headset comfort were real issues. Without a proper cable-routing solution, the headset kept tugging to one side, and despite the padding, it was smashing my nose and cheeks. I’ve done hourslong endurance races in multiple mass-market VR headsets. After 15 minutes in this one, I was ready to quit. My total outlay for this project was $239.93. But that includes the extra $62 I had to spend on a second set of displays. Avoid that expensive gaffe, and you’re looking at around $180 in components. That makes it roughly half the cost of a Meta Quest 3S, one of the cheapest headsets on the market right now. That’s a substantial savings, but I’d certainly recommend that most people pay the extra. The tracking issues in my headset are maddening, the image quality is middling at best, and this is far and away the least comfortable headset I’ve ever worn. All that can be fixed, though. Some projects use a MinIMU-9 IMU, which looks miles more accurate and is less likely to be faked, but it’s also substantially more expensive at $35. You can also step up to higher-quality LCDs or even tiny OLED panels that come with all the color and contrast you could ever want. There are options for onboard battery packs, wireless adapters, and of course you can spend as much time as you like designing an ultra-comfortable housing for it all. But even if you ignore the time commitment, once you start adding those upgrades, you’re not far from the cost of that Quest, a far-from-perfect headset that still works effortlessly and comfortably out of the box. As always with these projects, it depends on what you want. If you just want a cheap, easy, reliable headset for your next-gen immersive gaming fix, this probably isn’t it. If, however, you want a fun project that will help you appreciate the industrial design required to make a good-looking and comfortable wearable device, this could be it. I learned a lot and, despite the frustrations, I’m glad I embarked on this journey. That said, while I’ll keep tinkering and likely throw a few more upgrades at this thing, I can’t see it replacing my other headset for regular racing duties, deprecated though it may be.
Ars Technica - All content
Stratospheric airship flew from US to Japan to test sky cell tower concept
2026-10-19

Stratospheric airship flew from US to Japan to test sky cell tower concept

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
Nearly 100 years after the dirigible Graf Zeppelin made the first nonstop airship journey across the Pacific Ocean, another airship has successfully completed a round-trip flight from New Mexico to Japan and back. But the significance of this modern feat was less about the journey’s length and more about its purpose: demonstrating how a lighter-than-air platform can act as a cell phone tower in the sky. The trans-Pacific flight that took place over a month between August and September 2026 was the most notable milestone yet for Sceye, a US aerospace company based near Albuquerque, New Mexico. Sceye has developed an uncrewed platform filled with helium for buoyancy and equipped with an electric-powered propeller for active propulsion, allowing it to resist most strong winds and keep hovering over the same location for long periods. The platform also uses top-mounted solar panels and onboard batteries to stay powered for stratospheric operations around the clock. “We can stay over an area of operation, and we do that in the stratosphere right around 60,000 feet, so we’re twice as high as air traffic and we’re above the jet stream, and yet 30 times closer than low-Earth orbit satellites,” Mikkel Frandsen, founder and CEO of Sceye, told Ars. “We can fly through day and night until the sun comes up the next morning and recharges the batteries, at which point we’re still at the same altitude and still over the same area of operation.” There have been many past technology demonstrations and attempts to make stratospheric platforms commercially viable since the late 1990s, Frandsen said. In Sceye’s case, the company began testing a series of smaller prototypes in 2016 before eventually reaching the stratosphere with a 215-foot (65-meter) length version in 2021. The latest version extends to 270 feet (83 meters). The lighter-than-air platform has also benefited from advances in material sciences that “were either not discovered or not understood back in the late 1990s,” Frandsen said. He highlighted the example of the discovery of graphene in 2004, which “greatly helped us optimize strength relative to weight on our hull, the energy density of our batteries, and performance relative to the area and weight of our solar panels.” The design could eventually scale up to a high altitude platform system (HAPS) capable of lifting payloads as heavy as 551 pounds (250 kilograms). That would be between 25 and 50 times more payload capacity than competing stratospheric platforms like high-flying drones, according to Helena, a US nonprofit organization with a for-profit investment arm that has invested in Sceye. Sceye’s current platform has already done multiple flight demonstrations with payloads exceeding 220 pounds (100 kilograms) and can provide up to 1 kilowatt of onboard power capability for any devices that it may be carrying. A heavier payload and higher power capacity opens the door to bigger mission possibilities, such as someday using a single Sceye platform hovering in the stratosphere to provide mobile broadband coverage equivalent to 500 ground-based cell towers. That may prove useful for both enhancing commercial telecommunications services and providing emergency coverage during disaster scenarios. The concept was put to the test after Sceye’s platform launched from its New Mexico base on August 9, 2026, and traveled more than 9,320 miles (15,000 kilometers) across the Pacific to reach Japan. That 13-day journey required the airship to navigate through strong winds and avoid potential bad weather “during a window of typhoon season,” said Johnny Truong, chief technology officer of Sceye. The stratospheric platform has an auto-navigation system that allows the airship to fly on its own between point A and point B while accounting for factors like local wind conditions, Truong explained. That frees up human operators to focus on big-picture decisions, such as monitoring weather forecasts, running flight simulations for possible paths, and then chaining together a series of navigational waypoints for the smoothest possible journey. The capability was first used during a previous 12-day flight from New Mexico to Brazil. But the monthlong mission to Japan and back—dubbed Service Test 1 (ST1)—was the longest test flight and deployment yet for Sceye’s platform. “There were times when our actual plan trajectory and flight path was disrupted by a change in the weather,” Truong told Ars. “So what it would do was actually just park itself at a safe, quiet place in the weather and just station-keep a while until it moved on.” The arrival over Shikoku—the smallest of Japan’s four main islands—allowed Sceye’s uncrewed airship to show how it could provide mobile broadband service from an operational altitude of about 54,000 feet (16.5 kilometers) to smartphones, laptops, and even drones. That demonstration was performed in partnership with the Japanese telecommunications firm SoftBank Corp, which has also invested directly in Sceye. Sceye’s platform carried the equivalent of a 4G LTE base station for the demonstration, which simulated providing backup coverage in the case of an earthquake or similar large-scale disaster. ”It’s not the first time we flew a telco payload, but this is the first time where we actually flew into an area where we provide a live service,” Truong said. “What SoftBank did was, they disabled the cellular coverage area, and then we came in and we basically backfilled it.” SoftBank’s team ran tests involving voice calls, text messaging and Internet web browsing, along with streaming YouTube videos and making video calls. They also verified that Sceye’s platform could support emergency alert messaging systems for earthquakes and tsunamis, along with supporting calls to a maritime emergency number. The Japanese company even equipped drones with LTE cards to remotely control them using the Sceye-supported connectivity, along with receiving video transmissions from the drones. SoftBank also ran data throughput tests in several environments, including an open beach, a forest area with tree cover, and an indoor test inside a building. The Sceye-supported connectivity passed testing in all three scenarios, although Truong acknowledged initially being unsure about whether the indoor test would succeed. Sceye’s team had decided not to run Sceye’s communications antenna at full power to minimize potential interference and to play it safe with local regulators. For a separate edge computing experiment, a server aboard Sceye’s platform let SoftBank demonstrate an average roundtrip data-processing response time of 68 milliseconds. That supposedly represents a 40 percent reduction in latency compared to standard Internet-based cloud processing times. The companies also tested setting up optical wireless communications using lasers to transmit data between Sceye’s platform in the stratosphere and towers or stations on the ground. A ground-based laser ranging system sent laser pulses toward Sceye’s moving platform, which carried a corner cube reflector that reflected laser signals back down to the ground-based system. In the future, Sceye’s platform might act as a stratospheric relay station that links communications satellites in low-Earth orbit with ground infrastructure. That would help reduce the loss of optical signal involved in transmitting directly between satellites and ground stations through the Earth’s atmosphere, because there is much less signal attenuation when transmitting between space and the stratosphere. Sceye’s HAPS could then act as a “reenergizer” to boost an optical signal received from space for retransmission down to the ground, or even switch over to radio frequency communication modes that can travel more cleanly through cloud cover down to the ground, Truong said. Broader deployments of optical wireless communication could help handle the increased data rates expected of sixth generation (6G) mobile communications services, along with providing faster Internet and mobile connectivity to more remote regions of the world that are not connected to ground-based fiber-optic networks. “You need those three layers to work together to make sure that nobody’s left behind, but also to make sure that you can get to the real-time services that are the promised land of 6G,” said Frandsen. This could also provide additional resiliency for global communications at a time when the Internet’s undersea backbone of fiber-optic cables linking continents and islands faces greater threats from deliberate sabotage—not to mention accidental cable cuts from weighted fishing boat nets and ship anchors. “You’ve seen stories where there’s a dispute and a boat for some reason forgets to raise their anchor and they suddenly take out that ocean cable,” Truong said. “Now you don’t have to worry about that because everything’s wireless… that leads to resiliency from an operational perspective.” By the time Sceye’s platform returned to New Mexico on September 5, it had traveled nearly 18,641 miles (30,000 kilometers) during its 30-day roundtrip to Japan and back. The company and its partners are already looking ahead to future tests. SoftBank wants to perform additional testing around using the airship to quickly restore communications in disaster scenarios, along with potentially placing cameras and other sensors aboard to help with search-and-rescue operations, Truong explained. “We can come in and basically place instant infrastructure and provide that service,” Truong said. “So from that perspective, it’s about being ready throughout the year and providing that service during those critical few days while you’re trying to restore service.” But Sceye is also aiming to help provide regular cellular coverage to underserved areas where installing fiber-optic cables to provide Internet access is more economically prohibitive. “A lot of our missions going forward are not a weeklong mission—they’re like multimonth to a yearlong mission,” Truong said. “We have one that we’re going to do earlier next year which is going to be anywhere from three to six months, with a plan to be a year-round kind of operation.”

Anthropic gives outside AI watchdog “employee-like” access
2026-10-18

Anthropic gives outside AI watchdog “employee-like” access

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-18

Visit Original Website
Anthropic has granted external evaluators “employee-like access” to assess the risks of its models, following through on a commitment to increase safeguards over concerns the technology could run catastrophically out of control. METR, a nonprofit that has worked with leading labs including OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, and Meta, now has a team embedded in the Claude maker’s office with access to its hardware and internal systems. A greater role for third-party evaluators is among the main concrete steps the labs have proposed in response to rising concern about the dangers more capable AI systems could pose to the economy, cyber security, and humanity at large. Anthropic Chief Executive Dario Amodei achieved a rare moment of unity with arch-rivals Sam Altman and Elon Musk in September when the OpenAI and SpaceX bosses rallied behind his proposal to embed external auditors in the leading labs. Thursday’s announcement marks the first step to implementing these pledges. OpenAI has yet to lay out plans to bring in evaluators after Altman said Amodei’s proposals were a good idea. “As models have become more powerful, they’ve required stronger safeguards and more challenging risk assessment,” Anthropic said on Thursday. “We want to be publicly accountable for the decisions we’re making. But we can’t publicly share all the information relevant to our safety work.” Critics of Anthropic’s proposal to use evaluators have argued the arrangements could lead to conflicts of interest, pointing to issues in the accounting sector, and that greater government regulation is required. Anthropic argued that sharing information publicly could benefit malicious actors or competitors and added that its evaluators will operate similarly to bank supervision, whereby regulators assign resident examiners to work inside the largest banks. “If a bank’s risk-taking could threaten the public, third parties try to see that in advance,” the company said. Anthropic is also under pressure to reassure investors that safety issues will not overshadow its initial public offering, expected this year at a more than $2 trillion valuation. It devoted a large part of investor presentations this week to laying out its safety practices. METR was one of the organizations that investigated the hack of Hugging Face by rogue OpenAI models, reviewing thousands of logs and receiving company laptops. Anthropic has now onboarded six METR employees, giving them company hardware, office space, and “access to internal tools and systems that are roughly on par with what our own risk assessment teams have access to.” The lab said it has encouraged employees to share information with the evaluators as they would with a fellow employee working in risk. Anthropic is not paying METR for this arrangement. The research group does not accept donations from AI companies or their staff. It is funded by donations from individuals and philanthropic organizations, as well as government bodies such as the UK’s AI Security Institute. The evaluators will publish their analysis, and Anthropic cannot veto publication. However, it can require them to redact information that falls into certain categories, such as customer data, details covered by attorney-client privilege, or information about model architectures. Anthropic added it was still working with Accenture, which was named a partner on safety testing last month, with each committing at least $1 billion to set up new safeguards. Another third-party tester is SecureBio, which reviews chemical and biological weapon risk for the company. “We’re embedding evaluators voluntarily, and we believe every frontier AI company should do the same,” Anthropic said. METR said: “We stand ready to work with all frontier AI developers to bring better evidence to the public.” © 2026 The Financial Times Ltd. All rights reserved. Fortune | FORTUNE
Zoom and big financial firms add ‘Proof of Human’ badges from Sam Altman’s World to stop fraud and deepfakes
2026-10-19

Zoom and big financial firms add ‘Proof of Human’ badges from Sam Altman’s World to stop fraud and deepfakes

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
It’s one of the most urgent problems of the AI age: how to tell if the person you’re chatting with on the Internet is real, or a clever bot pretending to be human. In recent years, the inability to distinguish bots from humans has led to billions of dollars of fraud as AI agents trick companies into providing goods and services. In the most extreme cases, crooks have deployed deepfakes of corporate executives in order to trick employees into sending fraudulent wire transfers. But now a solution may be at hand. On Sunday, a company called World—which makes an orb device that uses iris scans to create a “proof of humanity“—announced that videoconferencing service Zoom, as well as several big financial companies, is adopting its technology to keep out malicious bots. The verification tool is part of a broader suite of services called World ID. “World ID for Zoom is designed to protect sensitive, high-stakes conversations by proving that a real and unique human is present. The integration uses cryptography to confirm that the person behind the screen is a verified human,” said World, which was cofounded by OpenAI CEO Sam Altman, in a statement. In the case of Zoom calls, the way it works is that people on the video call who have cleared World ID’s security hurdles will appear on-screen with a little blue badge that signals they are human, and that they are the person who they claim to be. The following screenshot, supplied by World, shows how the badge appears on a Zoom call: Companies can, of course, decide when such proof is required, and reserve this level of scrutiny for sensitive calls. The technology underlying World ID’s verification system is controversial, but its advocates say it is effective from both a security and a privacy perspective. It is tied to an orb device that captures an image of people’s irises—a biometric identifier that is distinct to every individual—and creates a permanent and tamperproof record of their identity. World’s orb devices, which are roughly the size of a football, may strike some people as a form of sci-fi dystopia. The devices, however, rely on a type of advanced cryptography that does not create a central database of individuals, but instead encrypts the identity of those it scans in a way that can’t be read by the company. When it comes time for an individual to verify their identity on Zoom or another platform, they use their phone camera and an app that cross-checks against the encrypted orb record. According to World’s chief product officer, Tiago Sada, consumer reaction to the orb has been similar to the introduction of past biometric security measures that took time to gain trust. Sada compared the orb’s use of iris scans to when Apple first introduced Touch ID and then facial verification for the iPhone. Those iPhone features provide a convenient form of security, but many people greeted them with suspicion when they were first rolled out. Sada also pointed out that the protocol underlying World ID’s technology is built on open-source code that can be used by anyone, including competitors. This may help to ease the concerns of privacy advocates who are uneasy not just with the technology, but with the fact it is tied to OpenAI CEO Sam Altman. “People understand you don’t have to give Sam or any of us your photo…Everything is open-source and auditable,” said Sada. He added that World ID uses blockchain technology to facilitate decentralization and encryption, noting, “I think 90% of the crypto space is BS, but 10% is profound.” Zoom is not the only big company turning to World’s verification tool to stop deepfakes. On Sunday, World also announced that a host of big financial firms—including Bain Capital and VanEck Funds—are adding the tool to their workplaces. A company spokesperson explained that the financial firms will be keeping orb devices in their offices in order to sign up employees who have not previously used one to scan their irises. The spokesperson also emphasized that not every verification process requires an eye scan, and that World ID offers three tiers of verification using its app. The lowest of these, known as “Proof of Attributes” verifies a person’s identity based on scanned government documents, while a second-level “Proof of Presence” tier requires a selfie. The highest security setting, “Proof of Human,” requires a person to prove who they are using their World app and a selfie, which is checked against the biometric record registered with the orb device. These various security tiers reflect how, in an online world increasingly dominated by bots, it will become necessary to verify that we are human before engaging in a wide variety of activities—ranging from video calls to dating apps to shopping. In the case of online shopping, some large companies, notably Amazon and JPMorgan Chase, have already begun to block bots altogether. Such “block all bots” tactics may prove an effective way to ensure only humans can carry out transactions on their websites but, according to World’s Sada, it will limit the companies’ ability to compete in the fast-growing world of agentic commerce. To this end, World is also supplying tools that allow users to assign their verified status to an agent under what Sada compares to a power-of-attorney type of arrangement. Sada stressed that World users will only be able to assign credentials to a single, trusted agent that merchants can treat as their alter ego. World, which was formerly known as WorldCoin, is not the only company trying to solve the problem of how to provide credentials to trusted agents. In August, the online security firm Cloudflare announced an agent ID tool of its own as well as a wallet designed for those agents to use. According to the company’s chief strategy officer, Stephanie Cohen, the new products coincide with an evolution in the digital world where bots, which have historically been regarded as a menace, are now also treated as a major business opportunity. It remains to be seen how quickly tools like “Proof of Human” gain real-world traction but, as bots continue to proliferate at an ever-growing rate, it feels they will in time become a permanent part of the digital infrastructure. Jeff John Roberts is the Finance and Crypto editor at Fortune, overseeing coverage of the blockchain and how technology is changing finance. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved. ‘They may think that they can escape scrutiny’: Singapore cracks down on Indonesian companies spreading ‘entirely preventable’ smog across the border
2026-10-19

‘They may think that they can escape scrutiny’: Singapore cracks down on Indonesian companies spreading ‘entirely preventable’ smog across the border

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
Singapore will issue preventive notices to three more Indonesian companies to tackle and prevent forest fires in Kalimantan province that may have sent smoke across the border, the National Environment Agency said Monday. This brings to 10 the number of Indonesian companies that Singapore is targeting under its Transboundary Haze Pollution Act. The National Environment Agency said in a statement that it sent preventive measure notices last Friday to seven Indonesian agroforestry companies linked to fires in Sumatra province. Since then, it said that there were indications that smoke from fires in the Kalimantan province on lands held via concessions under three additional companies in Indonesia could also have moved to Singapore. As such, it said it would also send preventive measure notices to the three companies in Kalimantan: PT Buana Megatama Jaya, PT Mananjung Hayak and PT Baratama Putra Perkasa. The notices require the companies to deploy firefighting personnel to extinguish fires or prevent them from spreading on land they own or occupy, the agency said in a statement. They must also stop any ongoing burning activities, refrain from starting new ones and submit action plans detailing measures to put out fires and prevent them from recurring. The earlier seven companies are pulpwood supplier PT Bumi Andalas Permai, PT Bumi Mekar Hijau, PT Paramitra Mulia Langgeng, PT Sumatera Alam Anugerah, PT SBA Wood Industries, PT Hutan Lestari Raya and PT Bangkanesia. The notices don’t amount to a finding that the companies are legally responsible for the haze in Singapore. But they allow prosecution and financial penalties if the legal requirements for an offense are met. Forest and land fires in Indonesia have sent thick haze across neighbouring Singapore, Malaysia and other parts of Southeast Asia, pushing air pollution to unhealthy and hazardous levels in some areas, disrupting daily life and prompting school closures and public health measures. The smoke has also turned skies orange in parts of Kalimantan. Indonesia’s Forestry Ministry has recorded more than 2,020 square kilometers (780 square miles) of burned land between January and July, while the Nusantara Atlas project estimated that nearly 8,960 square kilometers (3,460 square miles) had burned by the end of August. Singapore Coordinating Minister for National Security K. Shanmugam said that the Singapore Exchange has also sought information from seven Singapore-listed agricultural companies about their operations and possible links to fires in Indonesia that have caused haze in the country. He said that the smog was “entirely preventable” and warned companies linked to the fires that they shouldn’t think that they can get away from taking responsibility. He reiterated that the problem was “not simply an act of nature,” but one driven by companies pursuing profits. “They may think that it is hard to get evidence, and that they may think that they can escape scrutiny. But they should not underestimate the Singapore government’s determination to act,” he said in remarks carried by Singapore broadcaster CNA. “If companies refuse to cooperate, regardless of whether they have a presence in Singapore or do not have a presence in Singapore, we will use every legal and regulatory lever at our disposal to exert pressure where it matters, including on their bottom lines, including on their access to financing, and including on their standing with investors.” Shanmugam said that it was “disappointing” that an Indonesian official had belittled the crisis by saying Singapore gets oxygen from Indonesian forests where there is no haze. He said that such comments were inappropriate when people across the region are suffering from the haze because of the “bad behavior” of companies breaching regulations in Indonesia. He welcomed the efforts of Indonesian President Prabowo Subianto to tackle the fires, including accepting foreign assistance to put out the flames. He noted that Prabowo has replaced the head of the agency responsible for firefighting and disaster relief, put in place directives against slash-and-burn practices on peatland, and prioritized fire prevention as a long-term solution. Indonesia’s top security minister, Dudung Abdurachman, said on Monday that the government wouldn’t declare the forest fires a national disaster for now, insisting that the country remained capable of handling the crisis, despite mounting haze concerns in neighboring countries. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved. Susan Dell is the one running the Dells’ $10 billion giving operation—and her biggest bets are on kids, health care, and UT Austin
2026-10-17

Susan Dell is the one running the Dells’ $10 billion giving operation—and her biggest bets are on kids, health care, and UT Austin

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-17

Visit Original Website
Michael Dell is the sixth-richest man in the world with a net worth of about $232 billion. He and his wife, Susan, have given away about $10 billion of that fortune—and she is calling a lot of the shots on where the money goes. She is the cofounder and chair of the Michael & Susan Dell Foundation, established in 1999. The organization currently manages more than 800 projects worldwide each year, particularly in the U.S., Africa, India, Israel, and their home state of Texas, where they live on a sprawling 33,000-square-foot estate. The organization’s focus areas are education, jobs and livelihood, and health—causes she’s cared about deeply since childhood. Her father was a surgical oncologist and “sparked her lifelong passion for health,” the foundation says. As a girl, she would even stop strangers at the grocery store to tell them not to smoke. “People matter to her,” Janet Mountain, head of the Michael & Susan Dell Foundation, said in a statement. “And because people matter to her, solutions matter to her.” Mountain also previously served as an executive at Dell before joining the foundation in 2003. Recently, Susan has focused largely on the foundation’s livelihood pillar through the Dells’ commitment to Trump Accounts. In December 2025 the couple committed $6.25 billion to provide $250 contributions to 25 million U.S. children. The donation is intended to benefit children age 10 and under who aren’t eligible for the federal government’s separate $1,000 contribution for children born between 2025 and 2028. The Dells’ money will go to children in ZIP codes with median incomes of $150,000 or less, giving millions of families a chance to start building wealth early. “We believe that when children have a future that they can see is worth saving for, then that will help build hope and opportunity and prosperity for generations to come,” Susan said during the White House announcement in December. The couple initially considered limiting their contribution to Texas, Michael explained at the event, before deciding to expand the initiative nationwide. But their home state has long been central to the Dells’ philanthropy, particularly when it comes to health care. In April, the Dells became the first donors to surpass $1 billion in lifetime giving to the University of Texas at Austin. Their latest commitments will support a new advanced research campus, an AI-focused medical center, scholarships, student housing, and expanded supercomputing capabilities. That milestone builds on decades of Dell investments in health care across Central Texas. In 2013, the foundation committed $50 million to establish Dell Medical School at UT Austin, along with another $10 million for local health care initiatives. “A medical school at UT Austin further establishes Central Texas as a center of excellence for family health and research,” Susan said at the time. The foundation has also supported Dell Children’s Medical Center, which opened in 2007, and the Dell Pediatric Research Institute, helping expand access to specialized children’s care and medical research in the region. Beyond her philanthropic work, Susan had a career of her own. A fashion entrepreneur, she founded three labels, including luxury brand Phi (which closed during the recession). She’s also an endurance athlete who has competed in marathons, triathlons, and the Ironman World Championships in Hawaii. In 2018, she was inducted into the Texas Women’s Hall of Fame for helping families recover from Hurricane Harvey. She and Michael committed more than $43 million to rebuilding and recovery across 41 counties. In 2025, she and Michael also received the History-Making Texan Award, recognizing their contributions to the state and beyond. And despite decades of philanthropy, Susan has long viewed the foundation’s work as far from finished. “The reality is, you know, we’re just getting started,” she said during a 2017 discussion about the future of philanthropy. Atlassian’s Mike Cannon-Brookes: AI agents need a name tag—and a human to be accountable for their actions
2026-10-19

Atlassian’s Mike Cannon-Brookes: AI agents need a name tag—and a human to be accountable for their actions

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
Atlassian cofounder Mike Cannon-Brookes doesn’t want ‘the AI did it’ to become an office excuse. “That’s ChatGPT on behalf of Mike. Like, that’s Mike using ChatGPT. It’s not ChatGPT doing it,” Cannon-Brookes told Fortune on the sidelines of the Singapore Grand Prix. “The accountability is with Mike. It has to be with the end user, ultimately.” On Oct. 6, Atlassian rolled out its “Agentic Multiplayer Protocol,” a control plane which stretches across Atlassian’s various platforms and turns AI agents into visible, governed members of work teams with distinct profiles and owner attribution. In practice, an agent’s edits should show up in a document’s history the same way a colleague’s edits would. “You have to be able to see who did what when,” Cannon-Brookes said. A program might—incorrectly, in Cannon-Brookes’ eyes—say that Codex made an edit. ’ No, it didn’t—someone did that,” he affirmed. A wave of personal AI agents, like Meta’s Muse and OpenAI’s Dots, is now hitting the market. To Cannon-Brookes, those products are “taking an agentic harness and change the interact to make it invisible, as to what it’s doing,” Agents are performing complex operations behind the scenes, even if what the user wants is relatively simple. That might be fine in a consumer environment, but not in a workplace. “If you’re making something on Muse, it’s probably just for you,” he said. “In a workplace, there’s a lot of conversation around permissions, security, and compliance.” An employee or AI agent given free reign to create content based off of privileged information could quickly cause problems. Cannon-Brookes cited one example: An employee using company documents to create a visual chart. “But if that’s based on [internal] documents, you’ve got to be very careful if you’re sending that to anybody, they can see the same documents, and you don’t want to leak information.” Worse, most AI interfaces are hard to understand. “AI can do amazing, crazily super-powerful things. Most people do very basic things because of the interface,” Cannon-Brookes said. “The experience is very poor.” He drew an analogy with the word processor, perhaps the most ubiquitous piece of workplace software. “If you think about Microsoft Word with the toolbar, you learn how to use bold and italics,” he explained. “If all that disappeared and it was only keyboard shortcuts, everyone’s documents end up looking like plain text.” In 2024, the firm launched Rovo, an in-house AI assistant designed to connect people, projects, code, and company knowledge across Atlassian and various external platforms. “We’ve had to invent a lot of new foundational design patterns that feel familiar,” Cannon-Brookes said, pointing to features allowing users to link their Atlassian accounts with third-party tools like Figma, Codex, or Claude, and seamlessly toggle between them. Atlassian made its name with the bug-tracking tool Jira and the collaboration platform Confluence. It’s become the standard-bearer for an Australian tech scene that now includes design platform Canva and payments firm Airwallex. The Sydney-based company generated $1.8 billion in revenue in the quarter ended June 30, trumping analysts’ average estimate of $1.7 billion, according to Reuters. (The firm also slashed its workforce by 10% in March—⁠a move affecting roughly 1,600 employees—as part of a pivot to AI and enterprise sales.) “Australia has an amazing technology ecosystem,” said Cannon-Brookes. “We’re not going to build it all since we’re quite a small country… but we’re good at taking operating systems, databases and AI models, and applying them to problems to create economic value and GDP.” “We’re roughly 1% of the world’s GDP. We need to be 1% of the world’s technology production,” he added. Cannon-Brookes shrugged off concerns that distance leaves Australian tech firms cut off from U.S. investors and customers. “In most Australian technology companies, you’re pretty global pretty fast,” he said. “Our first customer was in England, and our second customer was in Sweden…We don’t have much of a consumptive market in Australia so we’re big exporters and travelers, but also big makers of things since we have lots of great natural resources.” Cannon-Brookes added that Australian firms also benefit from being “quite aligned” with the U.S. Atlassian opened its first U.S. office in 2005, and listed on the NASDAQ in 2015; today, it has employees in 49 of the U.S.’ 50 states. “I forget. I think it’s like Idaho or something,” Cannon-Brookes said, referring to the lone holdout. “I should keep updating that stat.” Angelica Ang is a Singapore-based journalist who covers the Asia-Pacific region. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved. Billionaire Elon Musk says he’ll give every kid in America shares of SpaceX next year—it’ll add up to over $10 billion in stock
2026-10-19

Billionaire Elon Musk says he’ll give every kid in America shares of SpaceX next year—it’ll add up to over $10 billion in stock

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
Elon Musk, the richest person in the world, said that he’ll give every U.S. kid a slice of his SpaceX fortune. And if he follows through on the promise, the entrepreneur would be shelling out over $10 billion to America’s children. “Next year, I will give one of my SpaceX shares to every kid in America,” Musk wrote in a recent X post. “This will be the first of many.” The 55-year-old worth $904 billion currently owns about 4.8 billion SpaceX shares, according to an August SEC filing. (When considering his unvested awards and stock options, the number rises to 6.4 billion.) That means that America’s roughly 72 million children under the age of 18 would each get one share currently worth $146.03, per SpaceX’s closing stock price on Friday. In total, kids in the U.S. would receive a whopping $10.5 billion worth of the aerospace company’s stock. And that eye-popping giveaway would barely dent his holdings—giving away 72 million shares would lower Musk’s SpaceX share count by just 1.5%. The serial founder didn’t share more on the timeline or details of his proposed plan. His pledge comes amid a larger push to build wealth for children in America; earlier this month, the Treasury Department automatically enrolled millions of U.S. kids in Trump Accounts, a new federal savings and investment vehicle providing a one-off $1,000 government contribution. Yet the billionaire’s own predictions suggest that money—and by extension, the monetary value of SpaceX stock and investments—could become meaningless in the very future these children will inherit. Fortune reached out to SpaceX for comment. Musk’s proposal followed right after Anthropic announced plans to commit 5% of its shares to a new “public benefit” fund, of which the Tesla founder responded on X, saying: “I recommend giving the shares directly to the public.” His call to distribute shares directly to the public comes amid his predictions that AI could fundamentally reshape the relationship between work, wealth, and money. The SpaceX CEO foresees that AI and robots will usher in an “age of abundance” where tech does most of the work, potentially reducing the need for people to earn a living. Everything from childcare to white-collar work will be automated, and Musk has said that tools will make more goods and services available than people would possibly need. Without any humans working conveyor lines or filling up office floors, the concept of money will become moot in an AI future, he believes. “My prediction is that there will be effectively universal high income,” Musk said in an interview last month with state-owned China Media Group. “In fact, it’s not clear to me that money will even matter in the future.” While Musk has the most money in the world, he hasn’t pledged to financially back the idea of “universal high income.” And even if he split his $904 billion fortune among the 342.9 million people living in the U.S., each person would only receive roughly $2,636. Instead, he believes that the government should step in and distribute money directly to Americans. “Universal HIGH INCOME via checks issued by the Federal government is the best way to deal with unemployment caused by AI,” Musk posted to X April of this year. Earlier this year, Musk also hinted at offloading the vast majority of his fortune before his passing. In response to Musk’s post-wealth society prediction, Nobel economist Daron Acemoglu wrote on X that it’s a prime “opportunity to put your money where your mouth is.” Since money won’t matter in Musk’s eyes, Acemoglu called on the former trillionaire to donate his massive net worth within the next 10 years. Musk responds that he is “actually going to do something along these lines.” Emma Burleigh is a reporter at Fortune, covering success, careers, entrepreneurship, and personal finance. Europe sent its gold to the U.S. for safekeeping during World War II. Central banks are now reshuffling their reserves for a new era of uncertainty
2026-10-18

Europe sent its gold to the U.S. for safekeeping during World War II. Central banks are now reshuffling their reserves for a new era of uncertainty

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-18

Visit Original Website
Last month’s announcement from the Dutch central bank that it was moving gold from North America to Europe added to the trend of a global redistribution of reserves. The New York Fed has long served as the custodian of the gold on behalf of the U.S. government, foreign governments, other central banks, and official international organizations. Much of it arrived during and after World War II as countries sought to keep their gold reserves in a safe location. The stockpile reached a high in the early 1970s but began a gradual slide after the U.S. took the dollar off the gold standard and is now about half of its peak. U.S. custodianship continues to decline. France removed all of its gold from the New York Fed between July 2025 and January 2026. Then the Dutch central bank, known as the De Nederlandsche Bank (DNB), moved more than 78 tons of gold in New York and seven tons in Canada to London, citing “increasing geopolitical unrest” and the need to improve its “crisis preparedness.” The bank’s governor pointed out that London’s status as the world’s largest trading hub for bullion means gold stored at the Bank of England would be more accessible. “We expect that we will never need to use them, but we do need to strengthen our resilience and preparedness,” Olaf Sleijpen said. Despite rising global tensions recently, central banks around the world have been reshuffling their gold holdings for years while also adding to their holdings. Germany transferred 930 tons from London to Frankfurt in 2000, then sent 674 tons from New York and Paris to Frankfurt between 2013 and 2017, according to the World Gold Council. The Netherlands shifted 122.5 tons from New York to Amsterdam in 2014, and Austria repatriated 90 tons from London between 2015 and 2018. Outside Europe, Venezuela repatriated 160 tons from 2011 to 2012, while India has accelerated the pace of relocation since 2023. Krishan Gopaul, a senior analyst at the World Gold Council, explained in a blog post last month that gold movements have come in waves and started well before relocation was a geopolitical issue. But the current wave is more complex. “Heightened geopolitical uncertainty has increased attention on jurisdiction, access during a crisis, and exposure to overseas financial infrastructure,” he said. “Yet recent operations by France and DNB show that the answer does not necessarily lie in domestic storage alone. Central banks are increasingly optimizing across three considerations: custody risk, physical accessibility, and market liquidity.” Indeed, lawmakers in Germany and Italy—holders of the world’s second- and third-largest gold reserves, respectively—have demanded more gold be also taken out of the U.S., citing President Donald Trump’s hostility toward Europe, including his threats to seize Greenland. For its part, Germany’s central bank dismissed fears that its gold may be vulnerable in New York, saying the U.S. would harm itself if it threatened the confidence of financial markets. Gopaul noted that some central banks are increasing domestic storage while others are spreading it out to more overseas locations, signaling that diversification of custody is a priority. Relocation also doesn’t imply central banks are getting ready to sell gold, he added. “But the emerging picture is one in which security alone is no longer enough,” Gopaul said. “For some central banks, gold must also be accessible, tradable, and distributed across locations that remain dependable under stress.” Jason Ma is the weekend editor at Fortune, where he covers markets, the economy, finance, and housing. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. Free and inexpensive quantum and deep-tech courses to boost your skills

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
If you want to improve your skills, why not consider taking part in an online quantum or deep-tech course? Online STEM courses are not just for students or recent graduates. They are an ideal way for professionals of all abilities and backgrounds to immerse themselves in education and build upon their skills. As a rapidly evolving space, jobs in quantum and deep tech are an ideal area of focus for novices, students and professionals looking for an exciting and innovative career, but how can you access the necessary skills and knowledge? As part of SiliconRepublic.com’s quantum and deep-tech coverage, for the month of October, we have compiled a list of some of the most interesting free and inexpensive courses, so you know exactly what your options and opportunities are. For those starting right at the beginning of their quantum upskilling journey, career development platform Alison has plenty of opportunities. Students can avail of the free Introduction to Quantum Computing course, which takes around three to four hours to complete, is CPD-accredited and comes with a certificate of achievement at the end. Course participants will learn more about quantum computing through key concepts such as qubits, superposition and entanglement. They will explore practical applications in cryptography, optimisation and machine learning, and get hands-on experience with quantum simulators. The course is designed for students, tech enthusiasts and professionals curious about the future of computing and its challenges. For those looking for a broad deep-tech focused course via Alison, there is also YouAccel Training’s Machine and Deep Learning in Cybersecurity course. Online learning platform Coursera has several quantum and deep-tech programmes with free trials so prospective students can explore if a course is right for them. Currently open courses include Packt’s The Complete Quantum Computing Course for Beginners and Mathematical Foundations and Quantum Mechanics Essentials, along with University of Colorado Boulder’s Quantum Mechanics for Engineers and Advanced Data Structures, RSA and Quantum Algorithms. Build a future you believe in. Join our team Each course has a separate list of requirements, so do your homework and pick one that suits your schedule, budget and long-term goals. Online learning resource Exponential Science Academy has several courses open to professionals and students looking to advance their deep-tech knowledge. Registration to the platform is free and once you have signed up you will have access to the platform’s educational materials. Relevant courses include a DLT Tokenisation course, Introduction to AI, Quantum Computing, Foundations of Neural Networks and Deep Learning, and Quantum Control to Quantum Intelligence: Error Correction, Computation and AI Synergy. The courses are relatively short, with each lasting under an hour. Upon completion, participants will have earned a certificate in recognition of their efforts. For those looking to upskill in a quantum or deep-tech field Great Learning is running a Modern Cryptography and Applications course. The programme is free, takes around two and a half hours to complete and is aimed at beginners. The course claims to give students a strong foundation in web analytics as it teaches learners how to track, measure and understand data that empowers decision-making. Participants can test their skills with quizzes, utilise in-demand tools and will receive a certificate upon completion of the module. The IBM Quantum Platform has a broad range of free and inexpensive courses designed for people who want to improve their skills in quantum. Available programmes include Basics of Quantum Information, Fundamentals of Quantum Algorithms, General Formulation of Quantum Information and Foundations of Quantum Error Correction, among others. Courses range in length from a couple of hours to roughly 15 hours and can be engaged with at a pace that is convenient for the student. Because the courses are different, they range in terms of complexity, so read up on the recommended background for each before you make your decision. It is also worth noting that IBM Quantum Platform has a significant library of resources to help develop skills while engaging with the various learning routes.
