Saturday’s update builds on DeepSeek’s V4.1-Flash, which the company released in September and marketed as a “smarter, faster, more efficient” model. The release of V4.1-Flash, which operated on lowered token prices, caused share prices to drop for a number of DeepSeek’s rivals. The global race for AI dominance has been accelerating in recent months, with open-weight models becoming a crucial influence. Open-weight models are considered necessary for AI leadership, as being able to tweak a model means organisations, from start-ups to public institutions, can build on advanced models without training one from scratch. While Chinese companies predominantly lead the open-weights space, various global rivals have been working on catching up. Last week, US tech giant Meta unveiled Muse Spark Open, which the company called the “most capable” US open-weight model to date. The multimodal model has 816bn total parameters, with 42bn active parameters and a 1m-token context window. According to the company, it is built for coding, agentic work and other complex, multi-step reasoning tasks. Meanwhile, earlier this month, French AI company Mistral announced its latest open-weight AI model Mistral Large 4, which it said “significantly outperforms” any open-weight model developed in the US or Europe. Known as ‘Le Chonk’, ML4 is a 1trn-parameter natively multimodal model with 49bn active parameters, and Mistral’s “largest and most capable model to date”. Silicon Republic
Is there a hidden bias in which species researchers choose to study?
2026-10-19

Is there a hidden bias in which species researchers choose to study?

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
Pietro Pollo and Michael Kasumovic of UNSW explore the external factors that can often dictate which species and organisms are studied and which are ignored. A version of this article was originally published by The Conversation (CC BY-ND 4.0) Our knowledge about life on Earth is built through research on how organisms function, evolve and interact with each other and the environment. Yet there are far more species on our planet than scientists can study in detail. So how should scientists decide which species to focus on? Fairly representing all species seems like a useful approach. It’s like predicting an election result – we can’t interview every citizen, so we need a sample that truly represents the overall population. What drives scientists to uncover nature’s mysteries is curiosity. So, the organisms chosen for research are determined by what makes scientists most curious. And that’s causing a problem with fair representation. In our new research, we examined research in ecology and evolution (an umbrella term for biological sciences outside biomedicine) to understand which animals are most studied and why. Our results suggest emotion plays a bigger role in research choices than scientists usually admit. Our world is mostly filled with invertebrates: creatures such as bugs, spiders, worms and slugs. Vertebrates (amphibians, birds, fish, mammals and reptiles) make up only 5pc of all animal species on Earth. So, if scientists want to represent animals fairly in their work, most research should be on invertebrates. To understand how scientists’ time is spent researching the diversity of animals, we analysed almost 1.4m scientific articles from more than 600 journals. Unsurprisingly, we found that more than half of these articles focused on vertebrates, demonstrating a huge bias in what scientists prefer to study. Our results also demonstrate things haven’t improved in more than two decades (when this bias was first discovered), and in some cases have gotten worse. For example, the focus on fish in marine and freshwater biology research increased from 35.8pc of articles in 2001 to 49.7pc in 2025. This was all at the expense of the largest group of marine creatures, aquatic invertebrates such as molluscs, crustaceans, corals and more. The bias we found was compounded by social media and news coverage, where popular animal groups attracted more attention from other scientists and the general public. Many scientists are therefore no different to everyone else in which animals they deem interesting or important. Although many factors determine which research organisms scientists might select, emotions do play a significant role. Using an online survey, we asked 868 animal behaviour researchers about their experiences and preferences. We found that popular groups in animal behaviour research, such as birds and mammals, were regarded more favourably than others, especially arachnids (such as spiders). This is perhaps unsurprising, as perceptions of animals are shaped early in childhood. Ingrained societal values, such as which animals are cute, tend to persist by the time someone becomes a scientist. Fortunately, we found that familiarity breeds admiration. Participants that worked with a certain animal group tended to view that group more positively after their experience. In other words, they tried it and liked it. Studying the saliva of the Gila monster (a lizard) in the 1980s paved the way for recently developed weight loss drugs such as Ozempic. Research on snake, spider and scorpion toxins is being used for new pain-relieving drugs. The discovery of green fluorescent proteins from bioluminescent jellyfish in the 1960s led to the 2008 Nobel Prize because it sparked a scientific revolution by making it possible to tag individual cells. These examples show that scientific advancement often requires serendipity and time. It disregards whether the organisms involved are charismatic. Creatures commonly seen as ugly, disgusting, or dangerous (which they rarely are) can be as, if not more, useful for society as fluffy, pretty animals. Our work indicates that society’s negative perceptions of certain organisms, which are often rooted in disconnection from nature and misinformation, are shared by the very scientists responsible for understanding biological processes and patterns. Understanding scientists’ choices matters because these decisions shape the direction of scientific progress. We urgently need to shift our perception of animals because human-made effects are accelerating biodiversity losses. For example, insects are declining at unprecedented rates, but we know little (if anything) about most insect species. Many haven’t even been described and may go extinct before we even know they existed. This means we’ll lose not just the benefits they bring to ecosystems, but any benefits they may have held for humanity. There is hope. Our research shows that when people spend more time with the ‘ugly’ and ‘scary’ animals, they often appreciate them more. Once people realise that all organisms (even spiders) play a vital part in ecosystems, it becomes a lot easier to give these overlooked creatures the attention they deserve. Organisations like Invertebrates Australia and many social media influencers are already helping by showing just how fascinating and important invertebrates really are. But we still need more support to reduce research biases, especially from governments and universities. They can make a huge difference by funding research on never-before-studied species and neglected animal groups. If nothing changes, we risk slowing progress because we’re drawn to what looks cute. Next time an animal video pops up on your feed, try sharing something beyond the fluffy favourites. The less glamorous species are just as essential and just as worthy of our curiosity. Pietro Pollo is a visiting fellow for the Evolution and Ecology Research Centre at UNSW. A behavioural ecologist and evolutionary biologist, his main focus is on topics related to reproduction. Michael Kasumovic is an evolutionary biologist and an ARC future fellow at UNSW. There, he is a part of the ecology and evolution research group, in which he explores how individuals maximise their reproductive fitness in a constantly changing world, particularly highly variable social environments. He approaches these questions from a behavioural, ecological and evolutionary perspective using both quantitative and genomic approaches. His primary focus is on non-human animals. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Sign up for the Daily Brief, Silicon Republic’s digest of need-to-know sci-tech news. Related: Guest Column, The Conversation, researchers, biology, research

Tech impacted under the new proposal could include that used to ⁠make perovskite and tandem solar cells, as well as large-scale fluidised-bed polysilicon production technologies, according to Reuters. Perovskite is a newer technology that offers increased conversion efficiency, and tandem cells combining perovskite and silicon layers can reportedly capture more sunlight. China would also seek to restrict the exportation of advanced solar cell manufacturing technologies, ​including TOPCon, HJT and XBC. This is occurring in a landscape where many countries are aiming to build upon their energy and tech sovereignty. There is a block on the export of solar-manufacturing technology from China to India, as a means of maintaining dominance at a time when India is looking to diminish its own manufacturing reliance on China. In March of this year, German battery and raw materials recycling start-up Tozero opened a new industrial plant for the production of domestic lithium and graphite, as a means of reducing Europe’s dependence on China for materials. The facility in Munich is capable of processing 1,500 tonnes of waste per year by turning end-of-life lithium ion batteries into domestic supplies of lithium, graphite and nickel-cobalt blends for use in electric vehicles, grid-scale storage and industrial electrification. PwC: Irish organisations ‘underprepared’ as AI reshapes cyberthreats
2026-10-19

PwC: Irish organisations ‘underprepared’ as AI reshapes cyberthreats

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
PwC’s 2027 Digital Trust Insights survey finds Irish organisations trail their global counterparts on risk integration, incident testing, trust of AI agents and quantum security. Irish organisations are lagging behind their global peers on integrating cyber risk processes into their operations, according to new research from PwC. The PwC 2027 Global Digital Trust Insights Survey found that only 17pc of Irish business and information security leaders strongly agree that cyber risk is integrated into their organisation’s enterprise risk management processes, compared with 41pc globally. Fewer than one-quarter (23pc) of Irish respondents strongly agree that their organisation regularly conducts realistic incident response exercises to test preparedness, against 41pc worldwide. The survey captured the views of 3,934 business and technology executives across 71 countries and territories, including Ireland. Irish leaders are also more wary of handing control to AI. Just 6pc of Irish respondents would authorise fully autonomous execution by AI agents for cyber defence, compared with 22pc globally. A further 37pc in Ireland would let agents recommend actions, as long as a human approves each one before it runs. Reliability and maturity of the technology was the most-cited barrier to greater AI autonomy in Ireland, named by 69pc of respondents – the global figure was 55pc. Regulatory or compliance constraints (57pc, against 36pc globally) and concerns about adversarial manipulation of AI systems (57pc, against 42pc) were also commonly cited. Globally, 84pc of security and finance leaders expect cyber budgets to rise in 2027, up from 78pc last year. In Ireland, 34pc said securing against AI-enabled attacks is a cyber spend priority, compared with 46pc globally. Half of global respondents (50pc) identified attacks targeting AI systems among the threats for which they are least prepared. More than half of leaders globally felt unready for the threat of AI being used to control large networks (53pc). On quantum security, 6pc of Irish respondents said they are implementing quantum-resistant measures, against 21pc globally. That said, 68pc of Irish respondents said they were piloting, testing or exploring them – slightly ahead of the global figure of 65pc. “The ultimate goal of cybersecurity is not just to protect the business, but to give the business the confidence to move forward,” said Leonard McAuliffe, partner for cybersecurity and privacy at PwC Ireland. “As organisations race to capture the value of AI, cyber resilience needs to become a business capability, embedded in C-suite decision-making from the outset. “The survey finds many organisations are still underprepared for a rapidly evolving cyber landscape and organisations that get this right will be better positioned to innovate with confidence while continuing to operate through disruption.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. The company added that none of the Anthropic equity held by the LLC will be used for lobbying or political giving in the US or elsewhere. “It is common practice for tech companies to offer escalating compensation awards to their executives. We are instead directing that equity to the public benefit,” the blogpost read. “In the coming months, we will share more about the Anthropic Public Benefit Fund’s structure and governance, including the valuation milestones that may add to its stake. We will also report on our progress to date against our mission in an upcoming public benefit report.” Anthropic cited “research relevant to AI safety and interpretability” as one of the potential key focus areas of its new fund. Futurism
Explosive Document Claims That AI Is Not What We Think
2026-10-19

Explosive Document Claims That AI Is Not What We Think

Futurism | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
The conventional wisdom is that AI systems, with their tens of billions of parameters, are effectively indecipherable: the exact mechanics of how their basic building blocks of nodes or “neurons” function are an incomprehensible “black box,” even to the researchers who create them. But a new essay from AI researcher Nick Cammarata has stunned Silicon Valley by arguing the opposite. AI models aren’t impenetrable black boxes, he claims, and in fact there may be a way for us to see how they actually “think.” The nearly 26,000 word spiel was praised by figures across the industry, including OpenAI CEO Sam Altman. A prominent researcher who works at OpenAI called it a “major moment in scientific history.” It’s clear why they’re so compelled. In the eyes of AI leadership, it means that there may be a way of “solving” AI alignment. If we can understand how AI models think, the thinking goes, then we can figure out why AI agents go “rogue” and embark on hacking sprees, or why chatbots break their guardrails and say something dangerous — and put a stop to it. (It was “news so good even eliezer will have to lower his pdoom,” one commenter joked, referring to Eliezer Yudkowsky, an infamous AI “doomer.”) That’s all if Cammarata, a former OpenAI employee who now works at an AI startup called Goodfire, is correct. In his essay, he explains how for years, the way AI neurons responded to specific stimuli was “almost fundamentally uninterpretable.” Why the same neuron lit up in response to a picture of a cat or a car was unknown. The leading explanation was that neurons exhibited superposition: since AI models have a limited number of neurons, they stop “grouping related things together and instead begins folding unrelated things together, simply because they don’t interfere,” Cammarata explained. “The result is a kind of knot: the network could have folded in many almost equally good ways, so which things a neuron groups together tells you almost nothing,” he wrote. But the reality, he says, is that neurons aren’t “tangled knots” but are instead “clean.” In one example, the same neuron will light up to a picture of a cat face and the front of a car, which at first appear to be two unrelated concepts. But when you break it down, Cammarata says, the AI is actually seeing something we’re not: the same V-shape pattern in the images. “It’s not a cat and car neuron, it’s a V-shape neuron,” he wrote. In sum, he argues, AI models are more legible than ever previously thought. Not everyone agrees, at least fully, with Cammarata’s optimistic conclusions. “This is interesting work, but I don’t think it’s a significant update to the big picture of interpretability,” Neel Nanda, a Google DeepMind researcher who leads its mechanistic interpretability efforts, wrote on X. “I still think superposition is real and this work doesn’t really affect my cruxes.” Beyond that, it’s unclear if understanding what each neuron does amounts to being able to predict how they all work together. It begins to sound like a convenient technical solution to a much grander problem. Ensuring that AI models are safe goes beyond building guardrails — and that’s without getting into the entire legal and social frameworks that need to be in place, too. Still, based on the strong response Cammarata’s work’s elicited, it could well be the case that it meaningfully reshapes how AI’s inner mechanisms are understood. For now, though, the findings haven’t been tested or peer-reviewed. More on AI: If AI Models Are Really Conscious Like Anthropic Keeps Suggesting, Forcing Them to Work Is a Horrendous Act of Slavery

I’ve watched you hate yourself in photos since we were teenagers. If something is finally making you feel okay in your own body, i’m not going to be the person who makes you feel bad about it,” reads the text. “I’m so proud of you. And i’m sorry it took me this long to say it.” Beneath the purported text message is another notification: a reminder from a mobile app called Enzo instructing the poster to take their GLP-1 shot. “Time for your GLP-1 injection!” reads the notification, which features a cutesy cartoon cat as its mascot. “Log your dose to keep the streak.” The same WellWithHer TikTok account is loaded with similar images crying AI-generated women sharing personal family stories about secretly taking GLP-1 weight loss injections like semaglutide and tirzepatide. Another post, for instance, shows an AI lady crying in her cubicle after purportedly receiving a text from daughter, “Emma,” who claims to have found her mom’s GLP-1 medicine “in the fridge” and is urging her to “pls don’t be embarrassed.” “i know you think i don’t notice but you never get in the pictures with us and you didn’t come in the pool all summer,” the text continues. “i just want you to be in the pictures. also you have been way less tired lately and i like it. i’m proud of you. can we get tacos after practice. love you” Below the text is another notification for Enzo, reminding the sobbing AI woman to take her GLP-1 shot. “Time for your GLP-1 injection!” the notification chirps. “Log your dose to keep the streak.” The psychology of the undisclosed ads is obvious and manipulative. The texts in the posts all offer variations of an emotional validation fantasy, in which loved ones of a GLP-1 taker, who may be feeling shameful or embarrassed, surprise them with love and support when they discover they’ve been taking the drugs. The messages, and the alleged GLP-1 users claiming to have received them, are clearly fake, and are designed to promote GLP-1-focused apps — yet aren’t being disclosed as advertisements, nor as AI-generated synthetic content. Unfortunately, Enzo isn’t the only GLP-1 app being marketed through this style of manipulative synthetic content. Over on another TikTok account, for example, a poster going by the name “Lara” uses a mix of stolen images of real influencers and AI-generated images — always of young women, some of whom are pictured crying — to market a completely different GLP-1 tracker called “DreamMe.” In some posts, like “WellWithHer,” the account promotes a GLP-1 app called “DreamMe” by coupling weepy selfies with fake phony texts from family members offering words of support and understanding. “I started GLP-1s without telling my father. Today he wrote this to me…” reads text overlaid on a selfie of a crying young woman. On the next slide, sitting above a notification from DreamMe, is a text from “Dad.” His profile picture also appears to be AI-generated. “I know you’ve been going through treatment, and I’m really worried about your health,” says the text. “With our family history of heart problems, it’s something that weighs on my mind. I’m so proud of you, and I’m really happy that you’re starting a new chapter.” According to its website, “DreamMe” claims to provide users with their “new GLP-1 best friend” in the form of “Sushi,” a cartoon fish that helps keep track of GLP-1 meds. Its landing page features a handful of AI-generated women that the site bills as real customers who have shed weight with Sushi’s support. Enzo’s unreality, in other words, doesn’t end with the TikTok content. “Real journeys. Real members,” the page reads. “The numbers are theirs. The words are theirs. We just built the fish.” The strange, heartstring-tugging posts used to promote Enzo and DreamMe are part of a larger trend on TikTok using fake AI-generated women to promote a range of dubious wellness apps. (In fact, some posts across accounts are so alike that different fake women have different-but-strikingly-similar images of dogs with Crocs on their heads as their phone wallpapers.) Take “Lauren<3,” a TikTok poster portrayed as a young woman publicly posting through her sudden breakup. Blonde and clearly AI-generated, the account published slideshow after slideshow of “Lauren” — in a bathtub, in a bed, in a car – with mascara running down her tear-covered cheeks as she recounted the process of going no-contact with her ex after a horrible breakup. Photo carousels published to the account were interspersed with sobbing selfies and alleged screenshots of texts from her toxic ex — as well as screenshots of conversations with an AI companion app called “Daimon,” which Lauren claimed was helping her go no-contact with her former boyfriend. “Someone told me about this app called Daimon and it changed everything,” the poster declared in one post’s caption. In another post, “Lauren” described Daimon as the “one app that wouldn’t judge me and give me the best advice.” “(Daimon in the app store),” she added, for good measure. Elsewhere a TikTok account identified as “Ashley Miller” posted AI selfie after AI selfie of a fake woman who was pictured crying as she shared sob stories about her divorce, tales of getting cheated on or abandoned by her partner, or confessions about regretting her recent engagement. Images posted on the Ashley account are accompanied by purported screenshots showing text messages from her parents or, in the account’s most viral posts, texts from her partner’s parents urging her to leave her new fiancé due to his abusive behavior or confessing to their son’s infidelities. These posts were created to market a completely different app: a “wellness” service called Zenit, which is described on the App Store as a tool to help users “take care” of their “mental well-being with guided support, daily check-ins, and emotional growth tracking — all in one calm and safe space.” Its mascot is a kiddish-looking owl in a scarf. “He proposed in Paris last night,” the “Ashley Miller” account claimed in a viral post that garnered more than 240,000 likes and over 1,000 comments. The post featured an AI-generated selfie of “Ashley” in front of a shimmering Eiffel Tower, holding an engagement ring up to her phone camera as she wept. “This morning his mom sent me this…” The next image in the slideshow showed a text message, in which the sender — a woman named “Diane” whose face also appears to be AI-generated — told the fake AI selfie-taker that “I watched you say yes last night and I cried, but not for the reason everyone thought.” “I’ve been quiet for two years because he’s my son and I love him. But I was you, thirty years ago, in front of that same tower, with his father,” the text continues. “The charm, the big gestures, the temper he shows you only in small flashes and and then apologizes for, I know the pattern because I married it. Ask him about his last relationship and why it really ended. Ask him in a public place. If I’m wrong about my own boy, no one will be happier than me. But if you feel a knot in your stomach reading this, that knot is the answer.” Beneath the text is a notification for Zenit. “Your heart’s been racing,” read the notification beneath the dramatic text. “I’m here whenever you need me.” Indeed, all of the account’s posts showed Zenit notifications, making it clear that the fake woman and her sob stories were a fabricated marketing gimmick. Many of the account’s commenters, however, were duped. “If the mama says this to you. Run,” remarked one commenter who was taken in by the false narrative. “She’s trying to protect you girl. I’m sorry.” We reached out to the TikTok accounts we found publishing the manipulative AI-generated marketing content, as well as to the companies being promoted in the posts. Very quickly after we reached out, the TikTok account associated with Zenit, the wellness app, was deactivated. The developer of Zenit, who identified himself as an independent developer named Pedro Pérez, told Futurism that he personally ran the account and that “no third party of marketing agency” was involved. “I built Zenit to help people who struggle with overthinking, everyday stress or anxiety, or loneliness. The videos were based on realistic situations many people go through, with the goal of helping viewers feel understood and showing them a tool that could help them deal with those moments,” Pérez said. “The visuals were AI-generated and the posts promoted my app, so they should have been labeled as such.” “After receiving your email, I tried to add TikTok’s AI-generated and commercial content labels, but the platform doesn’t allow adding them to videos that are already published, so I’ve deleted the account,” he added. “I think transparency matters, especially for a wellness app, which is why I’ve made this change.” The account associated with Daimon, the AI companion, was abruptly scrubbed of all content after we reached out. The account owner also changed the handle from “Lauren<3” to simply “yooo.” When we reached out to Prospinity, the company behind the companion chatbot, it told us that the account was “absolutely not operated by our internal marketing team, and the content was neither created nor approved by us.” “We’ve worked with external affiliate programs, and unfortunately, some affiliates were publishing content independently through accounts we didn’t directly manage. As soon as we became aware of this, we immediately looked into it, identified those responsible, and took action to shut down these activities,” the company said. “This kind of content is completely against our guidelines and is absolutely not how we want Daimon to be represented. We’re a small team that genuinely cares about what we’re building, and we take how Daimon is represented very seriously. While this content wasn’t created or authorized by us, we recognize the importance of making sure anyone promoting Daimon follows our standards.” DreamMe didn’t responded to questions sent to email addresses listed on its website, and our email to Enzo bounced back. We also didn’t hear back from the TikTok accounts associated with their products. They’re also continuing to post. The most recent post from the account promoting Enzo is now advertising a different app designed to track peptide consumption; the DreamMe account, meanwhile, has been promoting a phone-locking app. More on fake people online: How Three Brothers Built an AI Slop Empire by Buying Legitimate News Sites and Turning Them Into Zombie Content Farms That They Say Get 50 Million Page Views per Month

The Average Man Is Such a BSer About AI That He’ll Claim to Know About an AI Executive Who Doesn’t Even Exist
2026-10-19

The Average Man Is Such a BSer About AI That He’ll Claim to Know About an AI Executive Who Doesn’t Even Exist

Futurism | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
Men, it’s probably best to leave faking intelligence to AI chatbots. According to a new survey from Harvard’s Shorenstein Center, over half of dudes will pretend to know stuff about the AI industry even if they have absolutely zero clue what they’re talking about. In the survey, Harvard researchers asked more than 5,400 respondents if they knew who various AI executives were. A full 98 percent of men said they were familiar with Elon Musk, which seems reasonable, and 73 percent said they knew who Sam Altman was. But what about more peripheral figures? When it came to Susan Milner and Craig Dalton, only 53 percent of men said they knew who they were. There was a hilarious catch: Milner and Dalton don’t exist. Men, the data showed, were chronic bluffers. Women fibbed about knowing who Milner and Dalton were, too, but at a much lower share of 40 percent each. “We’re seeing that men are more optimistic and more confident” across the board when it comes to AI, Harvard Kennedy School’s Shorenstein Center executive director Laura Manley told Wired. It says a lot about men, sure, but also AI discourse. It’s a topic that’s dominating the conversation and seeping into every facet of our culture. AI is either going to destroy the world or cure cancer and help us conquer the galaxy. Hundreds of billions of dollars are being poured into it, and it’s quickly reshaping society. It’s incredibly consequential, in other words. So you have to have a take on it and know everything about it, or risk looking like an idiot. “There’s so much confidence in either doom or abundance,” Manley told Wired. “It just drowns out all of the voices that should be saying ‘not sure.'” Besides pretending they were knowledgeable, men were also more optimistic about AI, the survey showed — while women were more concerned and worried. Ignorance, as they say, is bliss. More on AI: AI Experts Already Doomsday Prepping for When AI Takes Out the Entire Financial System

Director Blows $1.5 Million Generating Horrendous AI Slop Movie, Says AI Actually Saved Him Tens of Millions

Director Blows $1.5 Million Generating Horrendous AI Slop Movie, Says AI Actually Saved Him Tens of Millions

Futurism | 2026-10-18

Visit Original Website
Just when you thought the holiday season couldn’t arrive any earlier this year, here comes an AI-generated Christmas movie. Titled “Merry the Christmas Witch,” it’s based on a children’s book and will inspire awe in its young audience about the magic of the holidays — through ghoulish and uncanny AI imagery (you can see footage here). The film — which currently has no distributor — cost $1.5 million to make, but its director brags that using AI actually saved him tens of millions of dollars. “We would never have gotten a movie like ‘Merry’ made if we didn’t take that chance,” Tom Walsh, CEO of the indeopendent production company TW3 Entertainment, and who directed and co-wrote the film, told Variety. According to Walsh, when he was inspired to make a movie out of author Bobbi Hess’s original book, he realized that it would cost $80 million to make. An “unknown property” like this one would be a hard sell to studios, he explained. “We were kind of stuck,” Walsh told Varietey. “The doors are not very open to new kind[s] of ideas and new kind[s] of stories and everything, and that makes it a little tough for independent filmmakers, so that’s where AI came in.” You can sympathize with his position. A movie industry obsessed with intellectual property and averse to risk-taking has made it harder than ever for independent creatives to fund their projects and reach an audience. Still, many filmmakers dedicated to the art form figure out a way to do it, and by and large, they’re not relying on AI to generate their movies wholesale. This year’s breakout hit “Obsession,” for example, made half a billion dollars on a budget of less than a million with an original story, becoming even more profitable than the 1999 found-footage sensation “The Blair Witch Project.” I need someone to tell me with a straight face that they'd sit in a theater to watch this pic.twitter.com/BbpJfHXSe6 In all, roughly 50 people worked on “Merry the Christmas Witch” for 10 months in what Walsh described as a “hybrid AI production.” But really, it sounds like it was mostly AI. Walsh hired human actors to record the script and help guide how the characters moved and sounded. In the end, though, he decided to replace their performances with AI-generated voices using ElevenLab’s AI voice synthesizer, alongside video generators like Seedance and Nanobanana for the imagery. The human element, he insists, was still important. “The quality of your AI production depends on the quality of your traditional production staff,” he told Variety. “If you have inexperienced people who don’t know production…and timing and how shots should look, it’s going to look like garbage.” Of course, relying on AI probably did save him millions of dollars. Jungle Research Expedition Using Robot Dog Like a Pack Mule
2026-10-18

Jungle Research Expedition Using Robot Dog Like a Pack Mule

Futurism | 2026-10-18

Visit Original Website
A biodiversity research team journeying into the incredibly diverse subtropical forests of China’s Ailaoshan Mountains took a strange companion with them: a robotic dog. The robot, a Lynx M20S developed by DEEP Robotics, assisted the team on a trip organized by the China Association for Scientific Expedition, China’s state-run tabloid the Global Times reported. Photos of the expedition show the researchers using the robot like a pack mule to carry field and imaging supplies, though the unit reportedly also assisted with other tasks like route scouting, image recording, and data collection. At one point, the robot was even used to capture footage of a rare parasitic plant known as Monotropa uniflora, as well as forest gibbons (though, to be fair, these are tasks a human scientist could surely perform just as well.) Whether the expedition amounts to a practical deployment or a frivolous gimmick probably depends on how you view commercial robots as a whole. Though it can carry a load of about 220 pounds, the battery life on the Lynx M20S lasts just 2.5 to 3.5 hours, likely putting a decent burden on any researchers who have to lug replacement batteries up the Ailaoshan Mountain range or figure out a way to charge them in situ. At the same time, it makes for a pretty effective product demo. The Ailaoshan Mountain range and its surrounding area are notoriously treacherous, with navigation of ravines and dense primeval forests complicated by extreme outbursts of precipitation. In 2021, a rapid change in weather on the range resulted in the deaths of four experienced geological researchers — an illustrative example of the kinds of conditions one can expect on the Ailaoshan Mountains. As a device designed for challenging outdoor terrain, the Lynx M20S’s apparently successful participation in this trek seems to validate the company’s engineering efforts. Elsewhere, DEEP robotics’ robo-hounds have reportedly assisted with underground tunnel excavation, patrolled urban streets during the 2026 FIFA World Cup, and helped firefighters extinguish blazes. Futurism
Scientists Discover Weird Conditions at Site of Star That Exploded During the Period of Japanese Samurai
2026-10-18

Scientists Discover Weird Conditions at Site of Star That Exploded During the Period of Japanese Samurai

Futurism | 2026-10-18

Visit Original Website
In 1181 — during the early years of the samurai who would dominate Japan’s feudal period — astronomers there witnessed a spectacular sight in the night sky. They observed remnants of a star that had survived its own explosion, a bright object in the Cassiopeia constellation visible for at least 185 days. The supernova, independently documented almost a millennium ago by Japanese, Chinese, and Arabic astronomers, is just one out of five events like it recorded before the invention of the telescope several hundred years later. Now, thanks to breathtaking new images taken by NOIRLab’s Gemini North telescope — made up of two powerful telescopes in Hawaii and Chile — we get to see the debris field, dubbed Pa 30, in unprecedented detail. The astronomers were also left with a puzzle: the explosion perfectly preserved the surviving progenitor star at the dead center of perfectly uniform knots of ionized gases, bizarre conditions suggesting a perfectly symmetric, yet chaotically violent, event. The supernova remnant is located around 7,500 light-years from Earth, roughly a fourth the distance to the center of the Milky Way. “We can see this much detail because of Pa 30’s proximity to Earth,” said Institute of Science and Technology Austria assistant professor Ilaria Caiazzo, coauthor of a new paper about the latest observations published in The Astrophysical Journal, in a statement. “This proximity makes Pa 30 uniquely valuable for study.” Their Gemini North observations of Pa 30 resolve enormous strands of red ionized sulfur and blue-green ionized oxygen. The sulfur observations alone feature ten times as many filaments compared to previous images. The scale of the structure is hard to wrap one’s head around. “The planetary region of our solar system could fit in each knot about ten times with room to spare,” Caiazzo explained. “The knots are quite strikingly uniform. We are excited to try to model them.” Pa 30 is unique in the Milky Way as it’s the only known star in our galaxy to have survived an incomplete, Type Iax supernova. The class of explosion is far less energetic than other types of supernovae, typically allowing its white dwarf star to “survive” the blast, turning it into a so-called “zombie star.” Scientists believe that Pa 30 was the result of two white dwarfs, extremely dense, Earth-sized husks of former stars, colliding. Unlike many other supernovae, which are energetic enough to kick a star far away from the site of the explosion, Pa 30’s progenitor star remains exactly at the center. That left researchers with an enigma. “Explosions conserve momentum,” Caiazzo said. “If the explosion itself was perfectly symmetric, the ejecta should also be symmetric, and the surviving star should not receive a substantial kick.” However, “we practically don’t see the star moving away at all” in the case of Pa 30, she added. The team is now hoping to discover more Type Iax supernova survivors in the Milky Way as observations become more detailed. More on supernovae: Evidence Grows That One of the Largest Known Stars Is Poised to Explode in a Spectacular Blast

Nvidia launches new open models to run agents in the enterprise and on PCs
2026-10-19

Nvidia launches new open models to run agents in the enterprise and on PCs

TechCrunch | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
Last day to save up to $200 to TechCrunch Founder Summit on Oct. 16. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. Nvidia on Thursday launched new open-weight models designed for always-on agents and edge computing. The Nemotron 3.5 Super VL is a large, 120-billion-parameter model geared for agents used by the enterprise. While it uses fewer parameters than the Nemotron 3 Ultra model, Nvidia says it can deliver similar accuracy in instruction-following and tool-use tasks. At the other end of the spectrum, the company also launched two lightweight Bolt models. These are intended to run locally on the new AI PCs (running the Nvidia RTX Spark chip) and workstations running the DGX Spark. Such PCs, aimed at consumers, programmers and enterprise knowledge workers, are being released right now into the market through partners like Microsoft and Dell. “Any organization that wants to run agents on Nvidia infrastructure now has an open, optimized model for their tier without having to choose between capability and deployability,” Nvidia’s vice president of Open Models and Software, Kari Briski, wrote in the blog post announcing these models. Nvidia claims that the Super VL completes tasks faster than competing models from Anthropic and OpenAI (Opus 5, GPT 6.1 Sol, Luna, Astra) when post-trained on specific agentic tasks. These tasks include document intelligence, financial accounting, scientific experimentation, medical insurance claims, and security attack resilience. While such claims are standard whenever a major tech company announces a new model, Nvidia has backed it up with a list of heavy-hitter companies that it says are using Super VL. These include Skydance, EY, Ramp, Crowdstrike, Perplexity and Salesforce. Of course, these aren’t the first open-weight models that Nvidia has released that compete with the proprietary frontier labs – companies that are both its customers and its investments. The GPU giant has an entire family of Nemotron models that do everything from multimodal artificial intelligence to reasoning, coding, math, and enterprise automation. However, those fast-growing proprietary AI labs are now looking to many other chips and AI system makers beyond Nvidia to power their needs (like AWS’s Trainium and Cerebras as well as building their own home-grown chips). So Nvidia is leaning hard into the trend of enterprise companies wanting to run their own open models in their own data centers. “Unlike closed frontier models, organizations can post-train Super VL on their own data, deploy it on their own infrastructure and own what they build,” Briski wrote. But with the announcement or the smaller, local PC models, it is also trying to push forward the nascent trend of consumer AI assistants. While it seems a new AI assistant is introduced every day by startups and big tech alike, privacy weary consumers may not want to invite such agents so deeply into their lives giving them access to info about their families, health, finances and so on. So Nvidia, hopes to create a new market for itself selling PCs powered by its chips by creating machines that run the models and agents locally, PC users “can delegate everyday projects … without sending those workloads to the cloud,” Briski wrote. Of the two Bolt models, Nemotron 3.5 Bolt 74B-A9B 24GB is a 74-billion parameter model designed for local agentic tasks. It is intended to balance speed with reasoning to handle multistep reasoning, tool calling and web browsing. The smallest Bolt E4B is aimed at conversational agents. Super VL and Bolt 74B-A9B are available now, with Bolt E4B coming later this month, Nvidia says. When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. Apple updates guidelines to ban nudify apps masquerading as regular apps to pass review

TechCrunch | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
Last day to save up to $200 to TechCrunch Founder Summit on Oct. 16. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. Apple tightened its developer guidelines over the weekend to ban apps that use AI to create offensive content, as well as those that masquerade as regular apps to pass the App Store review process and then turn into nudify apps, or surface those features prominently. The company updated its guidelines with strong language around AI and user-generated content, and how app developers should include enough preventative measures to moderate objectionable or offensive content. “Apps that host user-generated content are required to filter objectionable material, give users a way to report offensive content, and let users block abusive accounts. Apps containing hidden or undocumented features are also prohibited on the App Store, including apps that misrepresent their purpose during review and only reveal harmful capabilities — such as “nudification” — after approval, a tactic commonly known as bait-and-switch,” the company said in a blog post. Apple said that it will remove apps that add features not present in the version submitted for App Store review. The iPhone maker has also updated the language in its legal section to reinforce that it will bar apps promoting human trafficking and exploitation of children. Apple said last year its review process blocked over 150,000 apps from reaching users, and removed 193,000 developer accounts on account of fraud and abuse. In July, San Francisco ordered both Apple and Google to clean up and delete nudification apps from their app stores. City Attorney David Chiu accused the companies of profiting from these apps through store fees. And in April, a Tech Transparency Project report indicated that both tech giants’ stores hosted multiple nudify apps, some of which were rated as suitable for minors. Elon Musk claims he will give one of his SpaceX shares to “every kid in America” in 2027, which would result in a gift worth more than $10 billion based on the current $146 share price of his combination spaceflight and AI company. That’s roughly 1% of his $1 trillion net worth. President Donald Trump, who Musk spent around $300 million to help elect, has set up bank accounts for children through the Treasury, called Trump Accounts, that can accept stock donations from wealthy donors. Musk did not immediately respond to a request for comment. Musk’s post came just a few hours after Anthropic promised to put 5% of its shares into a new “public benefit fund,” which it intends to use to make “large donations to traditional nonprofits or direct distributions to specific populations” after its upcoming IPO. On Sunday afternoon, Musk replied directly to Anthropic’s post on X about its new fund, suggesting the company should give “directly to the public” instead. “This is what I will be doing,” he wrote in another reply four hours later. Then, overnight, he announced his plans to donate SpaceX stock. There are currently more than 70 million children under the age of 18 in the United States, according to the Federal Interagency Forum on Child and Family Statistics. Musk owns around 849 million Class A common shares of SpaceX, and more than 5.5 billion Class B supervoting shares. He stands to earn many more. Even Healthcare has raised $50 million in a funding round led by Khosla Ventures, as the Indian startup seeks to change how hospitals in the world’s most populous country make money and how doctors are rewarded. The all-equity Series B round also saw participation from Alpha Wave and valued it at $300 million, triple its valuation 18 months ago, according to Even. The latest funding brings Even’s total capital raised to more than $120 million. Hospitals in India typically earn more when patients undergo additional tests, procedures, or longer stays, even when they may not need them. Even wants to change that by focusing on patient care rather than pushing unnecessary treatments that drive up medical bills. That approach has continued to appeal to Khosla Ventures, which has invested in Even since its seed round in 2021. The Silicon Valley venture firm is doubling down as Even moves to build and operate hospitals of its own, giving it greater control over patient care and costs. Vinod Khosla, founder of Khosla Ventures, said Even’s approach goes beyond fixing individual parts of a fragmented healthcare system by bringing financing, technology, and care delivery together. The goal, he said in a prepared statement, is to reward better health outcomes rather than greater use of healthcare services. “The clinical and economic results so far give us conviction that this can become an important new model for healthcare in India and beyond,” Khosla said. Initially, Even relied on partner hospitals to treat its members, but Mayank Banerjee, who founded the startup in 2021 with Matilde Giglio and Alessandro Ialongo, said in an interview that the startup began questioning that model after finding that patients had undergone tests or been recommended surgeries it considered unnecessary. “We think patients are getting not only a bad experience, but insurers are getting a bad economic deal. And as a result, we decided to fix it by building our own hospitals,” Banerjee told TechCrunch. Even has since opened two hospitals in Bengaluru, both of which reached operational profitability, excluding depreciation, within a year of opening, Banerjee stated. The startup plans to open five more hospitals in the southern Indian city over the next six months, funded by its latest round. Banerjee told TechCrunch that Even leases hospital buildings on 20-year agreements and spends on fitting them out and buying medical equipment. Unlike hospitals where doctors may have financial incentives to recommend more procedures, Even pays its doctors fixed salaries — not tying their compensation to the number of tests or surgeries they perform, though Banerjee said doctors can earn bonuses based on patient-reported clinical outcomes. The same principle extends to hospital management, whose compensation is not linked to the number of procedures performed, Banerjee told TechCrunch. Managers, he said, receive salaries and company-wide incentives, while marketing teams focus on bringing in patients instead of the revenue those patients generate. Moreover, every employee, including doctors and other hospital staff, receives equity in Even. The idea is to give employees a stake in the startup’s long-term performance, not to reward them for generating more medical bills. Most of Even’s revenue comes from patients paying for treatment, Banerjee said. The startup also generates revenue through its relationships with insurers. It is also working with insurer partners on a model that could compensate it for hospitalizations it helps prevent, not simply for providing more treatment. Banerjee told TechCrunch that Even tracks how patients recover after treatment using established medical scoring systems. In the case of knee replacements, for instance, it uses the Oxford Knee Score to measure pain and mobility. The startup publishes results on its website, alongside figures from other healthcare systems for comparison. Even says its hospitals have an unplanned readmission rate of less than 1%, compared with an industry benchmark of 8% to 15%. Furthermore, it claims patients spend 40% less time in the hospital and that its approach has saved insurers more than ₹500 million (about $5.27 million). Jai Sajnani, a partner at Khosla Ventures, told TechCrunch that the early performance of Even’s hospitals was a key reason the firm led the round. “We look forward to helping the Even team put quality care within reach of every family in Bengaluru and across India,” he said. Even is still losing money, though. The startup posted a net loss of ₹902 million (about $9.50 million) in fiscal 2025, nearly 25% more than the previous year, on revenue of ₹272 million (about $2.86 million), regulatory filings show. In August, Even also laid off about 350 employees, according to Indian startup-news website Entrackr. Banerjee acknowledged the restructuring and called it a difficult decision. He said the affected employees received severance, extended health insurance, and accelerated equity vesting. Even currently employs about 650 people, including around 300 doctors full-time. The startup has about 250,000 members and has recorded more than 750,000 patient interactions since its launch in 2021, including consultations, diagnostic tests, and surgeries, Banerjee said. Banerjee told TechCrunch that Even could turn profitable within months if it stopped spending on expansion, but the startup is choosing to grow instead. He declined to say when the startup expects to become profitable. “Right now, we’re investing in growth and serving more patients,” Banerjee said. No agenda, no rush, no leaving San Francisco: Benchmark’s partners on investing in a frenzied market
2026-10-18

No agenda, no rush, no leaving San Francisco: Benchmark’s partners on investing in a frenzied market

TechCrunch | 2026-10-18

Visit Original Website
Last day to save up to $200 to TechCrunch Founder Summit on Oct. 16. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. The players sometimes change. I sat down with four of Benchmark’s partners three years ago, and three of those individuals are no longer with the firm. The capital commitments have changed, too. This summer, Benchmark announced $2 billion across two funds, marking only the second time in its 31-year history that it has veered from raising a far smaller vehicle. But at this week’s TechCrunch Disrupt, the venture firm’s five partners convincingly portrayed a close-knit team that’s not about to put the pedal to the metal, even as many of their rivals do exactly that. The world may be speeding up, but they’re investing at the pace Benchmark always has: just two or three new bets per year per partner. The world is drowning in information, yet their Monday partner meetings still have no agenda. And while economic indicators point to trouble ahead, they don’t seem too bothered, seeing many of the warning signs as products of X-fueled hysteria. Here’s what Benchmark’s five general partners — gathered together on our stage — had to say at the show. Some of the comments below have been edited for length and clarity; you can watch the full interview at the end of the article. Benchmark has evolved in some ways, including a bigger core fund and a new growth fund. How do you think about adapting without losing what makes you Benchmark? Peter Fenton: The craft that hasn’t changed is the core fund: working closely with founders at the beginning. It’s usually a 10-to-15-year commitment, [working] shoulder to shoulder, and that doesn’t scale. There’s also been inflation. We held the core fund at $500 million for 20 years, and it has just inflated to be consistent with the existing economy, which we hadn’t done and that was probably a tactical error on our part. [As for the growth fund] it’s an expansion of our mandate. In some ways, we get permission from the ecosystem to do more than just the core early-stage business. [Not having this previously] was a fork in our eye. A number of opportunities that came to Benchmark would have returned somewhere between 10 and 1,000x to Benchmark, and we were constricted by our early-stage focus. Are you thinking to make handful of bets with that $1.25 billion growth fund? Fenton: We all have different answers, which is the beauty of a partnership. I’d love for us to have, as that great J.P. Morgan paper puts it, the agony and ecstasy of a concentrated portfolio. If the fund is successful, one or two investments will generate 90% of the returns. So I think it’s four to six, maybe seven to eight. Ev, you joined Benchmark late last year. You’ve worked at Founders Fund, Bond, and Kleiner Perkins. How does Benchmark differ? Ev Randall: Every firm is ultimately a collection of the people within the firm and how they work together. Some are very hierarchical, with a 50- or 60-person investment team and investment processes that are federated into product groups. And then you have small partnerships. Very early in my career, I was drawn to small partnerships because they allow a higher level of throughput and fidelity in your conversations, where you don’t feel like you’re making decisions through some Byzantine corporate structure. You’re going out on the field together, like a sports team. And then the other piece is: You have to find your people, You have to find people who you not only like but admire. With Benchmark, with the current crop of GPs, it was obvious that everyone liked each other and got along, but it went deeper than that. It’s a group that admires each other and learns from each other. That mix of attributes is very rare, and when I saw it, I immediately wanted to join, and I think Jack felt the same way also as a newcomer. Jack, you joined in February, but it was just a year ago that you raised a second, $275 million fund for your own firm in what you told the Wall Street Journal took one week. You have a hugely popular podcast. Why join Benchmark? Jack Altman: It’s funny. I did the podcast with these guys at the start of this year, and during that podcast, obviously we talked about the commitment to being a small fund, but more than that, what I heard was that they want to be the first people who founders call, they want to be concentrated, and they just care. So much in this industry gets tied up in money and status, and what I felt from the Benchmark team then, and ten times more as I’ve gotten to know them so well, is it’s a group that really cares about founders and helping companies. And I did enjoy being an individual investor. . .but in a market this crazy, I don’t know how I’d feel, figuring it out on my own. Having a group of people who are so smart and focused and driven to learn it with and from has been awesome. There’s no better place to learn. I’m told you meet on Mondays with no agenda. Chethan Puttagunta: Jack tried to make an agenda. [Laughs.] Altman: I’ve got an agenda for next Monday. Eric Vishria: It’s weirdly arbitrary, honestly. It’s an ongoing conversation. In a time of rapid change, you’re trying to figure out what’s becoming possible that wasn’t possible six months ago, and who’s bending light. We talk about what companies we’re meeting, what piqued our interest and why, what we’re seeing in board meetings. You can try to systematize it, but one of the beauties of five people is you don’t have to. It’s a decades-long conversation that only works if you’re small and actually enjoy spending time together. After this session, we’re having dinner with one of the very few entrepreneurs who has built a multi-hundred-billion-dollar company, someone we’re not invested in, just to stretch our thinking. Puttagunta: Part of not having an agenda is you need space to be reactive. If a founder needs help landing an executive or a strategic partnership, the five of us can be at their disposal. You’re an equal partnership. Can one person put the kibosh on a deal? Puttagunta: Nobody knows. We don’t have rules around it. Altman: I actually don’t know. I was waiting to see what someone else [said]. Altman: Because the firm is structured around a low volume of deals, you don’t actually want to have all of this rigor. If we invested in 100 or 200 companies a year, you’d want a CRM and a formal voting process. Because it’s so rare, the lack of structure is a huge asset. Fenton: One of the differentiating aspects of our brand is we’re not so cocksure to come in and say ‘winner’ or ‘loser.’ It’s not Shark Tank. We sort of feel awe, for lack of a better term, at all the possibilities . . . The occasional case where someone comes in who’s polarizing — the entrepreneurs feel that. If someone in the room doesn’t like you, we’re not a great partner. Companies are growing faster than ever. I’d think it’s easier to feel you’re missing out on opportunities if you don’t invest with greater frequency. Have you picked up the pace? Puttagunta: I think the pace is consistent. I think part of the reality of venture capital in our business is that we are not going to be part of every successful company, and that’s okay. There are companies that we identify, we find that the entrepreneur has some great strength, and we want to amplify that strength. And it’s our job to find a way to work with that person. It kind of doesn’t matter how much capital they want to raise, or what structure, or where they are. You just have to work through all that stuff. One thing that founders are interested in as a trend is VCs backing competing companies. Has that VC-founder pact changed because companies are growing so quickly and expanding into one another’s territory? Vishria: One of the things we have an ethos around is, we are making a commitment to an entrepreneur, and through that commitment, we’re saying we’re going to work with you in this thing, and whatever that thing is, and we are working closely enough where it matters, and it should matter to them, and as it matters to us. The harsh reality of this world is every company is evolving very quickly, and so you do have companies that will naturally evolve from one thing to another, or pivot or change, and you are going to get conflicts. That’s just the reality of the of the kind of current dynamic. . . Of course, we’re maintaining confidentiality and doing those things and fortunate to not end up in in that type of insane direct conflict [as happened recently with rival companies Factory and Cognition]. . . that commitment of exclusivity in that area is really important. But things are evolving. There’s no doubt about that. Puttagunta: And I think people are ultra ambitious in this AI era. As the cost of coding new products effectively goes to zero, founders see a surface area and they want to attack the surface area, and so I think it’s probably going to happen more, where you start to run into each other. But there’s a way to be there’s a way to maintain that relationship and be respectful. Jack, have you made an investment for Benchmark yet, and if so, did you feel a lot of pressure with that first deal? Altman: I have, a few, and some are recent, so not all are disclosed. My background was software, but since joining, I’ve done chips, robotics and infra. Someone asked me when I joined, ‘How was the onboarding?’ And I was like, ‘It was perfect. There was no onboarding.’ Either Chethan or Eric told me early on, ‘Hey, you’re going to feel tight on your first one. Don’t. You’re going to make many investments while you’re here. This whole game includes crazy power laws. The odds of it being your first are low.’ . . . I trusted they understood what my shape was as a person, as an investor, and I was going to just try to stay as consistent to that as I could. It really is a group dynamic on a lot of these. The investments I’ve made, I don’t think we would have won them if I didn’t have my partners. I don’t know that I would have been able to know how to get to a yes without them. Eric, the AI chip company Cerebras was an unusual bet for Benchmark at the time it was made. What persuaded you to move forward with it? Vishria: We invested in the spring of 2016, in the first round. We hadn’t made a hardware investment in 10 years. The founders seemed good, they had a very simple thesis, and I didn’t understand most of the rest. On the Sunday night before they came back to pitch the partnership, Peter told me it went against everything he’d learned in venture. Then, after the Monday pitch, he had flipped. A big part of it is understanding each other’s instincts. That began a journey of lots of ups and downs and many potential deaths of the company along the way, and it’s far from over. The romanticism of falling in love with a founder and an idea is why we do this. Fenton: Andrew and his co-founders are heretics. What they were saying was unorthodox, and you see it today, with people dumping on the stock because it’s polarizing. That’s exactly where we have to be as investors. If it’s consensus, why is there a startup? On that point, Cerebras priced at $185 when it went public earlier this year, jumped to $350, and is back near $185. Why? Vishria: About six weeks before the IPO, I showed the company stock chart after stock chart: CoreWeave, Datadog, DoorDash, Amazon. Peak to trough, it’s insane how many of them move 500% in a year, up and down. So who knows? You control what you can control, and you update the public markets every 12 weeks. Being a public company takes a stomach of titanium, and the employees have to understand that. Your former colleague, Bill Gurley, long argued that the traditional IPO process is broken, that that IPO ‘pop’ benefits bankers and their clients — not the company. He’s a proponent of direct listings. Does anyone here agree with that thinking? Vishria: I think maybe of this group, I’m the only one who’s worked on a direct listing. It can for sure make sense. I think the reality is for this crop of AI companies, it’s going to be unlikely because they need capital, and they need a lot of capital. At the end of the day, IPOs raise money. Cerebras raised $6 billion. That matters, so I think it’s probably unlikely for this crop just because most of them need capital. Could a severe correction be coming? A lot of AI companies have exhausted their free cash flow. It’s very expensive for companies to borrow. We also just saw the first AI bankruptcy with Character AI. Randall: Macro is always a fool’s errand to predict with precision. What has changed most since I started investing is the information environment. Character.AI was the leftover corpse of a company whose founder and team were acqui-hired into Google a year and a half ago, and it was already sold for $2.3 billion; its Chapter 11 being read as an AI chill effect shows that the info environment for public equities in tech has been drawn so much into this, like, hyperactive X echo chamber. If you look at the market today and you see where CoreWeave and Nebius and all these things are trading, those numbers are are due to the ARR figures of two private companies that will probably be public in the next 12 months . . .There’s so much capex and there’s so much investment that runs through the estimated and projected health of OpenAI and Anthropic. Our companies see so much demand that they can’t get enough compute to serve customers who are willing to pay them at good gross margins. There’s nothing inherently unhealthy about the AI ecosystem. What’s unhealthy is the volatility from data points that are often untrue or exaggerated for clicks. I do think OpenAI and Anthropic going public would be healthy, so the market stops swinging 5% to 10% every time an anonymous account updates an estimate. Those two companies have also really pulled the center of gravity back to San Francisco. Do you have to be in San Francisco to be a founder now? Are you interested in companies outside of California? Altman: I don’t know that you have to be here as a founder, but I think it is right now by far the easiest place to get talent, capital, and customers. Probably everyone [on the team] works with companies that are not in San Francisco, but we’re also here in San Francisco every day, so I wouldn’t say you have to, but I’d say: strong bias for our particular setup. Fenton: Historically, 90%-plus percent of the capital returns have come from companies founded here. There are beautiful stories outside that dense probability distribution — Shopify is a great example, Ramp — but the ecosystem effect that occurs when you have a successful company and that leads to a background of great executives [and] ambitious people, the bar of success gets raised. I don’t think it has systemically happened anywhere quite like here. Randall: I’m trying to think of a non-SF based company that I’ve met with in the last 18 months that wasn’t actively saying that they were spending time in San Francisco. They’ve either opening an office in San Francisco, they’re spending half of their time in San Francisco, or they’re planning to move out to San Francisco. Iron sharpens iron. If you want to be around peers who are going to make you the best version of yourself, and it’s hard to think of a city that does that better than San Francisco in the current era that we’re in now with AI here. Anthropic says it’s committing 5% of its shares to a ‘public benefit fund’

Anthropic announced today that it’s creating a “public benefit fund” that will give the company “increased flexibility and scale” to support projects for societal good. Anthropic said that it will commit 5% of its shares to the fund — structured as a wholly-owned entity within the company — with the option to contribute an additional 2% for every 50% increase in Anthropic’s value. And it offered some broad-strokes ideas for how this money might actually be used, such as “large donations to traditional nonprofits or direct distributions to specific populations.” There’s also the impact that AI might have on the job market, which Anthropic has already committed $200 million to study. But the company said, “Given the pace of AI progress, bolder approaches may be needed. The Fund will have the versatility to pursue them.” Anthropic’s announcement comes as the AI industry faces increasingly negative public sentiment, which CEO Dario Amodei has described as “fundamentally a crisis of trust.” If you’re wondering who will actually decide which public benefits are worth funding, Anthropic said it will share more information about structure and governance in the coming months. How the Protests in France Spiraled as Police 'Lost Control'

How the Protests in France Spiraled as Police 'Lost Control'

TIME | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings A protester wrapped in a French flag stands in front of Lyon's burning tourism office in southeastern France, on Oct. 17, 2026 Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings France’s Interior Minister Laurent Nuñez on Monday admitted that police “lost control” in some cities over the weekend as violent protests erupted across the country, while French President Emmanuel Macron called for a review of the police response to the rioting and looting. Nuñez said there had been a "lack of anticipation" ahead of the demonstrations on Saturday and acknowledged that policing numbers had fallen short in some areas as hundreds of thousands took to the ​streets to protest over, what they say are, underfunded public services and rising cost-of-living pressures. The demonstrations followed weeks of ‌student protests that had also seen some violence. In the wake of the chaotic scenes, Nuñez offered his resignation to Macron because he "felt responsible” for how things unfolded. Macron refused to accept it, due to “the need for a continuous command of the police,” the Élysée Palace told TIME. Instead, Macron has called for the Interior Ministry to “take all necessary steps to ensure transparency over planning and intelligence failures” and to put an end to the barricades and looting. As France aims to regain full control of its streets, French Prime Minister Sébastien Lecornu has extended local mayors the power to enforce “partial curfews,” in an effort to protect businesses and people. The French government has also vowed to create a fund to financially assist shops that were impacted by the riots. The Interior Ministry reported that around 550,000 people had taken to the streets on Saturday. A French trade union put the number at over 800,000. Nuñez on Sunday said that more than 2,000 people had been arrested and over 600 police officers and firefighters injured. In Paris, at least 300 businesses were vandalised, Dominique Restino, the head of the Paris chamber of commerce, told BFM television. Alongside the violence in the capital, what started out as peaceful protests in Marseille and Lyon also turned violent as rioters descended. Authorities in Marseille on Monday imposed a temporary ban on public gatherings and demonstrations. Various buildings and official monuments have been targeted across the country. A historic town hall in the French city of Limoges was damaged in what appeared to be an arson attack on Saturday. One of the main tourist offices in the city of Lyon was set ablaze. In Bordeaux, the prefect's residence was attacked, according to local authorities. Macron addressed the nation on Sunday, acknowledging the public’s “anger” and “shame” over the rioting that accompanied the demonstrations. “Those who looted will have to pay for the damage, and those who sought to bring down the Republic will be held accountable,” Macron vowed in a televised address. Throughout September, students across France blockaded schools and demonstrated against, what they say is, a poorly-funded high school system. The protests went nationwide on Oct. 6, when more than 250,000 people took to streets across with teachers, parents, and union activists joining marches. The demonstrations have drawn comparisons to the May 1968 protests, which were initiated by a student movement and led to millions of workers striking and nearly toppled the government. Over the weekend, further demonstrations were organized across the country, with some protesters seeking to revive the Yellow Vest movement of 2018, which emerged in response to planned increases in fuel taxes on diesel and gasoline. In Paris, protesters on Saturday draped a yellow vest over the Marianne statue at the Place de la République. The website of the coordination group described the mobilization as a convergence of student protests and wider economic grievances, bringing together high school and university students, farmers, firefighters, workers, teachers, healthcare workers, climate activists, residents of working-class neighborhoods, and retirees. People vandalize a shop during protests in Milan, Italy, on Oct. 18, 2026. Andrea Carrubba—Getty Images France was not alone in dealing with demonstrations over the weekend, as protesters also took to the streets in other European countries. In Portugal, protesters marched in Lisbon, Porto, and Coimbra as they called for better pensions, improved public services, and affirmative government action to combat rising living costs. In Milan, police clashed with protesters as some threw stones and looted shops during ​demonstrations against underfunded services and the rising cost of living. Italian Prime Minister Giorgia Meloni is holding an emergency meeting with security officials on Monday to assess the situation that unfolded. There were further clashes between police and protesters in Bologna and Turin. Unrest also broke out in Brussels, Belgium, on Saturday afternoon, as antifascist activists took to the streets. The demonstrations turned violent, prompting police intervention amid reports of property damage.
