SAP's Signavio hit with function injunction in Germany
2026-10-19

SAP's Signavio hit with function injunction in Germany

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
Vendor says patent ruling only restricts one specific process, not the whole product, after legal challenge from Celonis A German court has issued an injunction banning SAP from providing certain features of its Signavio product, following a legal challenge from process mining biz Celonis. The ruling only applies to implementations in Germany, but is part of a broader legal tussle between the two companies over intellectual property. This case focuses on software which helps companies understand their own complex business processes in order to improve them and help prepare for an application migration. The Munich Regional Court ruled against SAP for infringing a Celonis patent covering the technology which determines the lead time between process stages of a process instance. The court also asked SAP to provide more information and offer financial statements about its alleged infringement. In a statement, an SAP spokesperson said: “The court’s ruling relates to one specific function within SAP Signavio Process Intelligence, not to the product as a whole. SAP Signavio Process Intelligence remains available to customers. S/4HANA Cloud migrations are not affected. SAP is working to ensure that there is no disruption to our customers.” SAP added: "SAP respectfully disagrees with the Munich court's ruling and has appealed the decision. SAP has separately challenged the validity of the asserted patent before the Federal Patent Court. In fact, the Munich court's decision represents only one of several ongoing disputes between SAP and Celonis, including patent actions brought by SAP in Germany and the United States. SAP’s first priority is ensuring that there is no disruption to our customers, and we will address all related matters through the appropriate legal channels." Celonis specializes in process mining, building software designed to gather data from people's interactions with business applications. It aims to uncover how organizations’ processes are actually performed, as opposed to how they were designed. For example, during a process mining project, pharma giant GSK discovered around 28,000 different variations for the process of running a sales order. Celonis was founded in 2011 and early customers included Siemens, with projects examining how processes run on the global engineering company’s SAP software. In 2021, SAP decided it wanted in on the same market and bought mining company Signavio, in part to help customers move from legacy software systems to the cloud. The ERP giant does not mandate Signavio for its migrations, but the software is bundled in packages under the moniker RISE with SAP, the lift-shift-and-transform commercial push SAP launched in 2021 to get users into the cloud and on its latest ERP offering, S/4HANA. The latest ruling is part of a set of legal disputes between Celonis and SAP. In March last year, Celonis filed a lawsuit in the US Court, Northern District of California, claiming the ERP giant had made it more difficult and expensive for third-party software companies to get hold of customer data stored in SAP's business software. Celonis was later allowed to amend and refile its complaint after the US court dismissed most of the claims. A trial is set for September 2027. SAP has also launched a case alleging a patent infringement action against Celonis at a Düsseldorf, Germany, court. It also filed suit in October last year at the District Court of Delaware over four US patents. Celonis has launched other cases alleging infringement by SAP, including in a Texas federal district court. ® Black cab drivers accused ride-hailing giant of misleading London's transport regulator, but judge says they left it too late to sue Vendor says patent ruling only restricts one specific process, not the whole product, after legal challenge from Celonis PARTNER CONTENT: A million reasons why advanced computing matters for genomics OWA borked and lengthy email queues reported on busy servers after Microsoft tweaks a Server default The first technology created with a self-disrupt button... Admin: 'I spent all weekend on my servers' after Microsoft breaks Exchange Server with October updates
2026-10-19

Admin: 'I spent all weekend on my servers' after Microsoft breaks Exchange Server with October updates

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
OWA borked and lengthy email queues reported on busy servers after Microsoft tweaks a Server default Microsoft has acknowledged that the October 2026 Windows Server updates may be destabilizing Exchange Server. The problems are related to this list of Windows Server updates (see here) and Microsoft makes it clear they are not related to the October 2026 security updates for Exchange Server. The software giant says the bug can hit Exchange Servers running Windows Server 2016, 2019, 2022, and 2025. The glitch might not be immediate and could impact Exchange Server OWA, which can throw up connection glitches or cause lengthy email queues, "with email crawling to a halt." Rebooting the server might make the problem go away temporarily, but a more permanent workaround is a registry edit. The destabilization issue could impact Exchange Server 2016, 2019, and the new Subscription Edition (SE), says the Windows maker. Microsoft strongly advised that customers should not uninstall the October 2026 Windows Server update and stated, "Not all Exchange servers will be impacted by installation of those updates equally; the severity and speed of impact varies based on Exchange server load. Some servers might never see a problem." However, for those administrators who found their weekend swallowed by borked Exchange Servers, the registry edit is currently the only workaround. The cause of the issue is a default limit on the amount of memory used by Endpoint Mapper when handling Endpoint Lookup calls. In Microsoft's Release Health Dashboard for Windows Server 2025 (Server 2022, 2019, and 2016 are also affected), the Microsoft said: "For workloads such as Exchange Server, the default limit of 512 simultaneous Endpoint Lookup calls might not be sufficient under heavy load." Hence, the instruction to use a registry setting to increase the maximum. It added, "We are investigating this issue and will provide more information when it is available." Customers were understandably frustrated. On Microsoft's support page, one wrote, "We have had the problem like every 20h since Wednesday, when we installed the windows updates for October and the exchange su. Reboot only solution," before noting that the registry setting appeared to have improved things. Another fumed, "Also please release your updates tested and without problems. Without giving us the job to test for you. We're already paying for the software and you want to make tests with us?! I spent all weekend on my servers because of this." The issue is bad form on the part of Microsoft and appears to be the result of a poorly thought-through default applied in the operating system which impacted Exchange Server. Microsoft has yet to commit to issuing an out-of-band update to resolve the issue, after all, not every customer is affected, and the problem depends heavily on how busy a server is. However, at least there's a registry workaround that might do the trick while Microsoft ponders its next move. ® Black cab drivers accused ride-hailing giant of misleading London's transport regulator, but judge says they left it too late to sue Vendor says patent ruling only restricts one specific process, not the whole product, after legal challenge from Celonis PARTNER CONTENT: A million reasons why advanced computing matters for genomics OWA borked and lengthy email queues reported on busy servers after Microsoft tweaks a Server default The first technology created with a self-disrupt button... Microsoft abandons UK AI supercomputer as grid struggles to keep up
2026-10-19

Microsoft abandons UK AI supercomputer as grid struggles to keep up

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
Tech giant walks away from flagship Essex project as power connection delays threaten to leave nation's AI ambitions waiting until the 2030s Microsoft has reportedly pulled out of plans for Britain's largest AI supercomputer, because the government-backed project is facing the rather inconvenient problem of having no electricity to run it. The tech giant has terminated its agreement with British AI infrastructure startup Nscale for a planned datacenter in Loughton, Essex, according to The Telegraph, which first reported the withdrawal on Sunday. The decision follows reports that the site, which was supposed to begin operating in 2027, might not receive enough electricity from the grid until the early to mid-2030s. Microsoft was expected to be the anchor customer at Loughton, which Nscale had pitched as the UK's largest sovereign AI datacenter. Its departure leaves Nscale without a publicly announced customer for the site, although the neocloud insists the project is still going ahead. When the deal was announced in September 2025, the plan was to pack 23,040 Nvidia GB300 GPUs into a 50MW facility, with room to expand to 90MW. Microsoft would use the computing power for its Azure cloud services, with the first chips expected in early 2027. One important detail remained: how to power it. As The Guardian reported last month, UK Power Networks warned Nscale that the grid capacity needed might not be available until the 2030s. A UK Power Networks spokesperson told The Register it could not discuss individual connections due to obligations under Section 105 of the Utilities Act. "However, in general, many datacenters, whilst they can connect to our distribution network, are awaiting works on the national transmission network which we do not own," the spokesperson said. Nscale, meanwhile, isn't letting the small matter of losing its biggest customer dampen its enthusiasm. “We remain committed to both the Loughton project and the UK and we are exploring alternative power sources for the site, in addition to other expansion sites in the UK. Our relationship with Microsoft remains strong and long-term, and this unfortunate London-wide grid issue has no impact on our other contracts,” Nscale told The Register. Microsoft did not respond to a request for comment. The project has already had its share of awkward moments. In March, it was revealed that the site earmarked for Britain's AI supercomputer was still being used as a scaffolding yard. Nscale has since cleared the land, but removing the scaffolding has proved easier than securing electricity. The government might be feeling a little red-faced too, having trumpeted the Loughton project as part of Microsoft's £22 billion UK investment announcement in September 2025. Just over a year later, Microsoft has walked away, and the site is still facing years of delays in getting the power it needs. The grid problems extend well beyond Essex. Ofgem proposed charging datacenter developers for connection requests earlier this year, hoping to discourage speculative projects from clogging up the queue. Demand for new connections had surged from 41GW to 125GW in less than a year, with datacenters accounting for at least 80GW. Some UK datacenter developers have turned to gas-powered generation rather than wait years for a grid connection, while others face growing resistance from the people living nearby. Residents near Google's Waltham Cross facility complained about noise, lighting and poor consultation at a public meeting in July. Britain isn’t alone in finding that the AI boom comes with substantial infrastructure demands. In the US, public enthusiasm for datacenters appears to be cooling, with concerns about electricity costs and environmental impacts increasingly souring attitudes towards new developments. For all the billions being thrown at AI infrastructure, the industry is discovering that building datacenters is the easy part. Getting them connected to the grid, and keeping the neighbors happy, is proving rather more difficult. ® Black cab drivers accused ride-hailing giant of misleading London's transport regulator, but judge says they left it too late to sue Vendor says patent ruling only restricts one specific process, not the whole product, after legal challenge from Celonis PARTNER CONTENT: A million reasons why advanced computing matters for genomics OWA borked and lengthy email queues reported on busy servers after Microsoft tweaks a Server default The first technology created with a self-disrupt button... Broadcom Wi-Fi bug turns sweet 16, still lets strangers hijack your router
2026-10-19

Broadcom Wi-Fi bug turns sweet 16, still lets strangers hijack your router

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
Chip giant knew about the flaw in 2018, researcher claims, but millions of devices may still be vulnerable A 16-year-old security flaw in Broadcom's Wi-Fi software could let attackers hijack millions of routers without knowing the network password, even though the chipmaker reportedly knew about the problem since 2018. Security researcher Quentin Kaiser disclosed the vulnerability, dubbed "WPSplinter," revealing two bugs in Broadcom's proprietary Wi-Fi implementation that date back to at least 2010. An attempted fix arrived eight years later, but Kaiser claims it didn't fully resolve the problem. The bugs lie in Broadcom's handling of Wi-Fi Protected Setup (WPS), the feature that lets users connect devices to a wireless network without manually entering a password. Kaiser found that specially crafted WPS messages could corrupt the router's memory, allowing someone within Wi-Fi range to run their own code on the device. They wouldn't need the Wi-Fi password, and the router's owner wouldn't have to do a thing. Kaiser demonstrated working exploits against routers supplied by Belgian internet providers Proximus and Orange. In one attack against an ASKEY TCG3000 gateway, he ran code on the device, pulled its Wi-Fi password from memory, and sent it back over the air. He then used the stolen credentials to join the network. He also found the flaw in Netgear and Technicolor routers supplied by VOO. He estimates that around 850,000 Proximus gateways could be affected, along with 500,000 at VOO and 210,000 at Orange Belgium. That puts the potential total at 1.56 million routers in Belgium alone, although Kaiser admits the figures are rough estimates. However, the problem is unlikely to stop at the Belgian border. Broadcom's Wi-Fi technology is used by dozens of manufacturers across hundreds of products, meaning the number of affected devices worldwide could be considerably higher. According to Kaiser, the chipmaker knew about the vulnerability back in 2018 and released a partial fix without publicly disclosing the problem. It subsequently recommended that manufacturers move away from its proprietary implementation and use the open-source hostapd software instead. Owners of routers running Broadcom's older eCos-based chips, including the BCM3380, BCM3383, and BCM33834, are in a particularly grim position. Kaiser calls the flaw a "forever day," meaning those devices may never get a fix. Routers using Linux-based BCM3390, BCM65xx, and BCM68xx chips aren't necessarily off the hook either, unless their manufacturers switch to hostapd. That leaves some older routers with no obvious route to a fix, while others depend on their manufacturers updating software that may be years out of support. Kaiser first reported the vulnerabilities to Broadcom on July 18, followed by a second report on July 27, giving the company and affected vendors 90 days before public disclosure. Broadcom responded on July 28 to say a patch was available and advised him to contact affected manufacturers directly. The following day, however, the company declined to assign a CVE identifier or provide him with a copy of the notification it said it had sent to manufacturers through its customer portal. Kaiser also contacted Proximus, attempted to reach Orange Belgium and VOO, and passed his findings to Belgium's national cybersecurity authorities. He subsequently opened a case with CERT/CC, which closed it on August 4 because Broadcom was itself a CVE Numbering Authority and had declined to register the vulnerability. By October 15, a day before his presentation, Kaiser said none of the affected devices he had identified had received a patch. Kaiser hasn't reported evidence of attacks in the wild, but the potential for mischief is considerable. Someone parked outside a home or office could potentially compromise a vulnerable router, steal network credentials, or use their access to interfere with traffic. Kaiser also credited AI with speeding up the investigation. He reckons the work would ordinarily have taken him months, but with the help of frontier models, he finished it in less than a week. ® Black cab drivers accused ride-hailing giant of misleading London's transport regulator, but judge says they left it too late to sue Vendor says patent ruling only restricts one specific process, not the whole product, after legal challenge from Celonis PARTNER CONTENT: A million reasons why advanced computing matters for genomics OWA borked and lengthy email queues reported on busy servers after Microsoft tweaks a Server default The first technology created with a self-disrupt button... Dr Garry Hunt talks to us about the astonishing Voyager probes
2026-10-19

Dr Garry Hunt talks to us about the astonishing Voyager probes

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
Voyager imaging team member joins The Reg's Kettle podcast to talk about his role in the mission as probe approaches light day's distance from Earth It's been nearly half a century since the Voyager probes launched from Earth on a mission to explore the outer solar system, and they're still running. You can listen to the latest episode of The Kettle on this page, Spotify, Apple Podcasts, or YouTube. You can also subscribe on those platforms to be notified when a new episode goes live. You're probably familiar with some of Voyager's iconic images, like the Pale Blue Dot and and its portraits of the outer planets. Those photos and observations wouldn't have been possible without Voyager's imaging team, eight members of which were selected from scientists from around the world. The UK's sole representative, Dr. Garry Hunt, joins us on this episode of The Kettle to discuss his role in the Voyager program, the future of the mission, and the state of science in the UK, US, and beyond. A lightly edited transcript is included below: Hi everyone, and welcome to another episode of The Register’s Kettle Podcast. I'm your host, Brandon Vigliarolo, and we have a very special episode for you this week. We're gonna step away from the doom and gloom of AI and the silliness of Silicon Valley to talk about something and to someone of considerably more historic importance. The Voyager space missions will mark fifty years of exploring the solar system and beyond next year. With us to talk about this is Reg Reporter Richard Speed and our very special guest, British astrophysicist Dr. Gary Hunt who was a member of the original Voyager imaging team. Thank you so much, Dr. Hunt, for coming on. And I'm gonna turn this over to Richard today, since you two have such a history together working on stories for The Reg, and I'm excited to hear about what he's going to pick your brain about. So Richard, take it away. Well Dr. Hunt, we've spoken a lot about the Voyager project over the years, but for anyone who's not aware of it, I wonder if you could summarize the mission, which is, I know, a big ask, and also what your role in it is and was. Well indeed, it's a unique mission in the sense that every 176 years the major planets Jupiter, Saturn, Uranus, Neptune, line up virtually in a straight line. And we wanted to explore the outer planets 'cause we've never been there before. I'm talking in the 1970s, late 1960s, '70s. So this is an unknown world. We thought that the major planets were keys to understanding the origin of the solar system, indeed the origin of life, so sending space missions there was a priority. But it needed to be done in a very economical way. With the planets lined up, that meant we could get there in the quickest way, requiring less fuel, and it would be the best way of going about it. So the opportunity came and NASA funded a mission to send two spacecraft at this unique time, which was in the mid '70s, 1977, to the major planets. NASA sent out an invitation to tender. They selected the ten instruments. Imaging had as an instrument in concept had been suggested, but they wanted a team of  real scientists, not astronomers, not basic astronomers. They understood this is a chance to do real detailed science, real physics, real chemistry, the whole understanding of what went on. So they were looking for an interdisciplinary group of people. They selected eight people from invitations and I gather there were about nearly a thousand people proposed and of those eight I was chosen. So I decided I suddenly found myself in this incredible position. I'm there to represent atmospheric physics. I want to understand the physics, the meteorology of the major planets. So here is an opportunity to explore the outer planets to learn about why we wanted to go to Jupiter, Saturn, Uranus, and Neptune. However, funding was tight. The president at the time was Nixon, and we only had funding as far as Jupiter and Saturn. So we were treading new territory. At the time the mission was sort of decided, we had not been through the asteroid belt. So the first question was: would the spacecraft even get that far? And of course the pioneers had just been launched at the beginning of the '70s and they managed to get through and raised a lot of questions. So I was part of the imaging team, this amazing set of what I will call prima donnas, because we all were. They were from Carl Sagan to Vern Suomi, who was a meteorologist, to Brad Smith, a whole collection of people. And we had to design the imaging system, the cameras, and be part of the organization that developed the choice of trajectories and all the other facets of this mission. In other words, we had to understand the risks as well as the potential rewards that came from it. So it was a big responsibility. And to put the whole thing in real context, I learnt in December '72 from NASA that I'd been selected. We had our first mission meetings in February of '73, and we had until the summer of 1977 to get the spacecraft launched. And the window for launching was ten days. So this was really a big precision job. So large responsibility and had many opportunities, not only to do the science, but the work that I was doing when I  took the data back to the UK was to actually serve as a trigger for planetary research within the UK. So this really was a major major part of a new world, new understanding. And as we have learned over the years, we have made history and just about all the observations we've made. Okay, great, thank you. So, I mean, as you said, it's been coming out for half a century, 50 years since the spacecraft were launched on their mission and they've been, as you say, Jupiter, Saturn, Uranus, Neptune, and so on. I was just wondering, why do you think they've lasted for so long? Because it's just incredible, really if you think about it. Well, firstly I will put my hand up and say that the engineers of JPL are exceptional. We've had a few hitches and they've overcome it. First of all, we also said we want a spacecraft. It's going to unknown territories. We don't know how long it's going to last. Reliability was the key. So it was built around the mariners, which have been the bedrock of the earlier missions to around Mars and flying by Venus. So that was the first thing. And so many of the aspects of it were built from equipment from the mid-sixties. So the issue is even more exciting to think that here we are 50 years after launch flying a spacecraft with equipment which was already 10 years old by the time we got it there. So this really is an example of reliability, which is the bottleneck. And of course, the other aspect of it we built in redundancy as well. So we knew that, if things went wrong, we had to find a way of easily overcoming it. As it's drifting away at the speed of thirty thousand miles an hour, you can't just sort of go out and repair it. You've got to be able to do things while the spacecraft is in motion. So there's a certain amount of redundancy, built in reliability, incredibly good careful planning, absolutely brilliant engineers who to this day are doing the most incredible things. Well, yes, I have to say, I agree, having spoken to the current Voyager team, they are a bunch of very impressive individuals. And they seem to keep eking out more and more life from the spacecraft. I know it's powered by RTGs, which will eventually expire and there won't be enough power to keep it going. But it seems they'll be able to continue well into the 2030s, maybe even beyond. I mean, what do you think is going to end the mission because I mean it must be coming up. Do you think it'll be a hardware failure? We have had some spacecraft failures over the years or do you think it's going to be more prosaic? Perhaps it'll be just a lack of funding. Well, if you go back to our elementary school physics, we know that as it moves away the signal strength disappears like one over the square of the distance, so it's getting pretty weak. It's incredibly weak. The brilliance of the deep space network, Goldstone, Madrid, Canberra, that's key. Furthermore the power on board and we have our own nuclear power plant on board, which is decaying plutonium, which is the key aspect of the spacecraft. That's getting less and less. We're losing several watts per year. There will come a time when the signal is literally so weak we can't pick it up. So that is one thing that we predicted about 2035. Could it become out of touch before then? Well, that's then politics versus science and engineering. And yes, it costs money to keep this thing running, but the way in which science budgets are being hacked at every moment, we are worried that once we pass our our big date of fifty years next summer, that perhaps that the politicians will say: You've had your time, enough is enough. I really hope that's not the case. Personally I hope that there's some development in deep space monitoring over the next few years that would enable us to keep picking it up beyond that. Or do you think it's simply a matter of distance and background noise that it would be unreachable? The signal is incredibly weak. and there'd be insufficient power in the spacecraft to actually power it. They've got to have some form of heating. It's pretty cold out there. or virtually everything has been turned off. They've just done a major activity with the onboard power system. They literally have done everything they can. We reckon to 2035 we will still be able – that'll be about the time it'll be too weak for anything on Earth to be able to pick it up. So that literally is the time we were anticipating the end would come, if we can keep the politicians out of the way. Well, absolutely and I mean I I think as we spoke recently it's approaching a one light day to kind of get a feeling of how far away these things are now where it takes an entire day to send a command to spacecraft a light day for the command to come back. Well, I think the issue is it's one light day in distance. Now if I'm sending you a message, suppose I'm speaking to you and you're on Voyager. I start talking to you now, it's 24 hours before you get my message, then it's 24 hours before we send it back again. Can you imagine operating like that? This is a very significant factor. This is the furthest anything it has ever got that we've been able to record. Voyager 2 will not get there until about 2030, I think it is. Because it's on a different path. But these are immense distances. So it's never happened before. It actually occurs, if you want UK time, on the 18th of November at 10.06 in the morning [0206 Pacific Time, 0506 EST]. So look at your watch when that time comes up and say, that's where Voyager 1 is. I must admit, speaking to the JPL team, and again, we work in technology, the thought of the fact you would send a command to the spacecraft, you won't even know if it's worked until the following day or a day or so later, at which point it could have done something awful to the spacecraft and it's too late to fix it. I just can't get my head around that kind of risk analysis and risk assessment, which you must do. Well, we've we've had that situation occur because you probably remember several months ago, they wasn't replying to signals and they discovered that the dish was actually pointing slightly away so it could not be picked up in Goldstone, but managed to find a way of turning it around so it could actually recommunicate. But there was another problem. All the codes for the very, very poor computer which actually has less power in it than I have in my Apple Watch I'm wearing. They were written in assembler code. Well those people who go back to the early days of computing, that is one of the very basic codes. There was nobody in JPL who had that experience. Somebody had retired and was willing to come back and find them. Everything was written down on pieces of paper. So it was a whole new ballgame. Amazon is funding a new nuclear plant in Indiana
2026-10-19

Amazon is funding a new nuclear plant in Indiana

The Verge | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
The proposed nuclear plant would provide 960 megawatts to power the grid — and Amazon’s data centers.﻿ The proposed nuclear plant would provide 960 megawatts to power the grid — and Amazon’s data centers.﻿ Amazon has signed an agreement with X-energy and American Electric Power (AEP) to fund the building of a new nuclear energy facility in Indiana. The proposed plant, which would replace the now defunct coal-fueled Breed Generating Station in Fairbanks, Sullivan County, aims to provide up to 960 megawatts of carbon-free energy for both the regional power grid and Amazon’s data centers. No timeline for construction or operation has been announced for the facility, which Amazon says “could” create 1,000 construction jobs and 100 permanent roles. The facility in Indiana is targeting to build 12 Xe-100 SMR’s, an advanced (but unproven) reactor that’s still undergoing development and licensing. These reactors can be cooled using either air, water, or a hybrid solution of both, which Amazon says should allow them to be deployed in water-constrained regions without impacting water demand in local communities. Indiana also happens to be home to a famously expensive and uncompleted energy project called the Marble Hill Nuclear Power Plant. It was abandoned half-finished in 1983 after $2.5 billion had already been sunk into it, and has been under continued demolition since 2008. Terms for Amazon’s agreement in Fairbanks haven’t been disclosed, so we can’t compare the cost between the facilities. This marks the second nuclear energy project that Amazon and X-energy are collaborating on, with the collective goal of working with regional utilities to deploy more than 5 gigawatts of new nuclear capacity in the US by 2039. In October last year, Amazon shared an early preview of its first planned nuclear plant, the Cascade Advanced Energy Facility in Washington. That project is similarly set to build 12 advanced reactors and create local jobs, but construction isn’t expected to start until the end of this decade. Amazon is one of several tech companies, including Meta, Microsoft, and Google, that are exploring their own nuclear energy projects to power AI data centers. Investing in energy grid infrastructure was also one of the commitments that Amazon outlined in a lengthy blog post earlier this month that aimed to sway some of the growing negativity around data center projects, though it’ll be many years before anyone — including Amazon — can reap the benefits of these new facilities. David Sacks is retweeting Lina Khan from the Biden administration talking about whether the AI industry needs an antitrust exemption to manage safety. They both agree that these companies don’t need antitrust exemptions. They actually should be more competitive. Again, putting that in the context of regulating these companies, thinking about the dynamic between the government and the biggest companies in the world. I’ll just ask the question, I guess, more directly. Do you think Newsom was right to give in to Paramount, or do you think he should have forced them to make good on that threat to leave? I can’t speak to the Paramount situation well enough to give you an answer, so it would be unfair for me to do so. I can say once again that our orientation today — and I think about the big AI companies and the big social media companies as well — has to be like, wait a minute, too much power in too few hands. Particularly when we talk about AI, where it’s potentially existential, but certainly transformative, which is probably a kind way to say it about the economy, that we’re going to have to get very engaged. Guardrails, of course, on both parts, but also addressing what I think on the AI front is potentially a significant change in employment in the United States. I’m not somebody who is necessarily convinced that we have to see a whole bunch of job loss, but that does feel like that’s the direction this is going. What I’m suggesting is we better be prepared, and we better be protecting people who are going to lose their jobs, or at least it appears they’re going to lose their jobs. And I’ll just give you this thought. If these days 4 percent unemployment is kind of the acceptable near full employment number, I think the problem we’re going to see is — if that’s sort of the level that we’re going to hopefully aim for and stay at — that in very short order, we could end up at 7 percent and have that be the baseline, or maybe 10 percent. And if you think about what’s happened, the upheavals of economies and of governments when you get to 10 percent unemployment in any country. We better be focused on how we’re going to give people the dignity of work and enough income so that they can make a life for themselves when you’re going to put people out of work by virtue of this technology. By the way, much of the benefit of that is going to go to a very, very few people. So we’ve got to wrestle with that and come to a conclusion. I wanted to start with some of the brinksmanship we have seen in the negotiations between, particularly, states and the big companies, because that brinksmanship is real. The threat is we will leave. We just covered, I don’t know, Kevin O’Leary in Utah talking about data centers, and the people of Utah really pushed back against Kevin O’Leary building that data center. But his argument was, “Well, I’m just going to shop around.” This is what states do. They compete with tax incentives and benefits, maybe for data centers, which will put everyone out of a job. They compete for land use rights. You’ve got a rural state with a lot of farms in it. How are you thinking about that dynamic? Do you feel like you’re in competition with the other states, or is this bipartisan realignment creating more of a level playing field where these companies are going to have to actually give something? We are in competition. We just are because it isn’t bipartisan, at least if you ask me. Unless there is a change in the government. I’ll pick on Texas for a second, and there is a chance that you’ll see a new governor in Texas as of November 3rd. In fact, a good chance. But if you look at Texas as it’s run today by a three-term, hoping to be a four-term, Republican governor and a Republican legislature, they have no intention of changing their alignment about incentives. In the state of Illinois, we don’t want to have to do that. I mean, we obviously want to protect the revenue that we’ve got for the state to pay for the things we think are valuable to our people, but we also want to make sure that we’re attracting jobs and not losing jobs. And in a world where South Carolina, Michigan, Texas, Florida — pick your poison. Michigan may be slightly different just because it’s got democratic control, but these are our competitors. When I’m competing to have a company move to Illinois, I’ve got to make sure, first of all, that I’m somewhere in the ballpark when it comes to the incentives, because otherwise we don’t even get considered. And then when I say somewhere in the ballpark, I mean, I want to give away less than more, and then I want to provide whatever other advantages I can that aren’t financial so that I can protect the resources that we have. An example of that is, we like to talk about a kind of white-glove service that we offer to companies. If you’re going to bring a factory here, let’s say, just to give an example, we want to make sure that we’ve got somebody who’s kind of a concierge for your company that’s walking you through each of our departments where you need a permit. It doesn’t mean we’re lowering the standards. It just means, a lot of times, the bureaucracy eats up so much time that it makes it impossible for a company to actually do what they want to do in your state. We try to make sure that if we’re going to attract a large company, with 1,000, 2,000, 3,000 jobs, that we’re at least making it a little easier for them to go through without having to give them massive tax incentives. So that’s the way I think about it. We are in competition. There’s just no doubt about it. Should we be? There’s a lot of discussion about whether we shouldn’t, and maybe at the federal level we should do something about leveling the playing field, but this is the world we’re living in right now. I wanted to ask those questions just to set the baseline. I really do think something is happening. A lot of young people are dissatisfied with the status quo. A lot of people in general are dissatisfied with what big tech companies are trying to do with AI, with the potential for job loss. And the relationship between the people, the government, and those companies seems more fraught than ever, especially as they’re trying to expand their physical infrastructure with data centers, as they all become defense contractors, literally taking our tax dollars to sell weapon systems and collect data for the government. That’s a big swirl. I wanted to start there, but I just want to take one second here and ask you the Decoder questions because I personally think the operation of government is so opaque. It’s very easy to figure out how big companies are run. The CEOs won’t shut up. They’re on podcasts all day long. I think most people have no idea how your job works. So you were elected governor. How is your office set up, and how have you structured the state government of Illinois? Just starting from where I sit, and then, of course, we look at it from a customer service perspective. But starting just at the top, I actually did something that no governor of Illinois has ever done — which is that I created not just one deputy governor. We obviously have a lieutenant governor who’s elected also, but a deputy governor is somebody getting appointed by me to serve under me, to help manage the government with me. What I did was put four deputy governors in place, a general counsel, and my chief of staff. Why is that important? Because what I’ve tried to do is to break down the silos of government. I took all of the health and human services-related departments — that’s the Department of Public Health, the Department of Human Services, the Department of Healthcare and Family Services, everything from health insurance, Medicaid, and public health during the pandemic — and put it all under one deputy governor working directly for me. The idea is that instead of my having, let’s say, 26 direct reports, I’ve got four or five direct reports plus my chief of staff, who each have, let’s say, six or seven departments underneath them. From my perspective, what I wanted to do was make sure that I’m breaking down the silos of government, making these departments that are often building walls, and they don’t want to talk to one another because they’re afraid they might lose jobs in one department to another department, or lose control of a program if they’re talking to one another. That’s the way it used to be. Now we’re like the deputy governor is there to either beat the heads together or at least bring them together so that they will be operating the government more efficiently. Okay, so that’s from the top. From the bottom, which is: how do people who live in Illinois interact with the state government? And obviously, technology is one of the upgrades that’s occurred and made it easier. Illinois, frankly, had not significantly invested in those processes before I became governor, or in the technology that was necessary. And I’ll just give you one example of this: We created something called BEACON, which is designed to address the problem that parents and families have with mental health for children. It turns out that there were seven different ways in which people approached the government to get help, and we weren’t coordinating those, and we weren’t helping to provide all of the resources. I’m talking about outside resources. How do you find a therapist? How do you find a psychiatrist? And how do you pay for those things? Medicaid, if you can’t afford it on your own. But the point is, there’s one door to enter through BEACON that allows you to get access to all of those resources, even if they’re in multiple departments. So that’s just one example. We’re trying to think of this as a customer service operation. It’s one thing to say this is how the org chart looks and another thing to say, this is how you access all the resources of the government that you need. We provide programs that really matter to people. We have a birth equity initiative to reduce maternal mortality, particularly in Black and Brown communities. And free diapers for some people. The problem is, people don’t even know that the programs exist, and how would you ever figure out that the programs exist, especially if you’re a working person who’s working two minimum-wage jobs? You don’t have any time, and you don’t have any money, and you don’t even know how to talk to the government. And so we’re trying to fix all of those problems of the government with the way that we interact with people. It’s funny. It feels like that is one of the bigger opportunities when it comes to AI, just letting the AI navigate a bureaucracy for you and communicate what resources are available. The flip side of that is that people hate AI. They truly dislike it, and it doesn’t feel like that connection has been made — that a lot of these tools can actually just navigate a bureaucracy for you. Are you thinking that should be an investment that you make, that it might be what turns the tide in terms of how people feel about these tools? Yes, but I’m not plunging headlong into it. And I realize that there are probably states that are plunging headlong into it. I mean, the United States government rolled out its AI chatbot to look at webpages across the government. I guess what I’m trying to do is make sure that… I created something called the AI Cabinet in my government, and that is to essentially bring the experts on AI together with the leaders of my mainstay agencies of state government, so that we’re thinking about privacy, safety, and efficiency. And those things have to be talked about together. You can’t just say we’re going after efficiency and we don’t really care about the other two. So that’s the idea, the AI cabinet. It’s also that, honestly, there are things, as you know, about AI that are dangerous. And so we’ve got our emergency management agency on that AI cabinet. We’ve got our state police on that AI cabinet. And remember, a lot of the people that work in those agencies aren’t particularly proficient at AI or really understand the potential and the potential dangers. That’s why we brought in experts, for example, from the University of Chicago, the University of Illinois, just as two examples of places where we’re drawing expertise. So yeah, there’s a lot to be done. I would say we’re trying to approach it carefully before it gets out of control to a point where we have no way to pull it back in. The other Decoder question I ask everybody is about decisions. You have a big job. You have some constraints in your decision-making process, right? There’s a state legislature, there’s a federal government, you have other agencies in your state. How do you make decisions? What’s your framework? Well, I don’t do it the way Donald Trump does. It’s funny you say it that way. I guess I start with the idea that we have a problem to solve and that decisions have to get made. Are those decisions ones that need to come to the governor? Because you’re right, I only have so much bandwidth. So the question is: which are the most serious ones that can’t be solved by other people, or are unlikely to get the outcomes that I would want by handing them over to other people? And then those automatically come to me. So what I’m making decisions about is kind of the first answer to your question, and then it’s how do I actually make the decisions? For me, I am not one of those people who thinks that I’m right 100 percent of the time. Maybe a good way to describe it, and I’m joking a little bit when I say it, is that I’m 90 percent sure I’m right, but there’s also a 10 percent chance that I’m dead wrong. I want to make sure that I’m getting input and a debate before I actually make the final decision. Someone once told me that the best CEOs are ones that, in their organization, are a little bit below average in terms of their capability and intellect. I’m not saying we want below-average leaders. I’m just saying you’re so good at hiring that you’ve got people in your organization who are really far better than you are at various things that you’re not particularly good at. Maybe knowing what you don’t know and being willing to ask for help is a philosophy that I have. Be willing to ask for help if it’s something you really don’t know. And I’ll be honest, when you become governor of a state, there are a whole lot of topics within the purview of a governor. Making decisions about all those topics, maybe a human being who has super intelligence might be able to do it, but most people don’t start out knowing everything there is to know about public health, public safety, and education. And so you start out with what you know and then try to add expertise to it and then make decisions based upon the best experts debating one another in front of you. At least that’s how I like to do it. Give me an example. Governor Kathy Hochul from New York was on the show. She said in her first week she needed to declare a state of emergency because of a hurricane, and she just didn’t know how, which was very humanizing in its way. Give me an example of when you became governor, and there was something where you just didn’t know. I mean, honestly, no one runs for governor thinking there’s going to be a global pandemic. You think you’re going to solve education problems for people or put more money in people’s pockets by raising the minimum wage or whatever, but COVID comes along. And I have to admit, the level of expertise, I don’t think any governor… Well, maybe Josh Green, the doctor who’s the governor of Hawaii, maybe he had a lot to [of expertise] start with — but for the rest of us humans, that was one where we were learning on the job. We were working 18 hours per day. My entire team was all in, and we had to do things like emergency procurement of ventilators and masks, and we had to go outside the box. It’s not like we could just put it through our central management services, look for a contract, or whatever. This was like, I need masks in three days. I’ll tell you a quick story, if you’re interested, about my interaction with Donald Trump at the time because I think it’s interesting, anyway. But I’m not a big fan of Donald Trump, in case you didn’t know. I was one year into office when the COVID pandemic hit, and I had not... I mean, I talked to his administration, but I didn’t talk to him directly. So I picked up the phone and called him, and I said, “Mr. President.” By the way, I wrote a script for myself because I know he likes to be flattered, and I’m not somebody who normally likes to flatter him, but I wrote a script for myself. I saved it; I even have it today. And I said, “Mr. President, you’re a great businessman, but you can understand that paying $8 for an N95 mask that should cost us 75 cents means we’re in a national emergency. We need to be able to get these things and afford them, and I’m competing against all the other states. We need to invoke the Defense Production Act in order to make sure that we’re making enough masks in the United States.” That’s what I told him. And then I said, “But I need masks right now. What can you do for me?” I said, “I need 2 million N95 masks, and I need 4,000 ventilators.” And he never delivered, just so you know, even though he promised that he would deliver. The challenges in COVID were mighty, and the organization that we had to create around it… I had meetings every morning just on this topic, figuring out, okay, how many hospital beds [are available]? We didn’t have a system that would tell us statewide how many hospital beds are available in each region. What happens if you have an outbreak and it really is overtaking some region? What do we do? And so these were all the things we had to learn on the fly. And all I can tell you is, once again, back to hiring great people to have around you, I really felt like I proved that I had done a good job of hiring because the people around me were tremendous. One of whom, by the way, is now running as my lieutenant governor partner in this election. It’s interesting you brought up COVID in the context of our conversation about generational political realignment. I would actually point to COVID as the beginning of a particular realignment around what the government should measure, how much control the government should have over people, and all of that has led, I think, directly into the bipartisan pushback against Flock cameras. The very notion of privacy is not, in my mind, left or right anymore. The CEO of Flock got himself in all kinds of trouble because he kept saying all the people who wanted to ban Flock cameras are criminals, and he would go on MAGA media and say this. I think that position was just rejected. We can see in states around the country that there’s pushback against Flock cameras. I would point to COVID as the beginning of that. The idea that the government could be too intrusive, and in particular, the government should not be able to measure things. “If you don’t test, there won’t be COVID” just became an idea. And the idea that we shouldn’t measure things, or we should tear down our systems of measurement, is a big part of the second Trump administration. But I see that privacy connection very clearly to COVID. In one world, the government coming together to protect its citizens and build a bunch of systems could have gone a very different way. In the one we live in, there’s ferocious pushback to that, on the left and the right in some ways. How do you feel about privacy right now? I know Illinois leads the nation. You’ve got the Biometric Identification Protection Act. Every time I fly to Chicago, I’ve got to push the button at the airport to say that it’s okay to look at my face. That’s one way of going about it. But then you do have, I think, hundreds of Flock cameras in Cook County alone. People have Meta glasses with cameras in them. There’s something happening with privacy that is running right into the aftershocks of COVID and how people feel about government intrusion. How are you finding a way through that balance? As you’re suggesting. I mean, talk about a collision. This focus on privacy is warranted and long overdue, to be honest with you. I understand what you’re describing about COVID, but I think something that came out of COVID got accelerated, and that was conspiracy theories. And I’m not suggesting that everything you hear now about the pushback around privacy is a conspiracy theory, but it is easy for these things to spread, and people have misinformation that’s informing them about how these things are used. Having said that, I guess what I think is that we are having to solve multiple problems at the same time, and that AI is coming. Let’s just go to AI for a second. It’s here. I mean, I say it’s coming, but it’s here. It’s not going to stop. We’re not going to become Luddites without any AI. So the question is, in the context of privacy, what guardrails are we putting on AI, and how are we managing it? This idea that the president and David Sacks had, where they got all the companies together and said, “Hey, how about you guys self-police?” That’s not going to work, at least not for protecting people’s privacy. And frankly, it’s not going to provide any safety either because everybody’s going to operate in their own self-interest. It’s one of the reasons why you need a government, in my view. I’m not suggesting the government is any smarter than these companies. But one of the reasons you have any kind of collective action of the government is because, separately, as you know, people operate in their own self-interest. I mean, you could have a Hobbesian world where people are killing each other, and there are no rules about it. So I think we’ve got to use the government as a tool and also be smart within the government. And that’s where the generational change, I think, is hugely important. Use the government as a smarter, less bureaucratic collection of people devoted to protecting the privacy of everyone and making sure that we’re addressing all the other challenges that come from AI. So I guess I’ll just say, I don’t think we’re anywhere near where we need to be, but I will say, if we don’t have a federal government, now I’ve got a lot of... I’m very proud of the things that we’ve done here in Illinois around the AI Safety Measures Act, which is the toughest in the country, the Children’s Social Media Safety Act, and so on. I’m proud of the work we’ve done, but state by state, this is not the way to do this. We’re doing the best we can in the absence of a federal government that is addressing these issues. And when I say federal government, I’m not just pointing at Trump. I’m pointing at Congress. No action on their part means that we’re going at it alone. And right now, I don’t know how many AI safety measure acts have been passed, but it looks like three to me. I mean, it’s New York, California, and Illinois. Maybe you could name a couple of others, but we three were the first. And yet, we’ve got 47 other states. This worries me a great deal. You’re raising issues, and I’d love to tell you I have all the answers. I can tell you what we’re trying to think about is that we are the… People sometimes say that the states are laboratories of democracy. They’re actually laboratories of policy, and we are trying to, at least in Illinois, use ourselves as a laboratory for policy while not creating disadvantage for ourselves. And so that’s a very challenging thing to do, and why we need to change what’s happening at the federal government. Focus on privacy for one second. I understand what you’re saying about AI. I think part of the privacy debate around Flock cameras, for example, is, well, sure, you can have one license plate reader in one intersection. And if the cops had to go there and pull the SD card out of it, drive it back to headquarters, upload it to their Apple IIe, and look at it manually, maybe people would feel one way about it. But those cameras are all networked. They don’t have any access controls really. There are infinite reports of local police chiefs tracking their girlfriends across state lines with these cameras. You add in a system like Palantir, which can synthesize multiple databases and do all kinds of tracking across all kinds of systems. People see that. The turn that is being made is that you can connect a lot of databases of information together, search them without a warrant, and track people across the entire country. You could ban that state by state. You could regulate it city by city, which we’re seeing happen with Flock, and you could maybe have some federal controls around privacy there. None of it’s really happening, but on the ground, people are ferociously pushing back. And that’s the connection I’m making to COVID, conspiracy theories, and how much power the government should have to surveil you and look at you. Something happened in there where people’s attitudes about that really shifted. And I would say one of my core beliefs is that Americans do not like being told what to do. Party politics aside, Americans don’t like being told what to do. Something about that flipped and connected to: we have a network of license plate-reading cameras, and we’re going to tear them down when we see them because there’s something about that that offends us as Americans. Illinois does have privacy laws. I think the last update to the biometric law you did was in 2024, and it has changed again. It’s gotten more vociferously opposed to tracking and surveillance in this country. Do you think you need another update to that privacy law? I think we should be looking at that question every year. I’ll give you an example of it. We passed it, and again, I’m back to AI because I think how you’re describing it was a perfect way to say it. If it was an SD card that they had to go grab and then analyze back at the station, it’d be a different thing. But it’s AI that’s operating these things, and that’s allowing this stuff to move instantaneously and track people. Look, should we be addressing it? Yeah, every year. It’s like the issue of ethics in government. What I mean is, the minute you put an ethics law in place, there’s somebody who’s trying to get around it. I mean, every year, it’s like every law... this is probably true across all of capitalism, maybe. There are always people trying to find a way around the law. Put a tax law in place, and someone’s trying to figure out how to get around it. And I think the point you’re making is that that’s why you need to have a proactive government, less bureaucratically and more aggressively trying to address these things. Are we going to be able to keep up with it? I’ll be honest, I don’t think so. And I also don’t think you can say, “Well, we’re just going to shut it all down” because there are things you’re missing out on. It’s sort of like, and again, I keep going back to AI, but I think a lot about it. My children are 21 and 24. They’re going to live to 100 or 110 years old because AI is going to solve a whole bunch of medical challenges, and already is. There’s a cure now for sickle cell anemia, and people who would otherwise die at 25 or 30 years old are going to live full lives. This is going to happen across the board in cancer and everything else. And so there’s all good in there, and then there’s the bad. So I know what you’re saying. I guess all I can tell you is I’m a believer that we can progressively — I don’t mean that in a political fashion — address these challenges. In Illinois, we passed a law that disallows the use of AI in therapy for people because we think too much information gets collected, not enough humanity in the process, and we think that it should have humanity in it. Again, we’re not trying to shut it down across the board, but we’re going to have to see how it gets used and where it’s improper. And I know that sounds imperfect because the technology is moving so fast that the idea that we’re going to be able to keep up with all the ways in which someone’s privacy could be breached seems very difficult, but I don’t know any other way to do it. That’s why you have a government, and maybe in the end we’re going to have this competing use of things like AI, where AI is monitoring AI, and AI that’s kind of privacy-friendly or focused on privacy is going to be monitoring the AI that’s not. But again, I know you don’t want to just talk about AI. I am concerned. This issue of privacy has been around for a long time. You’ve been talking about it, I’m sure; I’ve been talking about it since before AI. This is like everybody giving over their personal information in exchange for access to something. And that was happening for a while, and then we’re all getting ads that are personalized to us based on stuff that we’ve essentially given up privacy for. No, it’s funny. People believe, and we can write as many stories as we want about how your iPhone is not actually listening to you, but the ad tracking is so good that everyone believes that their iPhones are listening to them. The next turn is that it is now technologically feasible for these systems to listen to you all the time. And I can’t fall back on the idea that it’s cheaper for them to do the ad tracking when you hit I accept than to listen to you all the time, which was, for a decade, the right answer. The economically correct answer was that ad tracking was cheaper than listening to you all the time. That might be very different in the future. There are already devices that we cover here at The Verge that purport to listen to you all the time and turn that into some actionable data. And it feels to me like the tech industry is trying to drive a truck through our privacy law, through our lack of AI regulations, through an entire system of governance that is designed for the pre-AI era. The notion that you don’t have privacy in public worked really well when everyone didn’t have a cell phone that could connect to the cloud and instantly search every photo all at once, all the time, and do facial recognition. We live in that world now. I would say the public sentiment towards the idea that you don’t have privacy in public places has turned. They don’t like the Meta glasses. They call them pervert glasses for a reason. And somewhere in there is, well, the federal government should pass privacy law. It is a failure of imagination in the United States. We don’t have federal privacy law. And then somewhere in there is the notion that the states are laboratories of democracy and policy. It is amazing, by the way, how often on this tech and business show, Thomas Hobbes comes up and the very notion that the government is there to serve as an extension of us solving collective action problems. It comes up over and over again because I think the tech industry does not want to respect that dynamic. This is why I started with that relationship between government and business, and the pushback and the negotiation, because here it feels like the tech industry is in a bubble of its own making. Truly, in a media bubble of its own making. And then all of the polling is, well, we hate AI. In particular, young people hate AI. And then all of the action is around data centers where NIMBYism, the most American of policy levers, is being pulled to keep these data centers out of communities. And that’s one way to stop AI. You just pulled back on data center tax incentives in July. You’re pushing the state legislature to adopt a suite of guarantees on reforms for new construction. Do you think data centers and the pushback on data centers is an effective route towards AI regulation? No, I don’t. I think it’s an effective route toward data center regulation. I know that people have conflated and put together their opposition to data centers with their opposition to AI. And a lot of people can’t describe why they’re opposed to one or the other because they’re conflated with one another. But I can say that — I mean, at least our aim is focused on the idea, and people really feel this, right, and there’s real data to back this up — the data centers themselves put aside what they’re doing inside a data center. The data centers and what’s happening are eating up the available electricity, and that’s causing your electric bill to go up. At least that’s how everybody feels. There are a lot of things that we should have been doing in many years gone by to get to a point where electricity bills shouldn’t have to go up now. And that has to do with building out the grid. It’s a world where regulation and cross-border challenges have made it nearly impossible for a lot of the grid to get built the way that it should have. We produce a lot more electricity in the United States than we use, but we just don’t transport it well enough, and we would have a lot more available if we transported it better. Okay, but even if you think that can’t happen efficiently, we need to build more capacity on the grid. My only point is the opposition to data centers is, in part, a result of the economic situation that’s happening right now in the country, where electric bills, gas, rent, everything has gone up. Your electric bill shows up every month, and it continues to go up, and people kind of scratch their heads and say, “Who am I paying for?” This is happening, and this is particular to Illinois. We’re not going to have a whole discussion about energy policy, but I could just say this: We produce more nuclear power in the state of Illinois than any other state. Roughly 53 or 54 percent of our electricity comes from nuclear power, and we’re a net electricity exporter. So we have an excess in Illinois, but our bills are going up, and that’s because of the methodology of the regional transmission organizations. Again, not to get into all the details of it, but essentially, if Ohio decides to build a hyperscale data center, our bills in Illinois are affected, and that’s shocking to people who live in Illinois. But what they know is they don’t want their bill to go up, so they don’t want another data center nearby — even though the one in Ohio is actually causing the problem, even if you choose to oppose data centers. So this is a challenge. I think there’s a lot of information that needs to be spread. But most of all, we just need a whole lot more capacity and a whole lot more grid that will, I think, subside some of the concern about data centers. To your point, privacy is, I think, more related to AI than data centers. And it’s not just AI, but the way AI is used. So we’re going to have a lot of opposition. You make an excellent point. I think this feeling of being out of control — you are not in control of your environment anymore — that’s the thing that is causing anxiety. I want to go back to something really important that I was saying earlier. We need better people running for public office. We need smarter policy development in government. And part of that is a generational shift that’s needed and a better understanding. We have people who are serving in the United States Senate who have no idea. Was it the Secretary of Education, Trump’s Secretary of Education, who called it A1? I mean, because we’ve got older people who don’t have any exposure who are making policy theoretically on these things, and it should disturb us all. It’s one of the reasons we’ve got to advance the cause of better people and better systems in government. We are on the cusp of an election. There might be a new Congress. There might be new leadership in Congress. There are a bunch of old Democrats too. In fact, I could probably point directly to Joe Biden’s age in the last election as the reason that he lost. Do you think that you need a wholesale change of leadership of the older Democrats as well? You said, “Do we need a wholesale change?” I mean, I can tell you that I don’t think we should be telling — Well, now I live in New York. I’ll just ask you, do you think Chuck Schumer should be the Senate majority leader if the Democrats take the Senate? That’s a decision people in the Senate are going to make. I have no influence over it. I can tell you this: I am opposed to term limits because I think that you should have all the choices. You’re a voter. If you want to vote for the 75-year-old, I mean, go ahead. I’m just suggesting that a great case could be made for the 45-year-old who’s just as smart as the 75-year-old was when he was 45, or maybe is now, but has a modern look at the issues around AI privacy, everything else we’re discussing, and a more aggressive view of how we ought to be reining in big corporations in their invasion of our lives. But I think people ought to make their own choices. I mean, for us to say, “Well, we should have an age limit. You’re 70, that’s it, you’re out.” I think that belies who we are as a democracy. Well, you convinced me. I’m 46. I’m announcing my run for president here with you, Governor. That was a rousing speech. Listen, I would vote for you in a second if the choice was you and Donald Trump. Let me tie two ideas together at the end. We’ve talked a lot about AI regulation. You mentioned social media regulation a little bit earlier in this conversation. I know that Illinois has a social media law. You have a bell-to-bell ban on phones in schools for kids. I’m just going to pick on Mark Zuckerberg. It’s easy to pick on Mark Zuckerberg. If you look at how he talks about social media regulation, he basically describes the tragedy of the commons. You signed the landmark settlement with him. I think Illinois is due to get $750 or $760 million out of that settlement. And a big part of it is that TikTok and YouTube have to agree to the same terms, and then Meta will pay the rest of the money. And Zuckerberg very clearly has said that’s because if Instagram falls back on engagement bait and TikTok doesn’t, the kids will just use TikTok. And so the government has to get everyone to agree. I will tell you that Neal Mohan, the CEO of YouTube, does not agree. He’s not going to sign onto this. That’s one whole fight. Look at how Mark Zuckerberg talks about AI regulation. There’s none of that dynamic at all. He’s like, “It should be a free-for-all. We’ll all compete. If it’s not safe, the market will tell us. We’ll be fine.” And in my mind, these ideas are at total odds with each other. In the market where he makes all the money, the government has to step in and stop him from hurting kids because apparently there’s no market demand for safe social media. And in the market where the AI CEOs are telling us they might kill us all if we don’t stop them, it’s a free-for-all, and the market will tell us if they accidentally kill everybody. How do you reconcile those things? Are you just happy that you got the social media case done after a decade of failing to regulate social media, or do you think you can solve AI with regulation this time instead of letting it just cook? Look, I’m an optimist at heart, so I’m not going to suggest to you that I absolutely know that we can solve all the problems that we’re now facing, but I don’t know what else we can do. Other than, again, progressively making sure that we’re trying to address each of these things, trying to anticipate them to the extent we can. Can we be faster than AI is developing? I don’t know. But we can’t stop trying. I know that. And I would suggest to you that my optimism is founded because I think the vast majority of people want what you’re describing, which is reining it in. And again, without destroying companies. I don’t think we’re all trying to do that. What we’re trying to do is stop the invasion into our home, stop the invasion into our children’s lives. I do think we can do that, but that’s because we’ve solved a whole lot of huge problems. That’s kind of like my answer to: is AI potentially existential? The answer, of course, is yes. And can we solve it? I don’t know. Nuclear weapons... I mean, just to give something of an analogy to something else. I mean, nuclear weapons are still hanging over us as a danger to humanity. And so far anyway, we’ve managed not to blow up the world. I don’t know whether that gives you any optimism, but it does to me. We, as a country, can figure out how we want to rein it in here. That we, as a country, can work with our allies to figure out how we want to collectively try to rein it in. And then we have enemies in the world and competitors in the world, and we’ve got to figure out how we’re going to... Maybe it’s mutually assured destruction, like it was with nuclear weapons, but these are all things we’re going to have to work on, and work on fast. And it’s scary because someone analogized this to... I mean, think about the early 1950s when every kid had to learn that if there was a nuclear blast, an atomic bomb that went off, get under your desk. That wouldn’t have been very effective. But the point is, people lived with the idea that a terrible nuclear holocaust could occur with a nuclear explosion. And so I think we’re heading into that world again with AI. I hope I didn’t stray too much from your question, but — Let me ask you this, and it’s a little bit of a wild card here at the end. We cover this industry so closely here, and the big difference with nuclear weapons is that there weren’t five companies building bleeding-edge nuclear weapons in private capacity, led by five guys who all hated each other. But that’s the dynamic of the AI industry. They all truly hate each other, and they’re all trying to make as much money as they can as fast as they can. And then some of them think the AI is alive. If you ask Anthropic, and we’ve done this, “Is Claude alive?” They don’t say no. They will argue with you about the definition of alive and suggest that, to them, alive means having a biological component and Claude doesn’t have that, but it might be conscious. There’s a race to build AGI in the industry. Some people truly believe they’re building alien minds that have a different kind of consciousness. I’m not sure any government at any level has ever had to regulate that, or has ever come up against an industry that is religious about the fact that it might be building the next kind of intelligence. Where do you stand? Do you think that they’re building a next kind of consciousness that stands alongside humans, or do you think that this is normal technology as some others do? Boy, that is really another podcast for us, but I’ll just say this one thing: I just want to disagree with you for a second. Nobody could ever imagine a nuclear weapon either. And... But the government built nuclear weapons. It wasn’t — I understand. I’m not suggesting it’s the same. I’m suggesting it’s so new at the time that figuring out how you’re going to rein it in… Because remember, it’s not just one government, and the same thing with AI. It’s not just those five guys either. I mean, it’s like there’s somebody in China. Maybe five of those. I don’t know, and somebody in Europe or whatever. You could say we’re ahead, or they’re ahead. I don’t know. But all I’m saying is, yeah, this is a proliferated situation and it’s not government-controlled. One hundred percent, you’re right; it’s not like nuclear weapons in that sense. But it is like nuclear weapons in the sense that nobody’s ever seen it before. It’s a complete mystery to start with. How are we going to rein it in? I mean, nobody thought Russia was going to get the bomb, and they got it. We’ve figured out somehow how to deal with it. Again, you’ve got to be an optimist to believe we can end up with the right solution here. I don’t know that we have the answer to “how do we rein it in?” I will say, though, that if we had the power to create it, we have the power, in my view, to rein it in. Is it conscious? You’re asking a deep question. I mean, do you think it’s conscious? Because I understand why people think it might have consciousness or it’s going to be conscious. But I think that’s why the ethicists and technologists need to be involved. And especially, in my view, the government’s got to be involved because how else are you going to rein in the five guys and the companies that have developed this? There is no other way. I will tell you, I don’t think it’s conscious. And every now and again, you have an experience where it’s blindingly clear why other people believe that. When you watch a reasoning model take 90 steps over an hour and arrive at a conclusion, it’s impressive, and you can see why people believe that. I do not, and I have a lot of reasons for that. It just occurs to me that if you set out to regulate a technology like this, if you believe one thing, you would maybe go down one path. And if you believe that you were building a consciousness, you might go down another. Again, the whole industry itself does not agree on this. Jensen Huang and Dario Amodei do not agree on whether it’s alive. Jensen just wants to sell a bunch of chips, and he will happily tell you it’s just technology and you can control it. I think Dario thinks he’s building AGI, and that’s a different... There’s a whole reason he proposes different kinds of regulatory structures there. I’m just asking you, have you felt the AGI? Do you think it’s one path or the other? I’m not going to give you a satisfactory yes or no. I mean, I haven’t felt it. I guess that’s what I’m... My basic answer is I haven’t felt it. Governor Pritzker, this was very fun. Thank you for indulging this last existential loop of a question. You’re going to have to come back to Decoder soon. Questions or comments?
