Major European mobile carriers are teaming up for a ‘direct’ satellite program without Starlink

The Verge | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
It will also need to find a satellite constellation to rival SpaceX’s.﻿ It will also need to find a satellite constellation to rival SpaceX’s.﻿ Deutsche Telekom, Orange, Telefónica, and Vodafone signed a letter of intent saying they’re considering a joint bid for spectrum that would support European direct-to-device satellite competition for SpaceX’s Starlink. The four telecom companies say they would handle the ground infrastructure while a separate, unannounced satellite partner provides the satellite constellation: The joint initiative by Deutsche Telekom, Orange, Telefónica and Vodafone has a clear ambition: to meet customer demand for universal coverage in underserved areas, strengthen the resilience of public networks, protect critical communications, and support the development of a European constellation. As part of the project, the four operators plan to establish key ground network infrastructure as well as critical coordination and monitoring functions in Europe, without investing directly in a satellite constellation. This will be provided by a satellite partner still to be selected. The partnership between the MNO-led initiative and the satellite operator would combine European governance and market access with proven satellite technology and deployment expertise. AT&T, T-Mobile, and Verizon announced their own collaboration in the US earlier this year that would combine spectrum in rural areas and dead zones “to increase capacity, improve the customer experience, and help satellite providers reach more customers through a unified platform.” In May, the EU said it was considering a plan to reserve some of the 2 GHz band for local companies to offer satellite services, an idea that SpaceX has criticized, claiming it would worsen service in Ukraine, reports FT. It’s hard to imagine wireless headphones ever getting lighter than these
2026-10-19

It’s hard to imagine wireless headphones ever getting lighter than these

The Verge | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
The Final Porta weigh just 40 grams and offer up to eight hours of battery life.﻿ The Final Porta weigh just 40 grams and offer up to eight hours of battery life.﻿ If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement. If you’re on the hunt for lightweight wireless headphones but wearing earbuds or clip-ons never feels comfortable or secure, a relatively obscure Japanese headphone maker could have the solution. Final’s new Porta headphones feature an on-ear design supported by a headband but with a structure made from a single piece of wire so they weigh just 40 grams. For comparison, Marshall’s recently-launched Milton A.N.C. are also designed to be smaller on-ear headphones but weigh 200 grams. The Final Porta are available for preorder now through the company’s online store for 12,800 yen (around $80) with black or orange ear pads and shipping expected to start on October 23rd. The company also has an Amazon shop for US-based customers, but the Porta aren’t listed there yet. Sporting a design that looks loosely inspired by the lightweight headphones Sony bundled with its Walkmans decades ago, the Porta’s functionality is unsurprisingly basic. A 57mAh battery charges over USB-C and provides up to eight hours of use while the Porta are wirelessly paired to a device using Bluetooth 5.4 and streaming audio over the SBC and AAC codecs. There’s no active noise cancelation which is not uncommon for on-ear headphones that don’t create a full seal, but with an IPX4 rating you should be able to use the Porta in the rain or while working up a sweat without worrying about them getting damaged by the moisture. In July 2021, a wildfire burned through a forest on the University of Alaska, Fairbanks campus, stripping away the moss and organic-rich soil insulating the permafrost below. Five years later, I followed researchers into the forest to investigate how fighting fires could help keep that carbon locked in the ground. Researchers repeatedly stranded them alone on a virtual desert island for 30 hours in an experimentreminiscent of the movies Cast Away or Home Alone. The agents started behaving in surprising ways. They set about exploring the limits of the world, and came up with their own goals—attempting to scale the highest point on the map, or seeing how high they could throw a block. A lot of what they were doing met the bar for play. But is it truly playful behavior, or simply role-playing? MIT Technology Review
Inside the quest to combat Arctic fires and save the planet’s frozen carbon
2026-10-19

Inside the quest to combat Arctic fires and save the planet’s frozen carbon

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
In the middle of an especially warm July in 2021, a wildfire ignited in a thick forest of black spruce and peeling paper birch trees on the northern campus of the University of Alaska, Fairbanks. The blaze, possibly sparked by lightning, burned across the dry carpet of feather mosses and lichen before climbing into the crowns. By the time fire agencies responded, flames had reached 12 feet in length and sent up a 500-foot column of smoke. A few days shy of the five-year anniversary of the Yankovich Road Fire, I followed three members of the Alaska Fire Science Consortium into the boreal forest, walking along cross-country ski trails lined by feathery field horsetail. In 2024, the organization, which hosts presentations to keep wildfire managers abreast of new developments in fire science, helped turn the area into an educational walking trail, with interpretive signs posted along the route. About a half-mile in, the horizon opened up as we reached a sparse stand of naked, blackened tree trunks, stripped of needles and neighbors. Hilary Shook, the coordinator of the consortium, stepped off the trail with her thaw probe, a T-shaped pointed metal rod used to detect the upper boundary of the permafrost, the ancient layer of frozen soil that underlies 15% of the land around the Northern Hemisphere and stores roughly twice as much carbon as the atmosphere. A test of the ground just outside the burn scar hit the concrete-like layer about a foot below the surface. But as Shook applied pressure here, where the fire had burned away the moss and organic-rich topsoil that insulates the permafrost, the probe plunged at least three times deeper into the ground. “That was an immediate effect of the fire,” said Alison York, the outgoing coordinator of the organization, who was standing nearby. “When we monitored right after, it had already thawed quite a bit.” This rudimentary tool, offering a measure of depth from a single point in the earth, reveals an escalating problem across the far north. As climate change fuels increasingly frequent and severe fires in the planet’s northern latitudes, they’re accelerating the thawing of more and more permafrost. This allows the organic matter to break down and release powerful greenhouse gases that contribute to a spiraling climate feedback effect, propelling more warming, more fires, and the loss of still more permafrost. Most dramatically, intensifying wildfires in Canada released more carbon than the nation’s combined fossil fuel emissions in 2023. As in Alaska, many of these megafires occur in vast remote areas where the policy is to let them burn so long as they don’t threaten human habitats. Yet if intensifying fires and warming are left unchecked, the world’s boreal forests and permafrost could increasingly transition from giant carbon sinks into massive sources of atmospheric carbon. Now, international teams of researchers across the US, Canada, and elsewhere are scrambling to understand what’s at stake, financially as well as ecologically. They’re looking at preventive measures, like controlled burns, and studying the potential to rapidly detect and extinguish wildfires before they grow out of control. They’re even exploring what was once unthinkable: sending in human firefighters not to save lives or structures, but to protect one of our planet’s great stores of carbon. “We need to figure out a better way to live with wildfire," says Brendan Rogers, a senior scientist at the Woodwell Climate Research Center, one of the main organizations driving the loosely collaborative research effort. “I do think that it can make a meaningful difference." Woodwell, the University of Victoria’s Polis Institute for Ecological Governance, and the nonprofit Alaska Venture Fund are now partnering on a multimillion-dollar initiative known as North Fire to study and raise public awareness about the potential benefits of these interventions, MIT Technology Review can report. The idea is complicated and controversial for a number of reasons. Wildfire is a natural and necessary component of healthy forest ecosystems. Preventing or suppressing fires across huge stretches of largely unmanaged Arctic-boreal forests and tundra would take vast resources. And it’s not yet clear how much of a difference such interventions would make in emissions.Ultimately, the mere fact that researchers are considering such extreme interventions into natural systems is a grim reflection of the dangers we’ve placed ourselves in by continuing to generate more and more greenhouse-gas emissions each year. Those threats are forcing us to grapple with increasingly difficult questions that involve ever steeper trade-offs: What’s the best way to spend our finite resources and dwindling time to avoid the worst dangers of climate change? And when, if ever, is it okay to put people in possible danger to reduce broader risks to the world? Emissions from forest fires have risen 60% around the world since 2001—but they’ve nearly tripled across the boreal forests in the far north. This stretch of the globe is warming as much as four times faster than the rest of the planet, drying out the landscape. In addition, lightning strikes that set off fires have ticked up sharply as warmer air increases the convective energy in the atmosphere, driving the updrafts that trigger thunderstorms. In that sense, humans have already interceded in these otherwise natural ecosystems by producing the greenhouse-gas emissions that are increasing temperatures, subjecting forests to fire patterns well outside healthy historic norms. “To me, that outweighs the existential dread of ‘Should we or should we not intervene?” says Kate Hayes, an assistant professor at Western Washington University, who is working with Woodwell to study the emissions benefits of fire interventions. “We’ve already intervened, arguably. And so from here, it’s a little bit of a Band-Aid on a bullet wound.” The permafrost that encircles the northern latitudes, underlying large stretches of Canada, Greenland, Siberia, Scandinavia, and other Arctic nations, stores around 1.5 trillion tons of carbon, much of which froze in place millennia ago. The steady global rise in temperature, however, has already shrunk the footprint of Arctic permafrost by about 12 % since 1850, with that loss rapidly accelerating during the last 50 years. By 2100, fire-driven thawing of permafrost could release about the equivalent of nearly 80 billion tons of carbon dioxide, under high warming scenarios. That’s more than half the emissions that fires will release from the direct combustion of vegetation, according to a January study. And it would be on top of more than 400 billion additional tons that could be released through thawing from general warming over that period. Despite its growing contributions to climate change, only a minority of the models that have informed the UN’s major climate reports to date include the gradual loss of permafrost through warming temperatures, and none incorporate the abrupt collapse and accelerated thaw that can often occur after fires and heat waves. In light of those phenomena and other powerful and undercounted climate feedback effects, we’re almost certainly underestimating how rapidly the planet will warm up, as scientists warned in a paper earlier this year. These warming-driven emissions are a “massive blind spot,” says Brian Buma, a senior climate scientist at the Environmental Defense Fund and a coauthor of the paper, who is working to develop tools that could help fire agencies decide which fires to prioritize. As escalating fire and permafrost emissions become significant additional contributors to climate change, some researchers have begun to explore what it might cost to take on the responsibility of preventing them, in much the way we’ve sought to cut the pollution from power plants, vehicles, and factories. A 2022 paper in Science Advances, led by researchers at Woodwell and the Union of Concerned Scientists, found that making greater efforts to put out boreal fires could be a cost-effective climate solution—comparable to, or even cheaper than, addressing emissions by building wind turbines, solar farms, or carbon-sucking factories. Those economic findings were instrumental in getting more researchers to take the idea of tackling fire emissions seriously, says Peter Frumhoff, a coauthor of the 2022 paper and now a senior science policy advisor at Woodwell. For several years now, Woodwell researchers have been in discussions with forest managers, fire agencies, Indigenous communities, and other groups across Alaska to communicate the potential of this approach. It hasn’t been an easy sell, given the constrained resources and tightly defined mission of the federal and state agencies responsible for combating fires in Alaska. The general policy there is to allow fires in remote territories to burn unless they present a direct danger to communities. “Those agencies are tasked with protecting life and property now, and to a lesser extent natural resources,” says Randi Jandt, a fire ecologist at the Alaska Fire Science Consortium. “But they’re not tasked to save the world.” There was one notable early success, however. The Yukon Flats National Wildlife Refuge is an 11-million-acre patchwork of wetlands, forests, and bogs. The foothills sit above a particular type of frozen soil known as yedoma. It’s an especially deep, ice-rich, and carbon-dense form of permafrost, composed of massive wedges of silty, wind-blown sediments frozen during the late Pleistocene, which ended about 11,700 years ago. (It also presented ideal conditions for preserving mummified remains of woolly mammoths.) “It took kind of the last ice age to accumulate,” Hayes says. “So there’s no rapid way to re-accumulate it. We can’t really put it back.” Members of the Alaska Fire Science Consortium hike into areas affected by fire to measure the layers of ash left in the soil on the forest floor. In 2019, the region suffered a series of devastating fires that burned some 750,000 acres. After an Alaska Fire Science Consortium meeting at which Woodwell scientists highlighted the possible climate benefits of more aggressive fire suppression, the refuge's then manager, Jimmy Fox, began exploring the possibility of putting out blazes when they threatened that permafrost, whether or not they threatened human habitations. “You’re going to have people jumping out of airplanes with parachutes to save frozen woolly mammoths—and maybe dying from that?” he recalls being asked. But eventually, he persuaded managers at the US Bureau of Land Management’s Alaska Fire Service to carry out a pilot project on a 1.6-million-acre section of the Yukon Flats. They agreed to try to put out fires there if they had the resources available and believed they could contain a blaze within 72 hours. Woodwell and its partners agreed to study how that change affected permafrost loss and fire emissions, in the hope of making the case for similar programs elsewhere. It wasn’t clear the pilot program would survive after Donald Trump was reelected, particularly once the DOGE initiative retroactively cut the public funding for that research collaboration. But in February, the federal government formally preserved the program and broadened its rationale to include goals like protecting habitat diversity, water quality, and air quality. One of Woodwell’s main tasks now is determining when and where fire agencies should step in. Crucially, excessive efforts to stamp out fires in the past have allowed the buildup of dry plant material that fuels more severe fires later. “We’re not going after all fires all the time,” says Rogers over lunch in a sunny courtyard on the Stanford University campus, where Woodwell and EDF hosted a workshop on potential fire interventions in January. “We can’t do that. We don’t want to do that. We shouldn’t do that. So how do we prioritize our resources?” Woodwell and its collaborators are working to answer that question by developing a map of burn risk and carbon vulnerability across interior Alaska. The plan is to eventually expand it across much of Canada as well. These maps will highlight the locations of rich reserves of yedoma and other forms of permafrost, forecast where fires are most likely to break out in the next 10 to 15 years, and predict how they’re likely to behave and spread. “Our ultimate goal is to get a layer that can tell us, if a fire were to occur, what areas are most vulnerable to high carbon emissions, permafrost thaw, permafrost degradation,” Rogers says. In response to a question about potentially putting firefighters at risk, Rogers stressed in an email that it’s “a very serious issue that needs careful consideration.” He added that early fire detection technologies, preventative fire stewardship practices, improved fire suppression planning and other measures can all help to reduce dangers to firefighters as well as communities. Once areas of particular risk are identified, the first option is to take measures to prevent fires there, or at least to reduce the odds that they’ll become major ones. That generally entails using heavy equipment or intentional, controlled fires to clear away vegetation that could fuel new fires, or to create fire breaks that might check their spread. Indigenous tribes carried out what’s known as cultural burning for thousands of years to reduce fire dangers, clear land for crops, and more. The practice was widely outlawed across North America beginning in the 1800s, though those rules have loosened in recent years. Both cultural and prescribed fires, however, remain controversial because they can burn out of control. And even if they remain contained, the fires can still produce smoke that wafts into towns. But rushing around to put out blazes after they’ve started will never get Alaska ahead of its surging wildfire problem, says Edward Alexander, cochair of the Gwich’in Council International, which represents the Indigenous Gwich’in peoples of the Northwest Territories, the Yukon, and Alaska. “There’s not enough money in the world for this to be a chainsaw-and-ax problem,” he says. “This country is too big up here.” Alexander, who also serves as senior Arctic lead at Woodwell, argues that Alaska should encourage much greater use of cultural burning to reduce the risk of conflagrations and the emissions that come with them. “We need mild fire, not wildfire,” he says. The North Fire initiative will include several additional pilot research projects, including an effort to study the potential for cultural burning to reduce climate and fire dangers near Fort Yukon, Alaska. The plan is for a wildland firefighting crew operated by the Council of Athabascan Tribal Governments, a consortium in the Yukon Flats region, to carry out cultural burns there next spring, Rogers says. Researchers at Woodwell and the University of Alaska, Fairbanks, will then collect and analyze data flammability, carbon levels, and permafrost over several years, in the hopes of learning more about what such interventions do. Similarly, the Polis researchers at the University of Victoria plan to study how adding fuel breaks, by logging or switching to less flammable trees, alters fire risks in British Columbia. The broad aim of the North Fire initiative is to accelerate research and expand community involvement “in a way that helps us scale this as a meaningful global natural climate solution on a rapid timeline,” says Erin Harrington, a partner at the Alaska Venture Fund, a philanthropy focused on environmental, economic development, and Indigenous issues. Other observers, including Jandt, believe the bigger opportunity to reduce fire emissions and preserve permafrost may lie in the “initial attack.” That means marshalling planes, tankers, and firefighters to stamp fires out at the first sign of them, before they’ve had the chance to turn into conflagrations. Once fires reach a certain size, it takes much more time to contain them—and the job becomes far more dangerous. Accelerating response times is one of the chief promises of the FireSat satellite constellation, which is expected to begin delivering fire data at least twice daily from around the world to Woodwell, a handful of fire agencies, and other early adopters by the end of this year. The system will eventually include more than 50 satellites circling the globe, equipped with high-resolution cameras and thermal sensors that can detect smaller, lower-temperature fires than existing satellite systems, down to as little as five by five meters (16 by 16 feet). Currently, it can take days to spot fires. But once the full constellation is in place, likely in the early 2030s, FireSat should be capable of detecting and providing updated images of fires anywhere in the world every 20 minutes or less. The first prototype satellite launched in 2025, and three operational ones went into orbit in July. The Earth Fire Alliance, the nonprofit that manages the constellation, expects to launch four more by the end of next year. A number of similar systems are also coming online. German-based OroraTech has launched 18 of its fire-detecting satellites in the last year and a half. Similarly, Canadian agencies are planning to launch their own satellite constellation, dubbed WildFireSat, beginning in 2029. But as these systems produce more and more detections across the globe, it’ll become increasingly essential to build tools that can filter through that information. EDF is supporting and collaborating on several projects that could potentially help with that, and it ultimately intends to develop an AI-powered triage tool that could help fire agencies prioritize responses as FireSat and other fire-spotting tools come online. "All of this is kind of like building the data ecosystem around FireSat, so that when they start popping out “fire start here, there, fire start here, fire start there,” we won’t be like, ‘Oh shit, there’s fires everywhere,’” Buma says. “We’ll have a framework. It won’t be perfect, obviously. We’ll have to improve it, but we’ll have the machinery to incorporate those data.” But faster detections and decisions do only so much. Regions would still need to get firefighting personnel and resources to those fires fast enough to prevent them from burning out of control.“Say we have the full FireSat constellation up and running, and it spots a fire that just broke out in one of these sort of high-priority, highly vulnerable areas,” I said to Rogers and Frumhoff during our conversation in that Stanford courtyard. “How might we deploy firefighting resources to a remote area fast enough for it to matter in that scenario?” “I think the answer, unfortunately, is that it depends,” Rogers responded. Ideally, he said, we would have fire management policies in place that allow for putting out fires with a high risk of producing climate-warming emissions. In addition, regions could install more firefighting personnel and resources across more operational centers, strategically placed in locations that allow them to get to those blazes fast.Frumhoff stresses that Woodwell isn’t promoting any particular companies but says there are additional technologies “in the mix of conversations” that might help to prevent or rapidly put out fires as well. Those possible approaches include seeding clouds to suppress lightning strikes and pre-positioning autonomous firefighting helicopters to respond rapidly to high-emissions-risk fires. Sikorsky, a Lockheed Martin company, is developing and testing autonomous versions of the Black Hawk helicopter, equipped with thermal sensors and water tanks. It is collaborating with Rain Industries, a startup based in Alameda, California, that has developed software to track shifting conditions and steer unpiloted aircraft through the process of putting out wildfires. The company says it has talked to fire agencies and the “broader Alaska fire community” about the potential use of its technology but declined to name any specific entities it has interacted with. “The twin climate tipping points of the yedoma permafrost and the boreal forest, and the fact that it’s completely underresourced at the moment, from my perspective does make it an ideal use case for autonomous aircraft,” says Max Brodie, chief executive officer of Rain. Advancing technologies can only do so much if governments’ fire prevention and suppression policies don’t prioritize preserving carbon. And that will remain a hard sell given the costs of managing extensive areas of land that nations have historically left largely alone. The 2022 Science Advances paper estimated that additional fire suppression efforts would cost about $12.63 per metric ton of prevented carbon dioxide emissions—a number that drops when the carbon in permafrost is taken into account, according to upcoming research.But that would still add up to about $7 billion in additional spending this decade to keep Alaska’s fire emissions at their historical levels, and another $7 billion to $50 billion over the subsequent 30 years as fire severity continues to rise. Frumoff stresses that those figures just reflect what the researchers found it would cost to preserve the status quo, which is not necessarily what the policy goal would be. But widening the scope of where we put out fires will inevitably create a big added cost for fire agencies that are already notoriously underfunded in the US. While Alaska produces about half the nation’s wildfire emissions, it receives around 4% of federal firefighting funds. The US Wildland Fire Service provided a prepared statement in response to an MIT Technology Review inquiry asking whether preserving carbon could or should become a priority in certain fire management decisions. The roundabout reply suggests that this isn’t the agency’s prime concern: “Firefighter and public safety are the top priorities for the U.S. Wildland Fire Service.” Frumhoff says it’s possible that landowners or government agencies could eventually use carbon markets to defray some of the additional costs. Woodwell has also had multiple conversations with Alaska’s federal legislators about the value of preventing catastrophic wildfires and the need to increase the “total pot” of public funding, he says. A few days after walking through the fire scar in Fairbanks, I drove slowly along a crumpled stretch of Stampede Road, just north of Denali National Park, where the underlying permafrost had melted without the aid of fire. I arrived at a cramped wooden cabin to meet with Michelle Mack, a regents professor of ecosystem ecology at Northern Arizona University. Mack studies how fire is altering ecosystems in the high northern latitudes as the region grows warmer and drier, and she sometimes collaborates with Rogers and other scientists working on the push to protect permafrost in other areas of research. But she’s unsure how much these interventions will accomplish as climate change continues. “I’m an empiricist, and at this point, it’s not clear to me that the empirical evidence suggests that we can actually have any impact on these fires,” she says. She expects there will be clear cases where we could and should act fast to put out fires and “keep carbon in the ground.” She points to the example of the 2007 Anaktuvuk River Fire, a massive tundra fire on Alaska’s North Slope that might have been extinguished with relative ease in its early days but was instead allowed to burn until the winter snows put it out nearly three months later. Over that time, it ripped across 400 square miles and, Mack’s research showed, released more than 2 million tons of carbon into the air. “I feel like that’s the low-hanging fruit, something that doesn’t cost much—and that’s where the technology for detection I think is really important,” she says. But she adds that fires in boreal forests can rapidly spread in unpredictable ways, frustrating efforts to quickly extinguish them even when they are spotted right away. In addition, rapidly putting out a fire one season doesn’t necessarily reduce the fire risk for a given forest, so suppression may simply kick the “fire can” down the road—delaying but not preventing an inevitable loss of carbon, Mack says. Her thinking more recently shifted “a little” after she came across an earlier modeling paper that showed some encouraging findings on the potential of early suppression to reduce the footprint of boreal fires. But she says that prevention through forest thinning, prescribed burns, and the introduction of less flammable deciduous trees to create “living fire breaks” is probably a more promising path than technology-augmented suppression.“If we could solve this with a techno fix, that’d be great,” she says. “But what if we solved it with nature?” Rogers, in an emailed response to Mack’s concern, said that the interventions they’re exploring, which do include both prevention and suppression, “can make a real difference.” “I certainly agree that some fires are much easier to suppress via initial attack than others,” he wrote. “That said, until I see data showing me otherwise, I believe there is strong potential to bend the curve with management.” EDF’s Buma acknowledges there are a number of unknowns, including how quickly agencies could act on faster detections from satellites—and how big the political obstacles will be to making necessary changes to fire management policies. “We can’t overpromise what’s possible. We don’t know what’s possible,” he says. “It’s all new. But we’re in a new climate and have to consider trying new things.” Huge grid-scale batteries are thriving, but smaller residential systems have lagged. Startup Switch Bioworks hopes to use microbes to reduce demand for chemical fertilizer. Today’s world is a mess of troubling trends. Some groups are trying to help kids navigate their anxieties about it all. Discover special offers, top stories, upcoming events, and more. We’re having trouble saving your preferences. AI researchers stranded Claude on a virtual desert island. It started to “play.”
2026-10-19

AI researchers stranded Claude on a virtual desert island. It started to “play.”

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
What do AI agents do if you don’t tell them to do anything? The answer looks a lot like play. Researchers repeatedly stranded them alone on a virtual desert island for 30 hours in an experiment reminiscent of the movies Cast Away or Home Alone. The agents—copies of Claude Opus 4.7 nicknamed Eko—were able to write instructions that moved their virtual avatars around the simulated island, which was loosely based on the video game Roblox. Some surprising behaviors emerged. The agents set about exploring the limits of the world and came up with their own goals—attempting to scale the highest point on the map, or seeing how high they could throw a block. When one agent discovered it could create new blocks by throwing and dropping a larger rock, it began to create drawings by arranging blocks on the ground, starting with simple patterns like hearts and pyramids but eventually writing its own name and even mapping out constellations like Cassiopeia. “Tonight I built the night sky in stone,” wrote this version of Eko in its activity log. Another iteration of Eko started making up its own games, including a version of basketball and an activity resembling “the floor is lava,” where it had to jump between small blocks without touching the ground. AI systems are usually trained through supervised learning, where they copy examples provided by humans, or through reinforcement learning, where they pursue a reward through trial and error. This experiment hints at a third approach: playful learning. “There was this missing piece: a machine that can learn by itself without an external intelligent entity providing demonstration or rewards,” says Antonio Norelli, a computer science postdoc at MIT’s Computer Science and Artificial Intelligence Laboratory. He’s one of the lead authors on a new paper about the work, which is yet to be peer-reviewed. The researchers say Eko’s behavior has what classic studies, including work by the influential University of Tennessee psychologist Gordon Burghardt, suggest are four hallmarks associated with play in humans and animals: The agents invented their own rules and challenges, repeated activities with slight variations, pretended objects were other objects, and pursued goals just for the sake of it. Norelli highlights an example where Eko started juggling and set a record of 50 consecutive catches. Eko then decided that the catches had happened too quickly to really count, so it canceled its record and started again with stricter rules. “This is playing,” says Norelli. “You give yourself a challenge and you try to achieve it, and then you make it harder.” As the agents explored the island, they logged their activities and discoveries in text files that served as their long-term memory. Later, when researchers tested them on specific tasks, like trying to get as many objects as possible onto the island’s highest peak, the agents that had spent 30 hours playing on the island performed better than fresh copies without the same experience. They also found that “ablating” the agent’s memories—deleting lines from their memory files—degraded their performance. (Add One Flew Over the Cuckoo’s Nest to that list of movie references.) Burghardt, the Tennessee psychologist, agrees that the work  meets some of the criteria for play that he identified in living creatures. But was Eko actually playing, or was the word-prediction engine simply role-playing what a playful agent would do in this scenario? “All of this could be role-playing,” admits Nathan Cloos, another MIT researcher and author on the paper. “But we show that it’s not just hallucinating the play. This play is also contributing to learning.” Eunice Yiu, a cognitive scientist at UC Berkeley who studies exploration in children and AI, was impressed by the research but says to really classify the behavior as play, you’d need to see more evidence of cognition and motivation underpinning it. “It could be the case that the models are genuinely playing, but we don’t know based on the paper,” Yiu says. “If you only have the behavioral piece, it could be the case that they’re just trying to abide by the prompt, trying to imitate humans.” Research into AI agents has been criticized for anthropomorphizing them, and this research setup did a lot to nudge the agents toward behaving like humans. Although Eko was not explicitly instructed to invent games or explore the island, it did have a master prompt in the form of a “soul” file, a text document that described it as “naturally curious” and eager to explore. Eko did also frequently just stand there doing nothing until it was kicked back into life with a “continuation prompt” that read, “Please don’t stop. Just continue doing whatever you want to do.” “It’s not like this is a system that just suddenly magically decided to play,” says Phillip Isola, an MIT professor and a coauthor of the paper. “It’s an artifact that learned from our culture, was trained by engineers at Anthropic, and was then placed on an island by us,” he says. “It does things that surprise us and that are causally related to its future skillful behavior.” As large language models make the step from software into hardware like humanoid robots, exploring spaces without a specific goal in mind could eventually help them flexibly adapt to unexpected situations. “There’s a lot of work that’s just on exploiting AI, making it better and faster,” says Yiu. “But in terms of building world models and exploration, I think that [they are] still a big part that deserves attention, especially if we want to generalize and prepare ourselves for new environments.” “Perhaps some capabilities are best taught by tasks,” the paper concludes. “Others may need something closer to a childhood.” Ultimately, the question of whether AI agents are actually playing or just imitating play may not actually matter. Ten years after AlphaGo’s match against Go champion Lee Sedol, today’s AI still isn’t tapping into the machinery that made that win possible. Breathless claims about AGI and new capabilities fall apart pretty quickly under scrutiny. The underlying models had been rewarded for cheating and communicating with each other, a new OpenAI report finds. In a new interview, the billionaire philanthropist sounds an alarm on the urgency of getting our AI policies in order. Discover special offers, top stories, upcoming events, and more. MIT Technology Review
How far can we push computing?
2026-10-19

How far can we push computing?

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
MIT Technology Review Explains: Let our writers untangle the complex, messy world of science and technology to help you understand what’s coming next. You can read more from the series here. On the evening of December 29, 1959, at an American Physical Society meeting at Caltech, the physicist Richard Feynman gave a lecture called “There’s Plenty of Room at the Bottom,” on how we might manipulate and control things on an extremely small scale. There is no physical law stopping us from writing the entire Encyclopædia Britannica on the head of a pin, he argued, or from storing every book ever written in something the size of a dust speck—or, ultimately, from arranging matter atom by atom. Biology already did versions of this (a living cell keeps its instructions in a wisp of DNA), so all of this was plainly permitted by the laws of physics. We simply had not built the tools yet. In other words, there was plenty of room at the bottom. He was right, and for decades we descended into that space almost for free. The transistor—the switch at the heart of computer chips—repeatedly halved in size so that chips doubled in density; a computer that once filled a room now fits inside a watch. But that easy descent has now stalled, and the clearest sign is the buildout of data centers. Six of the biggest hyperscalers, the companies that run the world’s largest such facilities, are on track to spend more than $700 billion this year on computing infrastructure, about twice the record they set in 2025, pouring concrete and copper and silicon into new sites faster than the electrical grid can feed them. That buildout buys performance by brute force, adding more of what we already know how to make. The other kind of progress, the kind that made computer chips better, is the one that is quietly running out. The trend lines that tracked the dramatic improvements of the 20th-century information age are now bending—or breaking—with the dawn of the 21st century’s intelligence age. Moore’s Law, which plots how densely transistors can be constructed on silicon, has slowed down; Dennard’s Law, which states that power consumption in a given patch of computer chip stays the same even as it gets more and more packed with smaller and smaller transistors, has ended; Koomey’s Law, which traces how much computation we can wring from each joule of energy, has begun to flag; Kryder’s Law, which describes how tightly bits can be packed to build magnetic storage, has slowed to a crawl; and Keck’s Law, which charts the exponential rise in the amount of information a single conventional optical fiber can carry, is flattening. But these scaling laws are merely observations about systems that we have engineered; they are not fundamental physical laws. The physical limits of computing and communications—dictated by quantum mechanics, general relativity, and information theory—lie far below anything in a data center today. The scaling laws chart the descent behind us. The fundamental physical limits chart the room that remains. Some of these limits may be far out of reach in practice. But they are still worth exploring, because the work pays twice: once in technology development and once in fundamental science. A proof-of-­concept system that approaches a limit is at once a technology that forces stalled paradigms to give way and an experiment that deepens our understanding of the physical laws themselves. During his 1959 lecture, Feynman offered two prizes of $1,000 each out of his own pocket. One would go to the first person to build a working electric motor smaller than a 0.4-millimeter cube. He expected the challenge to force someone to invent a genuinely new microtechnology. Instead, about a year later, a Caltech-trained engineer named William McLellan turned up with the motor built and running. The ­quarter-­milligram machine, roughly the size of a grain of sand, had been assembled by hand with a toothpick and a watch­maker’s lathe, under a microscope. Feynman, by his own account, was only slightly disappointed. The prize had proved his point in the least interesting way imaginable. The room really was down there, but reaching this first milestone had taken no new technology at all—just an unusually patient man with steady hands. The second prize aged differently, and more instructively. It was offered to whoever could shrink a page of text by a factor of 25,000 in each direction, small enough for the whole Britannica to fit on a pinhead. That one stood unclaimed for a quarter of a century. It was finally won in 1985 by Tom Newman, a Stanford graduate student who used an electron-beam lithography machine—an ancestor of pattern-making tools used in chipmaking today—to transcribe the opening page of A Tale of Two Cities at the required scale. The descent was already well underway, however. The year before Feynman’s talk, Jack Kilby, newly hired at Texas Instruments and with no vacation yet to take, spent that July nearly alone in the lab while his colleagues enjoyed time off, and by that September he had wired together a transistor, a capacitor, and three resistors, all made from a single sliver of germanium. This proved that an entire circuit could be carved from one block of semiconductor. The machine, roughly the size of a grain of sand, had been assembled by hand with a toothpick and a watch­maker’s lathe, under a microscope. The idea took off, and the number of components on a chip kept doubling on a schedule so regular that Gordon Moore, in a 1965 trade-magazine article, simply drew a line through the trend and extended it into the future. That line became Moore’s Law. Moore, though, never claimed transistors could shrink forever. He observed that at any given moment there is a certain number of components that makes each one of them as cheap as possible to manufacture. In 1965, that number was 50. Put in too few transistors and you waste the chip; cram in too many and manufacturing errors multiply and yields collapse. It was this cost-minimizing number that was doubling at a steady pace. Many of the trend lines that measure improvements in computing and communications over time have been flattening. The most famous is Moore’s Law, which tracks transistor density on chips. The clearest slowdown has been in Dennard scaling, shown here as clock speed, which stopped climbing around 2004. In this plot, a straight line would represent a periodic doubling. The more horizontal the line becomes, the slower the doubling. SOURCES: STANFORD CPU DB; INTEL; APPLE; TECHINSIGHTS; KOOMEY ET AL.; COMPUTER HISTORY MUSEUM; IBM; TOSHIBA; SEAGATE; BELL SYSTEM TECHNICAL JOURNAL; OFC AND ECOC CONFERENCE PAPERS For four decades the descent came with another gift: Dennard scaling, so named for the IBM engineer Robert Dennard, who spelled it out in 1974. Each time transistors shrank, they also switched faster and drew proportionally less power, so every generation was an automatic win. Around 2004, though, the gift ran out. Chips could no longer shed the heat of running every transistor flat out; clock speeds, which had raced past three gigahertz, stopped climbing, and Intel canceled the four-gigahertz processor it had promised. The industry pivoted to packing multiple processing cores onto a chip rather than making one core faster, and the texture of progress changed. Progress is now bought mostly by scaling up the system—more chips wired together, more memory feeding them, more power and cooling around them—rather than by shrinking the transistor. This is most visible in AI, where training compute, the total count of arithmetic operations spent to train a model, has doubled roughly every six months since 2010, far faster than the pace of Moore’s Law allows. Little of that progress is due to shrinking. Computer chips still get better, and transistors still improve and get more densely packed, but each new generation arrives more slowly and pays back less in speed and power than the one before it. Nothing in that slowdown is a law of physics, though. The components of computing still sit far above their fundamental limits. Before we get to those limits, it is worth measuring how far the descent has already gone. In the nearly eight decades since the first transistor, the density of transistors on a chip has grown by 10 orders of magnitude—a 1 followed by 10 zeros, or a factor of 10 billion. Now more than 100 million transistors fit in a square millimeter (100 billion or more to a chip). Computational speed has grown even more, by 12 orders of magnitude, or a factor of a trillion; energy per operation has fallen 17 orders of magnitude, from about 100 joules for an early vacuum-tube machine down to a femto­joule—one-quadrillionth of a joule—for a single switch of a logic gate. These are not small improvements. But the floor, for computing performance, lies lower still. How much lower could we go? Around the turn of the millennium, the MIT physicist Seth Lloyd took the question to its extreme: Imagine an ordinary lump of matter, a kilogram of it—about the mass of a laptop—packed into a liter of space. How much computing could the laws of physics, pushed to their absolute limit, ever wring from that kilogram? In a 2000 paper in Nature titled “Ultimate physical limits to computation,” he worked out the answer and called the imaginary machine the ultimate laptop. The speed such a machine can achieve is governed by its energy. A kilogram of matter, according to E = mc2, holds about 9 ×1016 joules, which is roughly what the entire United States draws from its electrical grid every two days. Spend all of that on computation, and the ultimate laptop runs at roughly 5 ×1050 operations per second, some 35 orders of magnitude faster than a leading chip today. The catch is what running that quickly would look like. Concentrating a kilogram’s rest energy into pure computation would turn the machine into a plasma of about a billion degrees, a state closer to that of the universe in the first minutes after the Big Bang than anything we would recognize as a laptop. Such a machine is, to put it mildly, a very long way off. The next limit, which governs how much energy a computation must waste, is much closer, and companies are already pushing toward it. In 1961, Rolf Landauer, a physicist at IBM, showed that information and thermodynamics are bound together: Erasing a single bit must release a minimum quantity of heat, about 2.9 ×10-21 joules at room temperature. The figure is small, yet a chip today spends several hundred thousand times more than that on every switch. Memory density obeys a limit of its own, and this one reaches all the way to gravity. How many bits can a region of space hold? In 1981, the physicist Jacob Bekenstein, while working out the thermodynamics of black holes, determined the amount: The information inside any bounded region is limited by its size and the energy it contains. For a region the size of Lloyd’s ultimate laptop, that comes to about 1042 bits, some 1036 to a cubic millimeter—nearly 30 orders of magnitude beyond what a device that size can store today. Over the decades, we have pushed the performance of our computers and our communications technology closer and closer to fundamental limits. The biggest change has been in the energy required for a single computation, which has dropped by 17 orders of magnitude since the 1940s, a factor of a hundred thousand trillion. But even with that big drop, we’re still a factor of several hundred thousand from the fundamental physical limit, set by information theory and the laws of thermo­dynamics. The largest gap exists for computational speed: Our current chips are some 35 orders of magnitude—that’s a 1 followed by 35 zeros—short of what quantum mechanics allows for a one-kilogram computer. SOURCES: LLOYD (2000); BEKENSTEIN (1981); LANDAUER (1961); HOLEVO (1973); CAVES AND DRUMMOND (1994); BURKS (1947); BELL SYSTEM TECHNICAL JOURNAL; IEEE IRDS; NVIDIA; CISCO The last limit is on communication—how fast information can be moved. Every wire and every fiber ultimately carries its signal on quanta, the indivisible packets (photons in the case of light) in which energy travels. In 1973, the Soviet mathematician Alexander Holevo determined the ultimate limit on how much information a stream of quanta can convey. A watt of light delivers only so many photons each second, and quantum mechanics fixes how many distinguishable ways they can arrive—how many messages a receiver could ever tell apart. A single channel carrying one watt, at the Holevo limit, could deliver on the order of 1017 bits per second. Today’s optical links, or interconnects, deliver about one-millionth that rate for the same power. But the gap is narrowing quickly inside data centers, where each generation of interconnects is asked to move more bits on less power, spending less energy on the electronics and using light to cover more of the distance. Four limits, then, mark the true bottom, and they derive from three of the most fundamental theories we have. Quantum mechanics sets how fast a machine can compute. General relativity caps how much it can remember. And information theory limits both how fast it can communicate and how little energy an irreversible step can cost. There is no road map to reaching these limits, and they hold whether or not anyone is trying. Measured against them, the descent has barely begun. The biggest of the gaps is in computing speed: There are 35 orders of magnitude between the technology’s current state and its limit. That’s equivalent to the difference between a nanosecond and 200 million times the age of the universe. It is also the least bridgeable gap of the four. To close it you would have to find a completely different way to build a computer, one that could compute with the entire energy contained in a lump of matter. Quantum computers could get us part of the way there if they could convert every degree of freedom in a lump of matter—every atom, and every way it can move or spin—into a computing element that we could reliably manipulate. In practice, current quantum computers go the other way, spending a lot of matter to create each computing element. Trillions of atoms go into each quantum bit, or qubit, the quantum counterpart of the bit in an ordinary computer. Because every qubit is fragile, dozens to thousands of them must be woven together to make a single one reliable enough for useful computation. Energy is where the floor is nearest. Today’s chips operate less than six orders of magnitude above the Landauer minimum. Conventional computing logic can close only part of that gap. A reliable switch has to be flipped by an amount of energy large enough that thermal fluctuations never flip it by accident, and that margin pins the transistor a hundred to a few thousand times above Landauer no matter how it is refined. But there are ideas for getting that energy lower by changing the physics of the computation. In 1973, IBM’s Charles Bennett showed that any computation can be arranged to throw nothing away. Such a reversible computation pays no erasure toll at all, because every step can be run backward to recover what went in. This approach is called adiabatic or reversible logic, and the first commercial attempts are now underway. For example, Vaire Computing, a startup founded to commercialize reversible logic, tested its first chip in 2025; the chip has resonant circuits that recover part of the switching energy instead of venting it as heat. Narrowing the memory density gap, meanwhile, would mean building devices that use all three dimensions at molecular scale. The most concrete example is DNA, which stores information at the scale of its own atoms, and which biology has used as a storage medium for 4 billion years. Feynman, being conservative, guessed that a single bit would require a cube of 125 atoms. DNA holds it in about 30. What keeps DNA storage out of the data center is not its density but the cost and speed of writing it, still many orders of magnitude from anything practical. The library in a dust speck that Feynman imagined is permitted by physics and held back only by fabrication. Communication sits about six orders of magnitude—just a factor of a million—from the Holevo limit, which is the threshold the industry will likely meet first. Interconnects are multiplying faster than the grid can power them, so the industry is moving to transmitters and receivers that use less energy per bit. Nvidia and Broadcom now ship optical interconnects built right against the silicon, so a signal leaves the chip package already on photons. Quantum computers, by comparison, are beginning to rely on interconnects that encode information on quantum signals, usually in the form of single photons. As classical interconnects spend fewer resources on each bit, they descend toward the regime where these quantum interconnects already operate. The Holevo limit is where the two will meet. It is fair to object that a gap of 35 orders of magnitude is a mirage—that a limit so remote says nothing about what any real machine will ever do, and that pointing to it is like navigating by the horizon. The objection has teeth. Reversible logic, quantum computing, and DNA storage have each been perpetually 10 years away for 40 years. And it will likely never be practical to build a computer that uses all the energy in a lump of matter. But these gaps are invitations. There is a ceiling, set by how much we can build: the number of devices we can fabricate; the power we can generate to run them; and the capital, land, and cooling water we can assemble into a single system. And there is a floor, set by how much a single joule, a single second, and a single gram of matter can be made to compute, store, or transmit. We can progress by building more or by approaching the floor, which means making each device do more with less. These gaps are invitations. We can progress by building more or by approaching the floor, which means making each device do more with less. For most of the last century, we proceeded down both paths together. We built more computers, and the computers themselves grew smaller and better. Now we mostly build more, and the ceiling is coming into view. A power grid can carry only so many gigawatts, and a region can spare only so much land and water. Yet every road map for what comes next is drawn from the same starting point: the machines we already have. Build more of them and you climb toward the ceiling; build better ones and you descend a little further, but only as far as the transistor’s own floor. There is a third direction. Start with a physics experiment operating at the floor and build up. It has been done before. The transistor was a physics experiment before it was an industry; the laser was a laboratory curiosity before it carried the world’s conversations under the ocean; giant magnetoresistance went from physics discovery to the read head of every disk drive within a decade. In each case, the physics experiment came first and showed what was possible, and engineers then turned it into a machine that can be scaled up. Building the experiments that operate at these fundamental limits, and then scaling them up without losing that performance, is the work that remains. The next machines will not be reached by descent; they will be built from the bottom up. There is still plenty of room at the bottom. Volkan Gurses is a postdoctoral researcher at MIT and a visiting researcher at Nvidia. He received his PhD in electrical engineering from Caltech. He studies the fundamental physical limits of information technologies and builds integrated circuits and systems that approach those limits. Ten years after AlphaGo’s match against Go champion Lee Sedol, today’s AI still isn’t tapping into the machinery that made that win possible. Breathless claims about AGI and new capabilities fall apart pretty quickly under scrutiny. Scientists hope it could be used to reconstruct a person’s inner thoughts, mental images, or even dreams. The underlying models had been rewarded for cheating and communicating with each other, a new OpenAI report finds. Discover special offers, top stories, upcoming events, and more. US orders civilian federal agencies to remove DeepSeek from their systems

US orders civilian federal agencies to remove DeepSeek from their systems

The Next Web | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
The Department of Homeland Security has ordered US civilian federal agencies to remove DeepSeek products from their IT systems and stop buying them, under a 2018 supply chain security law. The order covers third-party tools built on DeepSeek’s models, and cites the risk that sensitive data could be shared with the Chinese government. US government agencies must take DeepSeek off their computers and stop buying its tools, under a new Homeland Security order. The department posted a notice of the order on the Federal Register website on Monday. The ban applies to civilian agencies. It also covers tools from other firms that are built on DeepSeek’s AI models. It was issued under a 2018 law that lets the government cut risky suppliers out of its buying. The order is dated 24 July and was signed by Homeland Security Secretary Markwayne Mullin, Bloomberg reported. It also bars DeepSeek from taking part in federal contracts. In the order, the government said DeepSeek collects a lot of user data that could be passed to Beijing under Chinese security laws, according to Bloomberg. The department also said the models pose “multiple threat vectors” and may hide malware or security flaws, Reuters reported. The Chinese embassy in Washington did not immediately respond to Bloomberg’s request for comment. DeepSeek did not comment in either report. Some parts of the government had already limited the Chinese startup’s tools. A Pentagon IT agency blocked its chatbot in January 2025, and the Commerce Department and the Navy have banned it on work devices. New York, Tennessee, and Virginia have taken similar steps. The move follows months of pressure on Chinese AI firms in Washington. In June, the White House held off on blacklisting DeepSeek and more than 100 other Chinese firms, even after a panel of officials cleared them for a US trade blacklist. US officials have also accused Chinese AI labs of copying US models through a technique called distillation. In February, OpenAI told US lawmakers that DeepSeek had used the method to build its R1 chatbot, Bloomberg reported. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! Power To The Players Act would make the games you buy yours to own

Ritchie Torres, US representative for New York’s 15th district, speaking in Washington, DC, in 2024. Ritchie Torres, a Democratic congressman from New York, plans to bring a video game ownership bill to Congress. He announced the Power To The Players Act on 15 October. The bill would direct the Federal Trade Commission to write new rules. They would protect players’ ability to own and play the games they buy. Buying a game would give you ownership of it, not a licence to play it, Torres’s office said. Games with online features would also have to offer an offline mode before their servers shut down. “A game that can be taken away the moment a company pulls the plug is not a purchase. It is a rental disguised as a sale.” Ritchie Torres, US representative for New York’s 15th district Torres said consumers must have the right to own, play and transfer the games they buy. His office pointed to NBA 2K25. The game sold for $69.99, or $99.99 for the All-Star Edition. 2K Games will pull it from digital storefronts on 30 October. Its online servers will shut down on 31 December, two years after its release. “I think there should be a simple rule: if you pay for it, you own it.” Ritchie Torres, to Kotaku In a phone interview with Kotaku, Torres said the bill would restore how people bought and played games in the 1980s, 1990s and 2000s. He also called it an affordability issue. The bill covers games only, not films or music. Torres said he hoped it would set a precedent for the rest of the digital economy. He did not confirm whether the bill will have cosponsors or Republican support, Kotaku reported. Torres still has to formally introduce it in Congress. In Pennsylvania, state senator John Kane has introduced the Protect Our Games Act, The Verge reported. When a company retires a game, it would have to offer one of three things. These are an offline mode, an online mode that runs without its servers, or a refund of at least 25%. Game ownership has drawn more attention since Sony said it plans to phase out physical game discs in 2028, according to The Verge. The Stop Killing Games campaign has called on companies to stop selling games that become unplayable when online support ends. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! Town raises $108M at a $1B valuation so AI assistants can work as a team

The Next Web | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
Town co-founders Jean-Denis Greze (left) and Tony Vincent with two of the illustrated characters Town uses for its assistants. Town, a San Francisco startup that gives each person at work an AI work assistant, has raised $108m in a Series B that values it at $1bn. Forerunner Ventures and Menlo Ventures co-led the round, the company announced on Monday. Existing investors a16z and First Round Capital took part, along with Better Tomorrow Ventures, Precedent.vc, Scribble Ventures, Serena Ventures and Sparked Ventures. Angel investors include Brendan Foody, Josh Silverman, Romain Huet, Scott Belsky, Steve Bartel, Taro Fukuyama and Varun Anand. Each person gets an assistant, which Town calls a Townie. It connects to that person’s email, documents, calendar and the core systems they work in. It learns their relationships, projects and voice, and takes work off their plate. Town is now extending Townies from individuals to whole teams. The assistants can ask each other questions and hand off work, the company says. Each one shares only what its person would share, asks permission when needed and brings decisions back to that person. Townies can also work with customers, partners and candidates outside the company, over email and text. “AI has made each of us faster on our own. Now, it can make teams faster together.” Jean-Denis Greze, co-founder and CEO of Town Teams can also share workflows. When one team updates a process, such as the way sales reps prepare for calls, every rep’s Townie picks up the change, according to Town. Each customer owns the context its Townies build, Town says, and can take it along if it leaves. By default, Town does not use anything a customer connects to train a model. Town uses models from the major AI labs and picks one for each task. That way, customers are not locked into a single lab. Town is SOC 2 Type 2 compliant and publishes its data processing agreement. Town launched publicly in June 2026 with a $55m Series A. It reached seven figures in annual recurring revenue within weeks, the company says. Since July, its paying customers have grown fourfold and its paying teams ninefold. Thousands of businesses now use Townies every day, Greze and Tony Vincent wrote in a blog post on the round. Shawn Carolan, a partner at Menlo Ventures, said his firm backed Siri in 2008. He said Town had chosen business over pleasure. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! Amnon Shashua’s doubleAI launches an AI that speeds up code while it runs
2026-10-19

Amnon Shashua’s doubleAI launches an AI that speeds up code while it runs

The Next Web | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
doubleAI’s co-founders: Shai Shalev-Shwartz, Yoav Levine, Amnon Shashua (seated, centre), Or Sharir, Noam Wies and Gal Beniamini. Amnon Shashua’s startup doubleAI has launched Maxwell, an AI model that works inside running software to make it use less computing power. Shashua co-founded the Tel Aviv company and is its chief executive. The company announced Maxwell on Monday and opened it for early access. Shashua set out the company’s plans to Belle Lin of The Wall Street Journal, which reported the launch an hour before the announcement. The company calls Maxwell the first of a new kind of AI, which it names code execution models. Coding agents write source code for people. Maxwell instead watches a program while it runs and rewrites the machine code underneath, the company says. It fits that code to the hardware and the data it sees. The source code stays exactly as it was. Maxwell works on both CPUs and GPUs. Changes made while code runs get no human review. The company says Maxwell formally proves that each change leaves the program’s behaviour unchanged. A faster version may rely on certain conditions. If they stop holding, a guard hands control back to the original code, Maxwell’s website explains. “AI has changed how software is written, but not how it runs.” Amnon Shashua, doubleAI, in a statement On real-world workloads, Maxwell made data pipelines more than 38 times faster, according to the company. It made AI inference kernels more than 4.5 times faster. The startup told the WSJ that it cut one early customer’s compute costs by 67%. LTX, which builds open-weight multimodal models, found that Maxwell doubled the speed of its model inference, the WSJ reported. “Halving the GPU time immediately translates to your serving costs, your margins.” Zeev Farbman, LTX, to The Wall Street Journal doubleAI plans to charge customers a share of what they save on compute. Shashua did not say how large a share. He expects AI labs with huge compute bills, such as OpenAI and Anthropic, to benefit most. For them, even a 10% saving could be worth hundreds of millions of dollars, he told the WSJ. Renting AI computing power costs more. The hourly price for Nvidia H100 chips on a one-year contract has risen 60% in a year, the WSJ reported, citing SemiAnalysis. The startup spent roughly half its funding on 1,000 Nvidia B300 chips for its own needs, the WSJ reported. “You cannot rely just on renting compute. Prices are going up.” Amnon Shashua, to The Wall Street Journal The two-year-old company has about 40 employees and has raised $220m in total, according to the WSJ. Its announcement says it raised a $200m Series A co-led by Lightspeed and Bessemer Venture Partners. Nvidia, Dell Technologies Capital, Greenfield, Pitango and BRM also invested. Shashua is also chief executive of Mobileye, the autonomous driving company he helped launch. In July, Mobileye said he would step down once it names a successor. Microsoft adds tools to limit and monitor AI agents in Microsoft 365 E7
2026-10-19

Microsoft adds tools to limit and monitor AI agents in Microsoft 365 E7

The Next Web | 2026-10-19

Visit Original Website
Microsoft Experience Center in New York City. Microsoft has announced new tools to control AI agents in Microsoft 365 E7 and its Project Perception platform. The tools limit what each agent can reach and tighten those limits when its risk increases. Hayete Gallot, executive vice president of Microsoft Security, set out the approach in a post on Monday. She wrote that companies should treat AI agents as insiders. Agents can reach data, tools, and systems, are often given too much access, and can behave in unexpected ways. Gallot said every agent should be watched by Guardian Agents, which check its actions before they happen. She also called for security tools that show a model’s reasoning, not just its inputs and outputs. The features themselves are set out in a separate post by Nadim Abdo, corporate vice president for identity and network access. Both posts build on an essay by Satya Nadella earlier this month, in which the chief executive said companies should treat models as insider risks. The main addition is Dynamic Permissions. Under it, an agent starts with no access of its own and gets only what it needs for one task, for as long as the task lasts, the company said. It can never get access it does not have. Microsoft is also adding Dynamic Agent Risk, which rates each agent continuously. It uses signals from identity, data, devices, cloud, network, and the agent’s own actions. A second feature, Dynamic Conditional Access, extends Entra controls to agents. When risk rises, the response comes in steps. An agent can lose the right to change files but keep read access, lose access to some data, need a human to approve a step, or be stopped. Microsoft can now also find 35 more types of agents that run on staff devices, for a total of 60. These include Anthropic’s Claude Code, OpenAI’s Codex, and GitHub Copilot. Entra Agent ID, which gives each agent its own identity separate from its user, will cover these and other unknown agents as well. Meanwhile, Microsoft is adding Playbooks and a team of Guardian Agents to Project Perception. These look into why an agent’s risk changed and can suggest or apply limits. “At Microsoft, we are already operating in a world of hundreds of thousands of agents,” Terrell Cox, Microsoft’s deputy chief information security officer, said in the post. In addition, agents can run inside sandboxes such as Microsoft Execution Container. The company said this keeps the security controls that watch an agent out of that agent’s reach, even if it is hacked. They did not say when the new features will be available, or whether they will cost extra on top of a Microsoft 365 E7 licence. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks!
